An introduction to monitored quantum systems and quantum trajectories: spectrum, typicality, and phases
Ryusuke Hamazaki, Ken Mochizuki, Hisanori Oshima, Yohei Fuji

Abstract
Thanks to recent experimental advances in simulating and detecting quantum dynamics with high precision and controllability, our understanding of the physics of monitored quantum systems has considerably deepened over the past decades. In this article, we provide an introductory theoretical review on the basic formalisms governing open quantum dynamics under measurement, along with recent developments in their spectral and typical aspects. After reviewing quantum measurement theory, we introduce the concept of quantum trajectories, which are the conditional dynamics of monitored states shaped by a set of measurement outcomes. We then discuss the spectral properties of the dynamical map describing the evolution averaged over measurement outcomes. As has recently been recognized, these spectral features are intimately connected to whether quantum trajectories exhibit typical behaviors, such as the ergodicity and purification. Moreover, we introduce Lyapunov exponents of typical quantum trajectories and discuss how these quantities serve as indicators of measurement-induced phase transitions in monitored quantum many-body systems.

2]RIKEN Center for Interdisciplinary Theoretical and Mathematical Sciences(iTHEMS), RIKEN, Wako 351-0198, Saitama, Japan

1
Introduction

Quantum measurement theory
[
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
]
has been at the heart of the foundation of quantum mechanics since its inception in the early 20th century.
In the last few decades, the study of monitored quantum systems has seen remarkable progress and has become an essential topic even for understanding the dynamics of open quantum systems
[
5
,
6
,
7
]
.
These developments have been driven by groundbreaking experimental advances in precisely manipulating and detecting quantum systems, from the seminal experiments
[
8
,
9
,
10
]
to the advent of quantum simulators and computers
[
11
,
12
,
13
,
14
]
.
A striking example is the observation that additional measurements on many-body unitary dynamics within a quantum processor can give rise to novel non-equilibrium phases, characterized by their entanglement structure
[
15
,
16
]
.
As seen from this example, monitored quantum dynamics is now recognized as linking diverse research areas, ranging from quantum information physics and condensed-matter physics to thermodynamics.

Conceptually, monitored quantum systems have an interesting structure that is not present in open quantum systems coupled to uncontrollable environment.
While both systems evolve under non-unitary dynamics, monitored systems retain access to the measurement outcomes, which can be recorded in a classical register.
The time evolution is then conditioned by the measurement outcomes unless we discard them, forming what is known as a quantum trajectory
[
17
,
18
]
.
Importantly, quantum trajectories can remain in pure states throughout the evolution, in contrast to open quantum systems driven by the environment where the state eventually becomes mixed.
This feature leads to phenomena unique to monitored systems, including the measurement-induced entanglement phase transitions
[
19
,
20
,
21
]
.

While (continuous-time) quantum trajectories were first introduced in the 1990s
[
22
,
23
,
24
,
25
,
17
]
, uncovering their mathematical properties remains an intriguing topic even today
[
26
,
27
,
28
,
29
,
30
,
31
,
32
,
33
,
34
]
.
One notable feature is that most quantum trajectories display universal behaviors for certain quantities, which we call ‘‘typical” behaviors of quantum trajectories in this article
1
1

1

We note that, while the term “typical” is often used in physics (e.g., statistical mechanics), it may be a rather informal word from a mathematical point of view.
For example, if we consider the convergence of sequences of random variables in mathematics, there are several distinct notions, such as the convergence in probability or almost-sure convergence (see footnote
51
for their precise statements).
In contrast, such exact meanings are not usually considered when one says “typical” in physics.
Still, we here adopt this terminology to introduce the intuitive notion in a physicist-friendly manner.
In this review, “convergence for typical (almost all) quantum trajectories” basically means the almost-sure convergence; when we want to stress this fact, especially in Chapters
5
and
6
, we will explicitly use the term “almost surely.”

.
Note that justifying such typical properties is by no means a simple task, partly because measurement probabilities determined by the Born rule depend nontrivially on the quantum state.
Nevertheless, many conditions for the emergence of typical behaviors have been identified, such as the uniqueness of the stationary state of the ensemble-averaged dynamics.
Examples that manifest typical features include outcome statistics, ergodic properties, state purification, and relaxation timescales governed by the Lyapunov spectrum.
Interestingly, some of these quantities also serve as crucial indicators for characterizing novel measurement-induced phase transitions in many-body systems.

In this review article, we give an introduction to monitored quantum systems and quantum trajectories from a theoretical viewpoint, emphasizing their spectra, typical properties, and phases.
Our aim is twofold:
First, we explain the basic formalisms of measurement theory and quantum trajectories in a pedagogical manner
2
2

2

Indeed, this article is originally based on a lecture given by the first author at “Summer Lecture Camp of the Hatano Laboratory” in Aug. 2024, although a significant amount of material has been added to this article.

.
Accordingly, the content in the first half of the article partially overlaps with the existing literature
[
18
,
7
,
35
,
36
,
37
]
.
We also emphasize that this article is not intended to be a comprehensive survey of the field of open quantum systems, for which numerous excellent books and reviews are already available
[
5
,
38
,
6
,
18
,
7
,
39
,
40
,
41
,
42
,
43
,
44
,
45
,
46
,
14
,
35
,
47
,
48
,
21
,
49
,
50
]
.
Second, which we believe will make this review unique, is the highlighting of recent advances concerning the typical properties of quantum trajectories and their connections to the spectral properties and non-equilibrium phases manifesting in monitored systems.
Despite recent progress on this topic in the community of mathematical physics, these developments are not widely shared among many physicists studying monitored quantum systems.
We aim to bridge this gap by presenting core ideas in an intuitive and physicist-friendly manner, occasionally at the expense of full mathematical rigor.

The rest of this review is organized as follows.
In Chapter
2
, we introduce basic concepts of quantum measurement theory.
After reviewing simple projective measurements, we discuss some fundamental concepts such as indirect measurements, positive operator valued measures (POVMs), CP-instruments, and completely-positive trace-preserving (CPTP) maps.
We also explain how these maps and instruments can be represented in various ways.
In Chapter
3
, we overview the formalism of quantum trajectories.
By taking the continuous-time limit of a repeated measurement protocol, we derive a stochastic equation for a quantum trajectory, whose ensemble average leads to the quantum master equation.
We then discuss physical quantities characterizing quantum trajectories, especially nonlinear observables and the statistics of quantum jumps.
We also briefly explain some related concepts, such as numerical methods for quantum trajectories and the quantum diffusion equation.

In Chapter
4
, we discuss the spectral properties of CPTP maps and quantum master equations.
We review some important conditions for the steady states of CPTP maps, such as irreducibility and primitivity, and provide a detailed discussion of the rigorous criteria for these properties.
We also explain their connection to the steady-state properties of quantum master equations.
While most of the content in this chapter is devoted to rigorous discussions of steady-state properties, e.g., uniqueness, we also include one section that overviews miscellaneous recent topics beyond the steady-state properties, such as the spectral gap and spectral statistics.

In Chapter
5
, we discuss the typical properties of quantum trajectories, focusing on the ergodicity of linear observables and purification, on the basis of the seminal results by Kümmerer and Maassen.
We explain the notions of these properties and their relation to the steady-state properties of the averaged dynamics discussed in Chapter
4
.
Instead of rigorous formulations, we try to provide physical intuition for these concepts by presenting simple examples.
In Chapter
6
, we review recent developments on the typical properties of quantum trajectories, i.e., the ergodicity of nonlinear quantities and Lyapunov exponents.
Key results are summarized in Table
1
.
These concepts play a foundational role in understanding measurement-induced phase transitions in Chapter
7
.

Unique SS

5.1
)

6.2
)

6.3.1
)

6.3.2
)

Table 1:

Sufficient conditions for a typical trajectory to satisfy the ergodicity of observables linear or nonlinear in density operators and for the convergence of Lyapunov exponents (LEs) and a nonzero Lyapunov gap.
Here, the ergodicity means the equivalence between the long-time average in a single trajectory and the long-time ensemble average over all trajectories.
These conditions involve the uniqueness and full rankness (positive definiteness) of a steady state (SS) of the corresponding CPTP map, which will be discussed in Chapter
4
, and the purification property of quantum trajectories, which will be discussed in Sec.
5.2
.

In Chapter
7
, we overview the measurement-induced phase transitions of quantum trajectories, which have attracted recent attention as a novel type of non-equilibrium phase transition in quantum many-body systems.
While entanglement and purification transitions are well-known examples of measurement-induced phase transitions, we also discuss the relevance of the Lyapunov spectrum of quantum trajectories, which has been uncovered only recently.
In Chapter
8
, we conclude this review article and state some future prospects.

2
Basics of quantum measurements

In this section, we discuss some of the basics of quantum measurement theory, starting from the review of projective measurements.
We especially introduce some primary concepts required to understand the following sections, such as indirect measurements, CP-instruments, CPTP maps, and various representations of channels and instruments; an interested reader may refer to, e.g., Refs.
[
7
,
36
]
for further details.

2.1
Projective measurement

Let us first consider a projective measurement of an observable
\hat{A}
, which is a Hermitian operator in a set of linear operators acting on the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, denoted as
\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
.
We assume that
\mathcal{H}
is finite-dimensional throughout this paper.
Prepare a density matrix
\hat{\rho}
, which is a normalized (i.e.,
\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\rho}]=1
) positive semidefinite operator in
\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
.
Then, the projective measurement of
\hat{A}
probabilistically transforms
\hat{\rho}
into one of the states corresponding to the projectors
\hat{P}_{\eta}
onto the eigenspaces of
\hat{A}
.
That is, assume that
\hat{A}
is decomposed as

\displaystyle\hat{A}=\sum_{\eta}a_{\eta}\hat{P}_{\eta},

(1)

where
a_{\eta}
are the eigenvalues of
\hat{A}
and
\hat{P}_{\eta}\>(=\hat{P}_{\eta}^{\dagger})
are the Hermitian orthogonal projectors satisfying

\displaystyle\hat{P}_{\eta}\hat{P}_{\eta^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\delta_{\eta\eta^{\prime}}\hat{P}_{\eta},

(2)

\displaystyle\sum_{\eta}\hat{P}_{\eta}

\displaystyle=\hat{\mathbb{I}}.

(3)

Then, when we measure
\hat{\rho}
in the basis of
\{\hat{P}_{\eta}\}_{\eta}
, the normalized post-measurement state becomes

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}^{\prime}_{\eta}=\frac{\hat{P}_{\eta}\hat{\rho}\hat{P}_{\eta}}{p_{\eta}}

(4)

with the Born probability

\displaystyle p_{\eta}=\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{P}_{\eta}\hat{\rho}\hat{P}_{\eta}]=\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\rho}\hat{P}_{\eta}].

(5)

Note that when
a_{\eta}
is degenerate, the rank of
\hat{P}_{\eta}
becomes equal to the degree of the degeneracy.
In particular, if
\hat{A}
is nondegenerate, i.e.,
\hat{P}_{\eta}=\ket{\eta}\bra{\eta}
,
\hat{\rho}_{\eta}^{\prime}
and
p_{\eta}
are simply given by
\hat{\rho}_{\eta}^{\prime}=\ket{\eta}\bra{\eta}
and
p_{\eta}=\braket{\eta|\hat{\rho}|\eta}
, respectively.

2.2
Indirect measurement

While the projective measurement scheme discussed above completely destroys the pre-measurement state, we can instead consider measurements for which the quantum back-action is moderate.
Here, we specifically consider indirect measurements, where we first attach a meter M to the system S, let them interact, and perform the projective measurement on the meter.
See Fig.
1
(a) for the case with a pure state.

Figure 1:

(a) Indirect measurement for the case with a pure state.
We let the state
\hat{\rho}=\ket{\psi}\bra{\psi}
of the system and the state
\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{M}}=\ket{0}\bra{0}
of the meter interact via
\hat{U}_{\mathrm{int}}
and measure the meter’s state.
If the outcome is
b
, the post-measurement state for the system becomes
\hat{\rho}_{b}^{\prime}=\ket{\psi_{b}^{\prime}}\bra{\psi_{b}^{\prime}}
.
(b) Repeated measurement: we repeat the above process (a) and obtain the quantum trajectory
\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}=\ket{\psi_{\bm{b};n}}\bra{\psi_{\bm{b};n}}
depending on the measurement outcomes.

To be more precise, we consider a composite Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{M}}
, where
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}\>(\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{M}})
denotes the Hilbert space for the system (meter).
The initial state is assumed to be

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}\otimes\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{M}}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}],

(6)

where the system and meter are decoupled.
The initial state
\hat{\rho}\otimes\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{M}}
is evolved by a joint unitary operator
\hat{U}_{\mathrm{int}}
as
\hat{U}_{\mathrm{int}}(\hat{\rho}\otimes\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{M}})\hat{U}_{\mathrm{int}}^{\dagger}
, which entangles the system and meter.
For simplicity, we assume that the projection operator for the meter is a rank-one operator,

\displaystyle\hat{P}_{\mathrm{M}b}=\ket{q_{b}}\bra{q_{b}},

(7)

where
\ket{q_{b}}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{M}}
and
b
denotes the label of the measurement outcome.
Then, the post-measurement state reads

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}^{\prime}_{b}\otimes\ket{q_{b}}\bra{q_{b}}=\frac{1}{p_{b}}\braket{q_{b}|\hat{U}_{\mathrm{int}}(\hat{\rho}\otimes\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{M}})\hat{U}_{\mathrm{int}}^{\dagger}|q_{b}}\otimes\ket{q_{b}}\bra{q_{b}}

(8)

with probability

\displaystyle p_{b}=\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{SM}}[(\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathrm{S}}\otimes\hat{P}_{\mathrm{M}b})\hat{U}_{\mathrm{int}}(\hat{\rho}\otimes\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{M}})\hat{U}_{\mathrm{int}}^{\dagger}]=\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{S}}[\braket{q_{b}|\hat{U}_{\mathrm{int}}(\hat{\rho}\otimes\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{M}})\hat{U}_{\mathrm{int}}^{\dagger}|q_{b}}],

(9)

where
\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{SM}}[\cdots]
and
\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{S}}[\cdots]
denote the trace of the composite system (S and M) and the system S alone, respectively.
By writing

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{M}}=\sum_{a}\sigma_{a}\ket{\sigma_{a}}\bra{\sigma_{a}},

(10)

and introducing the measurement operator

\displaystyle\hat{M}_{ab}=\sqrt{\sigma_{a}}\braket{q_{b}|\hat{U}_{\mathrm{int}}|\sigma_{a}},

(11)

we have
3
3

3

In the following, we remove the subscript S for the trace of the system for simplicity, when no ambiguity would arise.

\displaystyle\begin{split}p_{b}&=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{\rho}\sum_{a}\hat{M}_{ab}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{ab}\right],\\
\hat{\rho}^{\prime}_{b}&=\frac{1}{p_{b}}\sum_{a}\hat{M}_{ab}\hat{\rho}\hat{M}_{ab}^{\dagger}.\end{split}

(12)

If we take the average over the outcomes
\{b\}
, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}^{\prime}=\sum_{b}p_{b}\hat{\rho}^{\prime}_{b}=\sum_{ab}\hat{M}_{ab}\hat{\rho}\hat{M}_{ab}^{\dagger}.

(13)

Note that
\sum_{ab}\hat{M}_{ab}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{ab}=\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathrm{S}}
holds, which follows from
\hat{U}_{\mathrm{int}}^{\dagger}\hat{U}_{\mathrm{int}}=\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathrm{SM}}
and
\sum_{b}\ket{q_{b}}\bra{q_{b}}=\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathrm{M}}
.
Then, the mapping of
\hat{\rho}
in the form of Eq. (
13
) is called the Kraus representation.
In that context,
\hat{M}_{ab}
is also called the Kraus operator.

In a special case, we can consider a situation where
\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{M}}=\ket{\sigma}\bra{\sigma}
is a pure state. Then, we can omit the label
a
and find

\displaystyle\begin{split}p_{b}&=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{\rho}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b}\right],\\
\hat{\rho}^{\prime}_{b}&=\frac{1}{p_{b}}\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\rho}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\end{split}

(14)

with

\displaystyle\hat{M}_{b}=\braket{q_{b}|\hat{U}_{\mathrm{int}}|\sigma}.

(15)

2.2.1
Positive operator valued measure (POVM)

Let us define an operator

\displaystyle\hat{E}_{b}=\sum_{a}\hat{M}_{ab}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{ab},

(16)

which satisfies
p_{b}=\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{E}_{b}\hat{\rho}]
.
Then, the operators
\{\hat{E}_{b}\}
satisfy the following two properties:

•

The resolution of identity:

\displaystyle\sum_{b}\hat{E}_{b}=\hat{\mathbb{I}}.

(17)

•

Positive semidefiniteness:

\displaystyle\hat{E}_{b}\succeq 0.

(18)

Here,
\hat{X}\succeq 0
means
\braket{\phi|\hat{X}|\phi}\geq 0
for all
\ket{\phi}\in\mathcal{H}
.
The above positive semidefiniteness obviously follows from
\braket{\phi|\hat{E}_{b}|\phi}=\sum_{a}\|\hat{M}_{ab}\ket{\phi}\|^{2}\geq 0
.

Conversely, if the above two conditions, i.e., the resolution of identity and positive semidefiniteness, are met, the set
\{\hat{E}_{b}\}
is called a positive operator valued measure (POVM).
Note that the POVM is more general than the set of projection operators
\{\hat{P}_{\eta}\}
, which is a special type of POVM.
Indeed, elements of the POVM do not need to be orthogonal to each other
\hat{E}_{b}\hat{E}_{b^{\prime}}\neq\hat{E}_{b}\delta_{bb^{\prime}}
in general, in contrast to the set of projection operators.

2.2.2
Examples

Let us provide two examples of measurement operators and POVMs.
As a first example, we consider a measurement with an error
\epsilon\>(0\leq\epsilon<1)
.
We set the meter’s initial state to
\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{M}}=\ket{0}\bra{0}
and the measurement basis to
\ket{q_{b}}=\ket{0}
or
\ket{1}
.
The joint unitary operator
\hat{U}_{\mathrm{int}}
tries to copy the basis states
\ket{0},\ket{1}
of the system onto the meter; the measurement becomes projective if the copy is perfect (
\epsilon=0
), while it becomes weaker if there is an error in the copying process.
To account for this process, we can set the joint unitary
\hat{U}_{\mathrm{int}}
such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{U}_{\mathrm{int}}\ket{0}\otimes\ket{0}&=\ket{0}\otimes(\sqrt{1-\epsilon}\ket{0}+\sqrt{\epsilon}\ket{1}),\\
\hat{U}_{\mathrm{int}}\ket{1}\otimes\ket{0}&=\ket{1}\otimes(\sqrt{1-\epsilon}\ket{1}+\sqrt{\epsilon}\ket{0}).\end{split}

(19)

Using Eq. (
15
), we find

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{M}_{0}&=\sqrt{1-\epsilon}\ket{0}\bra{0}+\sqrt{\epsilon}\ket{1}\bra{1},\\
\hat{M}_{1}&=\sqrt{1-\epsilon}\ket{1}\bra{1}+\sqrt{\epsilon}\ket{0}\bra{0}.\end{split}

(20)

The corresponding POVM reads

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{E}_{0}&=({1-\epsilon})\ket{0}\bra{0}+{\epsilon}\ket{1}\bra{1},\\
\hat{E}_{1}&=({1-\epsilon})\ket{1}\bra{1}+{\epsilon}\ket{0}\bra{0}\end{split}

(21)

with
\hat{E}_{0}+\hat{E}_{1}=\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathrm{S}}
.
Note that
\hat{E}_{0}\hat{E}_{1}\neq 0
unless
\epsilon=0
(projective measurement).

Another example is the swapping between the system and meter.
We again set the meter’s initial state to
\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{M}}=\ket{0}\bra{0}
and the measurement basis to
\ket{q_{b}}=\ket{0}
or
\ket{1}
.
The joint unitary operator describing the interaction is taken as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{U}_{\mathrm{int}}\ket{0}\otimes\ket{0}&=\ket{0}\otimes\ket{0},\\
\hat{U}_{\mathrm{int}}\ket{1}\otimes\ket{0}&=\cos\theta\ket{1}\otimes\ket{0}+\sin\theta\ket{0}\otimes\ket{1}.\end{split}

(22)

In this case, the measurement operators become

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{M}_{0}&=\ket{0}\bra{0}+\cos\theta\ket{1}\bra{1},\\
\hat{M}_{1}&=\sin\theta\ket{0}\bra{1}.\end{split}

(23)

The POVM reads

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{E}_{0}&=\ket{0}\bra{0}+\cos^{2}\theta\ket{1}\bra{1},\\
\hat{E}_{1}&=\sin^{2}\theta\ket{1}\bra{1}\end{split}

(24)

with
\hat{E}_{0}+\hat{E}_{1}=\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathrm{S}}
.
We find that
\hat{E}_{0}\hat{E}_{1}\neq 0
unless
\theta=m\pi/2
with
m
being integer.
Physically, when
\sin\theta=\pm 1
, this can be regarded as a toy model for the spontaneous emission of an atom.
Namely, an excited atom
\ket{1}
(system) becomes a ground state
\ket{0}
with emitting a photon to the vacuum
\ket{0}
(meter).
Note that a more sophisticated treatment to describe the spontaneous emission is discussed in Sec.
3.2.4
using continuous-time quantum trajectories.

2.3
CP-instrument and CPTP map

Let us next discuss how the measurement process and the change of a quantum state are characterized in a more abstract manner.
For this purpose, we first note that the post-measurement state
\hat{\rho}_{b}^{\prime}
is rewritten as

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{b}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{p_{b}}\mathcal{E}_{b}[\hat{\rho}],

(25)

where
\mathcal{E}_{b}:\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}]\rightarrow\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}]
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{b}[\hat{\rho}]=\sum_{a}\hat{M}_{ab}\hat{\rho}\hat{M}_{ab}^{\dagger}.

(26)

Here,
\{\mathcal{E}_{b}\}
satisfies the following properties:

(a)

The sum of
\mathcal{E}_{b}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}=\sum_{b}\mathcal{E}_{b},

(27)

is trace preserving (TP): a linear map
\mathcal{F}:\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}]\to\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}]
is called TP if

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}[\mathcal{F}[\hat{X}]]=\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{X}]

(28)

for any
\hat{X}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}]
.

(b)

Every
\mathcal{E}_{b}
is completely positive (CP): a linear map
\mathcal{F}:\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}]\to\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}]
is called CP if

\displaystyle(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{A}})[\hat{R}]\succeq 0

(29)

for any auxiliary system A with Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{A}}
and for any positive semidefinite operator
\hat{R}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{A}}]
(i.e.,
\hat{R}\succeq 0
).
Here,
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{A}}:\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{A}}]\to\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{A}}]
is the identity map on A.

In general, the set of linear maps
\{\mathcal{E}_{b}\}
satisfying (a) and (b) above is called a CP-instrument and characterizes measurement processes.
For the Kraus representation in Eq. (
26
), (a) readily follows from the condition
\sum_{ab}\hat{M}_{ab}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{ab}=\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathrm{S}}
, and (b) follows because we can write

\displaystyle(\mathcal{E}_{b}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{A}})[\hat{R}]=\sum_{a}(\hat{M}_{ab}\otimes\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathrm{A}})\hat{R}(\hat{M}_{ab}\otimes\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathrm{A}})^{\dagger}=\sum_{a}\hat{X}_{ab}^{\dagger}\hat{X}_{ab}\succeq 0,

(30)

where
\hat{X}_{ab}=\sqrt{\hat{R}}(\hat{M}_{ab}\otimes\mathbb{I}_{\mathrm{A}})^{\dagger}
is well-defined since
\hat{R}
is positive semidefinite.

Let us explain the physical meanings of (a) and (b).
Since the change of a state averaged over the measurement outcomes is given by
\mathcal{E}[\hat{\rho}]
, the TP condition for
\mathcal{E}
means that the net probability is kept normalized.
For CP, we first note that it is stronger than the condition for the positivity of a map, which requires that
\mathcal{E}_{b}[\hat{\rho}]\succeq 0
for all
\hat{\rho}\succeq 0
.
The positivity ensures that a state after applying the map is positive semidefinite, given that the initial state is positive semidefinite.
However, CP requires more than that, and we allow attaching any auxiliary systems.

One concrete example of a map that is positive but not CP is the matrix transpose in a certain basis,
\mathcal{T}[\hat{\rho}]=\hat{\rho}^{\mathsf{T}}
4
4

4

For qubits, we expand
\hat{\rho}
in the computational basis states as
\hat{\rho}=\sum_{ij}\rho_{ij}\ket{i}\bra{j}
and define
\hat{\rho}^{\mathsf{T}}=\sum_{ij}\rho_{ji}\ket{i}\bra{j}
.

.
Specifically, let us consider one qubit for S and another qubit for A.
If we apply the map
(\mathcal{T}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{A}})
to the Bell state

\displaystyle\hat{R}=\frac{\ket{00}\bra{00}+\ket{00}\bra{11}+\ket{11}\bra{00}+\ket{11}\bra{11}}{2}\succeq 0,

(31)

we have

\displaystyle(\mathcal{T}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{A}})[\hat{R}]=\frac{\ket{00}\bra{00}+\ket{10}\bra{01}+\ket{01}\bra{10}+\ket{11}\bra{11}}{2},

(32)

which has a negative eigenvalue and thus is not positive semidefinite.
Therefore,
\mathcal{T}
is not CP, while it is clearly positive.
Note that if
\hat{R}
is taken to be a separable state
\hat{R}=\sum_{k}q_{k}\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{S},k}\otimes\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{A},k}
with some probability
\{q_{k}\}
,
(\mathcal{T}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{A}})[\hat{R}]=\sum_{k}q_{k}\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{S},k}^{\mathsf{T}}\otimes\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{A},k}
becomes positive semidefinite.
Therefore, the negative eigenvalues of
(\mathcal{T}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{A}})[\hat{R}]
mean that
\hat{R}
is entangled.
This is known as the positive partial transpose (PPT) criterion
[
51
,
52
]
to detect the entanglement of mixed states
5
5

5

Note that this criterion is independent of the basis used to define the transposition, since a basis change does not alter the eigenvalues of the partially transposed matrix
[
36
]
.

.

Similar to the CP-instrument, we can consider a linear map
\mathcal{E}
that is both TP and CP.
Such a map is called a CPTP map and characterizes general quantum channels.
By definition, a quantum channel
\mathcal{E}=\sum_{b}\mathcal{E}_{b}
is a CPTP map if the set
\{\mathcal{E}_{b}\}
is a CP-instrument.
However, some CPTP maps may not be associated with a physicalmeasurement process and can describe more general dynamics in open quantum systems.

2.4
Representations

In the previous section, we have seen that the map in Eq. (
26
), which is given in the Kraus representation, satisfies the conditions for a CP-instrument.
Here, we discuss that the converse is also true: any CP-instrument can be represented using the Kraus operators.
For this purpose, we introduce another representation, called the Choi-Jamiołkowski representation.
We also discuss other types of representations, such as the Stinespring representation and the “natural” representation.
See Refs.
[
36
,
37
]
for further details.

2.4.1
Choi-Jamiołkowski representation

We first consider how CP of a linear map
\mathcal{F}
can be characterized.
To this end, we introduce the Choi-Jamiołkowski representation
\hat{F}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}]
of a map
\mathcal{F}:\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}]\rightarrow\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}]
6
6

6

Two remarks are in order.
First, in general, we can consider maps
\mathcal{F}:\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}_{1}]\rightarrow\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}_{2}]
where
\mathcal{H}_{1}
and
\mathcal{H}_{2}
are different.
However, for simplicity, we only consider the case where they are the same.
Second, while Eq. (
33
) is often called the Choi-Jamiołkowski isomorphism, the original formulations by Choi
[
53
]
and Jamiołkowski
[
54
]
are slightly different
[
55
]
.

defined as

\displaystyle\hat{F}=(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{S}})[\ket{\Phi}\bra{\Phi}]=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{ij}\mathcal{F}[\ket{i}\bra{j}]\otimes\ket{i}\bra{j},

(33)

where
\ket{\Phi}
is the maximally entangled state between the original system and the copied system,

\displaystyle\ket{\Phi}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\sum_{i}\ket{ii}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}},

(34)

with
d=\mathrm{dim}[\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}]
.
Note that we can easily show that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{S}}[\hat{A}\mathcal{F}[\hat{\rho}]]=d\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{SS}^{c}}[\hat{F}(\hat{A}\otimes\hat{\rho}^{\mathsf{T}})]

(35)

for every
\hat{A}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}]
and
\hat{\rho}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}]
, where the subscript
\mathrm{S}
(
\mathrm{S}^{c}
) means that the trace is taken over the original (copied) system.
This means that

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[\hat{\rho}]=d\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{S}^{c}}[\hat{F}(\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathrm{S}}\otimes\hat{\rho}^{\mathsf{T}})],

(36)

which indicates that
\hat{F}
and
\mathcal{F}
are in one-to-one correspondence.

Notably, CP becomes evident in the Choi-Jamiołkowski representation.
That is,

\displaystyle\hat{F}\succeq 0\Longleftrightarrow\text{$\mathcal{F}$ is CP.}

(37)

The fact for
(\Longleftarrow)
follows from the definition of CP.
To see the other direction
(\Longrightarrow)

[
36
]
, we first notice that
(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{A}})[\hat{R}]\succeq 0
is ensured if
(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{A}})[\ket{\phi_{k}}\bra{\phi_{k}}]\succeq 0
for every
k
, where
\ket{\phi_{k}}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{A}}
are the eigenstates of
\hat{R}
, i.e.,
\hat{R}=\sum_{k}r_{k}\ket{\phi_{k}}\bra{\phi_{k}}
with
r_{k}\geq 0
.
Then, for each
\ket{\phi_{k}}
, we can find a map
\hat{V}_{k}:\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{A}}
such that
7
7

7

To see this, let us consider the Schmidt decomposition of
\ket{\phi}
between S and A, which is given by
\ket{\phi}=\sum_{l=1}^{d}\beta_{l}\ket{e_{l}}\otimes\ket{E_{l}}
where
\beta_{l}\geq 0
are the Schmidt coefficients.
Here, we have omitted the subscript
k
for simplicity.
Introducing
\hat{v}_{1}=\sum_{l=1}^{d}\ket{e_{l}}\bra{l}
and
\hat{v}_{2}=\sum_{l=1}^{d}\beta_{l}\ket{E_{l}}\bra{l}
, we find
\ket{\phi}=\sqrt{d}\hat{v}_{1}\otimes\hat{v}_{2}\ket{\Phi}=\sqrt{d}(\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathrm{S}}\otimes\hat{v}_{2})(\hat{v}_{1}\otimes\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathrm{S}})\ket{\Phi}
.
Since
\ket{\Phi}
is the maximally entangled state, we have
(\hat{v}_{1}\otimes\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathrm{S}})\ket{\Phi}=(\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathrm{S}}\otimes\hat{v}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}})\ket{\Phi}
.
Then, we can take
\hat{V}=\sqrt{d}\hat{v}_{2}\hat{v}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}
.

\displaystyle\ket{\phi_{k}}=(\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathrm{S}}\otimes\hat{V}_{k})\ket{\Phi}.

(38)

Therefore,

\displaystyle(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{A}})[\ket{\phi_{k}}\bra{\phi_{k}}]=(\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathrm{S}}\otimes\hat{V}_{k})(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{S}})[\ket{\Phi}\bra{\Phi}](\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathrm{S}}\otimes\hat{V}_{k})^{\dagger}\succeq 0

(39)

if
\hat{F}=(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{S}})[\ket{\Phi}\bra{\Phi}]\succeq 0
.

2.4.2
Kraus representation

Using the above result, we can further show the equivalence between the Kraus representation and CP map
[
36
]
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\text{ is represented as }\mathcal{F}[\hat{\rho}]=\sum_{a}\hat{M}_{a}\hat{\rho}\hat{M}_{a}^{\dagger}\Longleftrightarrow\text{$\mathcal{F}$ is CP.}

(40)

The fact for
(\Longrightarrow)
was already discussed in Eq. (
30
).
To see
(\Longleftarrow)
, we first note that the corresponding Choi-Jamiołkowski representation of the CP map,
\hat{F}
, satisfies
\hat{F}\succeq 0
, as described in Eq. (
37
).
By decomposing
\hat{F}
as
\hat{F}=\sum_{a=1}^{r}f_{a}\ket{f_{a}}\bra{f_{a}}\>(f_{a}\geq 0)
and using
\ket{f_{a}}=(\hat{m}_{a}\otimes\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathrm{S}})\ket{\Phi}
for some operator
\hat{m}_{a}
as in Eq. (
38
), we have

\displaystyle\hat{F}=\sum_{a=1}^{r}(\hat{M}_{a}\otimes\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathrm{S}})\ket{\Phi}\bra{\Phi}(\hat{M}_{a}\otimes\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathrm{S}})^{\dagger},

(41)

where
\hat{M}_{a}=\sqrt{f_{a}}\hat{m}_{a}
.
Comparing Eq. (
41
) with the definition of
\hat{F}
in Eq. (
33
) and recalling that
\hat{F}
and
\mathcal{F}
are in one-to-one correspondence, we can conclude that
\mathcal{F}
admits a Kraus representation.
From this construction, we can see that
r\geq\mathrm{rank}[\hat{F}]
, where
\mathrm{rank}[\hat{F}]\leq d^{2}
.

Therefore, each element in a CP-instrument
\{\mathcal{E}_{b}\}_{b}
can be given in the Kraus representation by Eq. (
26
) because of CP of
\mathcal{E}_{b}
.
Furthermore, the TP condition for
\sum_{b}\mathcal{E}_{b}
leads to
\sum_{ab}\hat{M}_{ab}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{ab}=\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathrm{S}}
.
Likewise, a CPTP map
\mathcal{E}
is represented as

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}[\hat{\rho}]=\sum_{k}\hat{M}_{k}\hat{\rho}\hat{M}_{k}^{\dagger},

(42)

where

\displaystyle\sum_{k}\hat{M}_{k}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{k}=\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathrm{S}}

(43)

is satisfied.

2.4.3
Stinespring representation

We have another representation of a map, which is relevant for the setup of open quantum systems where the bath is traced out after unitary time evolution.
Let
\mathcal{F}:\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}]\rightarrow\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}]
be a CP map.
Then, there exist a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{E}}
and an operator
\hat{V}:\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{E}}
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[\hat{\rho}]=\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{E}}[\hat{V}\hat{\rho}\hat{V}^{\dagger}],

(44)

which is called the Stinespring representation
[
56
]
.
This representation can be constructed as follows.
Since
\mathcal{F}
is CP, it admits a Kraus representation
\mathcal{F}[\hat{\rho}]=\sum_{a=1}^{r}\hat{M}_{a}\hat{\rho}\hat{M}_{a}^{\dagger}
.
If we define

\displaystyle\hat{V}=\sum_{a=1}^{r}\hat{M}_{a}\otimes\ket{a},

(45)

where
\{\ket{a}\}
is an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{E}}
, we find the representation in Eq. (
44
).
This construction implies that the Stinespring representation is possible whenever
\mathrm{dim}[\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{E}}]\geq\mathrm{rank}[\hat{F}]
(since the minimum value of
r
is
\mathrm{rank}[\hat{F}]
).
Moreover, if
\mathcal{F}
is a CPTP map, we find from Eq. (
45
) that
\hat{V}
becomes an isometry, i.e.,
\hat{V}^{\dagger}\hat{V}=\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathrm{S}}
.

Now, since the isometry
\hat{V}
can be written as
\hat{V}=\hat{U}(\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathrm{S}}\otimes\ket{\psi})
, where
\hat{U}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{E}}]
is unitary and
\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{E}}
, we find
[
36
,
57
]

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}[\hat{\rho}]=\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{E}}[\hat{U}(\hat{\rho}\otimes\ket{\psi}\bra{\psi})\hat{U}^{\dagger}]

(46)

for a CPTP map
\mathcal{E}
.
This representation, which is slightly different from the original Stinespring representation, offers an intuitive picture for describing open quantum systems: attach an environment
\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{E}}
to the system’s state
\hat{\rho}
, let them interact via the unitary
\hat{U}
, and trace out the environmental degree’s of freedom.

Similarly, it is known that a CP-instrument is represented as
[
36
,
58
]

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{b}[\hat{\rho}]=\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{E}}[\hat{U}(\hat{\rho}\otimes\ket{\psi}\bra{\psi})\hat{U}^{\dagger}(\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathrm{S}}\otimes\hat{P}_{E,b})],

(47)

which corresponds to the decomposition of Eq. (
46
) into
\mathcal{E}_{b}
.
Here,
\hat{P}_{E,b}
is the projection operator onto the basis
b
in the environment.
This representation reminds us of the setup of the indirect measurement, which we saw in Eq. (
6
).

2.4.4
Natural representation

Finally, we show a simple representation of a map, which is called, e.g., the natural representation
[
37
]
.
We first consider the vectorization of an operator
\hat{A}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}]\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}
as

\displaystyle\hat{A}=\sum_{ij}A_{ij}\ket{i}\bra{j}\longmapsto\ket{A}=\sum_{ij}A_{ij}\ket{i}\otimes\ket{j}.

(48)

In this formulation, we can easily confirm that

\displaystyle\hat{B}\hat{A}\hat{C}\longmapsto(\hat{B}\otimes\hat{C}^{\mathsf{T}})\ket{A}.

(49)

Now, a linear map
\mathcal{F}:\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}]\rightarrow\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}]
from
\hat{\rho}
to
\hat{\rho}^{\prime}=\mathcal{F}[\hat{\rho}]

can be represented by
\hat{\mathcal{F}}:\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{S}}
from
\ket{\rho}
to
\ket{\rho^{\prime}}
.
When
\mathcal{F}
is a CP map, using the Kraus representation of
\mathcal{F}
and Eq. (
49
),
we find

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{F}}=\sum_{a}\hat{M}_{a}\otimes\hat{M}_{a}^{*}.

(50)

We stress that the natural representation is different from the Choi-Jamiołkowski representation.
While the natural representation is naive, it is not straightforward to judge CP of
\mathcal{F}
from the natural representation
\mathcal{\hat{F}}
, unlike the Choi-Jamiołkowski representation
\hat{F}
in Eq. (
33
).
We also note that Eq. (
50
) is intuitively understood as an interaction between the “ket space” and “bra space.”
If we consider a one-dimensional chain of qudits, the total system is given by a ladder composed of the two Hilbert spaces; therefore, this representation is also called the ladder representation
[
59
]
.

3
Quantum trajectories and quantum master equation

In this section, we discuss the quantum dynamics driven by repeated measurements.
Because of quantum back-action, the time-evolving state depends on the set of random measurement outcomes, which determines the so-called quantum trajectory
8
8

8

The term “quantum trajectory” is often used to describe the trajectory of a quantum stochastic equation under continuous-time measurements
[
7
]
.
However, in this review, we also use the term to describe stochastic time evolution under discrete-time measurements.

.
By taking the continuous-time limit, one finds a quantum stochastic equation that describes continuous-time quantum trajectories
[
22
,
23
,
24
,
25
]
(see Refs.
[
60
,
61
,
62
]
for mathematical foundations).
If we average the dynamics over measurement outcomes, we obtain a quantum master equation, especially the Gorini–Kossakowski–Sudarshan–Lindblad (GKSL) equation.
The purpose of this section is to introduce these basic concepts, as well as to review some related topics.

3.1
Repeated measurements

As discussed in the previous chapter, a measurement on an initial state
\hat{\rho}_{0}
with the outcome
b
changes the state as in Eq. (
25
), where
\mathcal{E}_{b}
is given in the Kraus representation by Eq. (
26
).
Averaging
\mathcal{E}_{b}[\hat{\rho}_{0}]
over all possible measurement outcomes leads to the CPTP map

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{1}=\sum_{b}\mathcal{E}_{b}[\hat{\rho}_{0}]=\mathcal{E}[\hat{\rho}_{0}].

(51)

We now consider repeating the above process.
Assuming that the measurement on the meter is the same at each step, i.e., if the outcome at
s
th measurement is given by
b_{s}=0,\dots,B\in\mathbb{N}
with
s=1,2,\ldots
, a quantum trajectory is described by an infinite sequence of measurement outcomes

\displaystyle\bm{b}=(b_{1},b_{2},\cdots).

(52)

See Fig.
1
(b) for the pure-state case.
If we assume the Kraus representation given in Eq. (
14
)
9
9

9

In the following, we consider the case where the initial state of the meter is pure and thus the summation over
a
is not necessary.

, we find that a state after
n
measurements reads

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}=\frac{\mathcal{E}_{b_{n}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{E}_{b_{1}}[\hat{\rho}_{0}]}{p_{\bm{b};n}}=\frac{\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n}\hat{\rho}_{0}\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n}^{\dagger}}{p_{\bm{b};n}}.

(53)

Here,
\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n}
is the product of Kraus operators

\displaystyle\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n}=\hat{M}_{b_{n}}\hat{M}_{b_{n-1}}\cdots\hat{M}_{b_{1}},

(54)

corresponding to the sequence of measurement outcomes up to step
n
,

\displaystyle\bm{b}_{n}=(b_{1},\cdots,b_{n}),

(55)

and
p_{\bm{b};n}
is the probability that
\bm{b}_{n}
is realized:

\displaystyle p_{\bm{b};n}=\mathrm{Tr}[\mathcal{E}_{b_{n}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{E}_{b_{1}}[\hat{\rho}_{0}]].

(56)

In particular, if the initial state is a pure state,
\hat{\rho}_{0}=\ket{\psi_{0}}\bra{\psi_{0}}
, the post-measurement state remains pure and is given by

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{\bm{b};n}}=\frac{\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n}\ket{\psi_{0}}}{\sqrt{p_{\bm{b};n}}}

(57)

with

\displaystyle p_{\bm{b};n}=\braket{\psi_{0}|\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n}^{\dagger}\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n}|\psi_{0}}=\|\hat{\sf{M}}_{\bm{b};n}|\psi_{0}\rangle\|^{2}.

(58)

Equations (
53
) and (
57
) show that the time-evolving state is given by a conditional state that depends on the sequence of outcomes
\bm{b}
under repeated measurements.
We refer to this sequence of conditional states as a quantum trajectory.
Note that the average over all possible measurement outcomes leads to the CPTP map,

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{n}=\mathbb{E}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}]=\sum_{\bm{b}_{n}}p_{\bm{b};n}\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}=\mathcal{E}^{n}[\hat{\rho}_{0}].

(59)

Here and hereafter, we denote by
\mathbb{E}
the average over all possible sequences of measurement outcomes
\bm{b}
.
We again stress that the quantum trajectory in Eq. (
57
) remains pure under time evolution, whereas the averaged dynamics generally leads to mixed states.

Before ending this section, we briefly comment on our notation, e.g.,
\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}
.
While the state
\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}
is essentially determined only by a finite sequence
\bm{b}_{n}=(b_{1},\cdots,b_{n})
, we keep the entire
\bm{b}
in the subscript.
This is in order to view this state as the
n
th-step state of a quantum trajectory determined from all infinite sequences of measurement outcomes
\bm{b}
.
This view is important to define the probability measure of quantum trajectories and the average
\mathbb{E}
defined from it.
Since this measure and average do not depend on the specific time step
n
, it is convenient to describe, e.g., multi-time correlation functions.

3.2
Continuous-time limit of repeated measurements

3.2.1
Continuous-time limit

We next consider a system that is continuously measured, which can be regarded as the continuous-time limit of the repeated indirect measurements discussed above
[
60
,
61
,
62
,
7
,
35
]
.
For this purpose, let us assume a simple situation where the state of the meter is given by

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{M}}=\ket{0}\bra{0},

(60)

and the interaction
\hat{V}_{\mathrm{int}}
between the system and the meter before the measurement acts over an infinitesimal time interval
dt
.
Specifically, the joint unitary evolution is given by

\displaystyle\hat{U}_{\mathrm{int}}=e^{-i\hat{V}_{\mathrm{int}}dt}\simeq\mathbb{\hat{I}}_{\mathrm{SM}}+\mathcal{O}(dt).

(61)

Let us assume that the projective measurement on the meter is performed in an orthonormal basis
\{\ket{b}\}\>(b=0,\cdots,B)
, which includes the meter’s initial state
\ket{0}
.
Then, since the interaction time is small, we expect that the measurement outcome is mostly
b=0
, whereas the other outcomes
b\geq 1
occur with a small probability proportional to
dt
.
From this observation, we can scale the measurement operators in Eq. (
14
) for
b\geq 1
as

\displaystyle\hat{M}_{b\geq 1}=\sqrt{dt}\hat{L}_{b}.

(62)

Indeed, we find that the probability becomes proportional to
dt
with this choice,

\displaystyle p_{b\geq 1}(t)=\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\rho}(t)\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{b}]dt,

(63)

where
\hat{\rho}(t)
is the pre-measurement state at time
t
.
The post-measurement state is given by

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{b\geq 1}(t+dt)=\frac{\hat{L}_{b}\hat{\rho}(t)\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}}{\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\rho}(t)\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{b}]}.

(64)

Next, we notice that
\sum_{b=0}^{B}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b}=\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathrm{S}}
.
By requiring this relation to hold up to the order of
dt
, we find
\hat{M}_{0}=\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathrm{S}}-i\hat{H}_{\mathrm{S}}dt
in the absence of the measurement, where
\hat{H}_{\mathrm{S}}
is an intrinsic Hamiltonian of the system.
Then, we find that
\hat{M}_{0}
should have the form

\displaystyle\hat{M}_{0}=\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{\mathrm{S}}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{b\geq 1}\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{b}dt-i\hat{H}_{\mathrm{S}}dt.

(65)

Up to the order of
dt
, the corresponding probability is given by

\displaystyle p_{0}(t)=1-\sum_{b\geq 1}\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\rho}(t)\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{b}]dt,

(66)

and the post-measurement state is

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{b=0}(t+dt)=\hat{\rho}(t)-i[\hat{H}_{\mathrm{S}},\hat{\rho}(t)]dt+\hat{\rho}(t)\sum_{b\geq 1}\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\rho}(t)\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{b}]dt-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{b\geq 1}\{\hat{\rho}(t),\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{b}\}dt.

(67)

To summarize, after the small duration time
dt
, there are two possibilities.
One is that the meter does not change its value from
\ket{0}
.
In this case, the system undergoes a continuous-time evolution as given in Eq. (
67
).
The other is that the meter changes its value to
b\geq 1
.
In this case, the system suddenly changes its state discontinuously as in Eq. (
64
), which is called a quantum jump.

Figure 2:

Schematic illustration of a quantum trajectory.
Starting from
\ket{\psi(0)}
, the state undergoes a non-Hermitian continuous time evolution governed by
\hat{H}_{\mathrm{eff}}
, unless a quantum jump described by the jump operator
\hat{L}_{b}
occurs, which suddenly changes the state.
Here, the jump times are denoted as
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
.

If we assume that the meter’s state is immediately reset to
\ket{0}
after a quantum jump
b\geq 1
, the time evolution of the system consists of discrete quantum jumps interspersed with continuous time evolutions, as illustrated in Fig.
2
.
Importantly, if we postselect a trajectory
\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{no}}(t)
free from quantum jumps at all times, i.e.,
b=0
for all
t
, the time evolution of the system is given by

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{no}}(t+dt)=\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{no}}(t)-i\left(\hat{H}_{\mathrm{eff}}\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{no}}(t)-\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{no}}(t)\hat{H}_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\dagger}\right)dt+\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{no}}(t)\sum_{b\geq 1}\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{no}}(t)\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{b}]dt,

(68)

where

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{\mathrm{eff}}=\hat{H}_{\mathrm{S}}-\frac{i}{2}\sum_{b\geq 1}\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{b}

(69)

is the effective non-Hermitian Hamiltonian.
Equation (
68
) represents a simple non-Hermitian time evolution with the third term on the right-hand side ensuring the normalization of
\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{no}}(t)
.
Indeed, the formal solution of Eq. (
68
) is

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{no}}(t)=\frac{e^{-i\hat{H}_{\mathrm{eff}}t}\hat{\rho}(0)e^{i\hat{H}_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\dagger}t}}{\mathrm{Tr}[e^{-i\hat{H}_{\mathrm{eff}}t}\hat{\rho}(0)e^{i\hat{H}_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\dagger}t}]}.

(70)

This type of non-Hermitian dynamics has been extensively investigated in recent years
[
42
]
, while it requires postselecting a rare trajectory without any jumps, which occurs with an exponentially small probability in time.

3.2.2
Stochastic equation

Let us denote the number of jumps for
b\>(\geq 1)
up to time
t
by
N_{b}(t)
.
Then, we can consider the increment of the jump number,
dN_{b}(t)
, which is 1 only if there is a quantum jump at time
t
10
10

10

More precisely, we consider a quantum jump that occurs during
[t,t+dt]
.

with type
b
and is 0 otherwise.
From this definition, we have

\displaystyle dN_{b}(t)dN_{b^{\prime}}(t)=\delta_{bb^{\prime}}dN_{b}(t).

(71)

Moreover, following the discussion in the previous subsection [especially Eq. (
63
)], we obtain

\displaystyle p_{b}^{c}(t):=\mathrm{Prob}_{t}[dN_{b}(t)=1|\hat{\rho}_{c}(t)]=dt\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\rho}_{c}(t)\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{b}]=dt\braket{\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{b}}_{c;t},

(72)

where
c
indicates that we consider a quantum trajectory without average
11
11

11

Note that the subscript/superscript
c
will be omitted if it is apparent that we consider quantum trajectories from the context.
We also sometimes omit the explicit time-dependence (
t
) of the state when it will raise no confusion.

,
\braket{\cdots}_{c;t}:=\mathrm{Tr}[{\hat{\rho}_{c}(t)\cdots}]
, and
\mathrm{Prob}_{t}[\cdots|\hat{\rho}_{c}(t)]
denotes a conditional probability at time
t
for a given state
\hat{\rho}_{c}(t)
.

Using this notation, the possibilities given in Eqs. (
64
) and (
67
) are unified as

\displaystyle d\hat{\rho}_{c}=-i[\hat{H}_{\mathrm{S}},\hat{\rho}_{c}]dt+\hat{\rho}_{c}\sum_{b\geq 1}\braket{\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{b}}_{c;t}dt-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{b\geq 1}\{\hat{\rho}_{c},\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{b}\}dt+\sum_{b\geq 1}\left(\frac{\hat{L}_{b}\hat{\rho}_{c}\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}}{\braket{\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{b}}_{c;t}}-\hat{\rho}_{c}\right)dN_{b}.

(73)

We note that for pure states, the corresponding equation reads

\displaystyle d\ket{\psi_{c}}=\left(-i\hat{H}_{\mathrm{eff}}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{b\geq 1}\braket{\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{b}}_{c;t}\right)\ket{\psi_{c}}dt+\sum_{b\geq 1}\left(\frac{\hat{L}_{b}}{\sqrt{\braket{\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{b}}}_{c;t}}-1\right)\ket{\psi_{c}}dN_{b}.

(74)

We can see this by noticing, e.g., that
d[\ket{\psi_{c}}\bra{\psi_{c}}]=\ket{d\psi_{c}}\bra{\psi_{c}}+\ket{\psi_{c}}\bra{d\psi_{c}}+\ket{d\psi_{c}}\bra{d\psi_{c}}
,
dN_{b}dt=0
, and
dN_{b}dN_{b^{\prime}}=\delta_{bb^{\prime}}dN_{b}
.

A single quantum trajectory is characterized by the set of jump times
\{\tau_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
and the corresponding jump types
\{b_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, i.e.,
\{(\tau_{k},b_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
dN_{b_{k}}(\tau_{k})=1
.
For pure states, the quantum trajectory after
K
jumps is represented as

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{c}(t)}\propto e^{-i\hat{H}_{\mathrm{eff}}(t-\tau_{K})}\hat{L}_{b_{K}}e^{-i\hat{H}_{\mathrm{eff}}(\tau_{K}-\tau_{K-1})}\hat{L}_{b_{K-1}}\cdots e^{-i\hat{H}_{\mathrm{eff}}(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1})}\hat{L}_{b_{1}}e^{-i\hat{H}_{\mathrm{eff}}\tau_{1}}\ket{\psi(0)}

(75)

with
\braket{\psi_{c}(t)|\psi_{c}(t)}=1
.

3.2.3
Averaged dynamics

If we average Eq. (
73
) over the measurement outcomes, we obtain the renowned GKSL equation
[
63
,
64
]
.
To be specific, we treat the dynamics of

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}(t)=\mathbb{E}[\hat{\rho}_{c}(t)],

(76)

where
\mathbb{E}
denotes the average over all quantum trajectories, i.e., over
dN_{b}(t)
for all
t
.
To proceed, we note that for a function
g
of
\hat{\rho}_{c}(t)

[
35
]
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[g(\hat{\rho}_{c}(t))dN_{b}(t)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{[0,t)}[\mathbb{E}_{t}[g(\hat{\rho}_{c}(t))dN_{b}(t)|\hat{\rho}_{c}(t)]]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{[0,t)}[g(\hat{\rho}_{c}(t))\braket{\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{b}}_{c;t}dt]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[g(\hat{\rho}_{c}(t))\braket{\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{b}}_{c;t}]dt,

(77)

where
\mathbb{E}_{[0,t)}
and
\mathbb{E}_{t}
denote the average over
dN_{b}(t^{\prime})
with
0\leq t^{\prime}<t
and
t^{\prime}=t
, respectively, and
\mathbb{E}_{t}[\cdots|\hat{\rho}_{c}(t)]
denotes the conditional average for a given state
\hat{\rho}_{c}(t)
.
In Eq. (
3.2.3
), we first take the average over
dN_{b}(t)
using Eq. (
72
) for the given
\hat{\rho}_{c}(t)
and then take the average over
\hat{\rho}_{c}(t)
(i.e., taking the average over
dN_{b}(t^{\prime})
for
0\leq t^{\prime}<t
).
We have also used the fact that the average over
dN_{b}(t^{\prime})
for
t<t^{\prime}
, which is included in the average
\mathbb{E}
, does not change the result in obtaining the third line from the second line.
Applying this equality to Eq. (
73
), we find the GKSL equation

\displaystyle\frac{d\hat{\rho}(t)}{dt}=\mathcal{L}[\hat{\rho}(t)]=-i[\hat{H}_{\mathrm{S}},\hat{\rho}(t)]+\sum_{b\geq 1}\left(\hat{L}_{b}\hat{\rho}(t)\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\{\hat{\rho}(t),\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{b}\}\right).

(78)

This GKSL equation leads to the formal solution

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}(t)=e^{\mathcal{L}t}[\hat{\rho}(0)],

(79)

which is a CPTP map.
The map
\mathcal{E}_{t}=e^{\mathcal{L}t}
satisfies the Markovian condition
\mathcal{E}_{t}\circ\mathcal{E}_{s}=\mathcal{E}_{t+s}
for
t,s\geq 0
.
It is also known that the generator of a Markovian CPTP map has the GKSL form, where
\hat{H}_{\mathrm{S}}
and
\hat{L}_{b}
can be time-dependent in general
[
63
,
6
]
.

3.2.4
Examples

Here, we first discuss an example of the spontaneous emission of a two-level atom.
For simplicity, we consider a toy model where the two-level system is coupled to a single-mode photon
12
12

12

This is a simple toy model.
In reality, we need to consider various modes of the photon, but we neglect them.

, which serves as a meter.
The interaction Hamiltonian, after the rotating-wave approximation
[
65
]
, reads

\displaystyle\hat{V}_{\mathrm{int}}=g(\hat{\sigma}^{-}\hat{b}^{\dagger}+\hat{\sigma}^{+}\hat{b}),

(80)

where
\hat{\sigma}^{+/-}
denotes the raising/lowering operator for the atom and
\hat{b}
is the annihilation operator of the photon.
We assume that there is no photon initially,
\ket{0}_{\mathrm{ph}}
.

Consider a measurement of the photon number after a short time interval
dt
with
\hat{U}_{\mathrm{int}}=e^{-i\hat{V}_{\mathrm{int}}dt}
.
Then, the measurement operator [cf. Eq. (
15
)] corresponding to photon detection (
b=1
) reads

\displaystyle\hat{M}_{1}=_{\mathrm{ph}}\braket{1|\hat{U}_{\mathrm{int}}|0}_{\mathrm{ph}}\simeq{-i}g\hat{\sigma}^{-}dt={-i}\sqrt{\gamma dt}\hat{\sigma}^{-},

(81)

where
\gamma=g^{2}dt
.
Comparing Eq. (
81
) with Eq. (
62
), we find

\displaystyle\hat{L}=\sqrt{\gamma}\hat{\sigma}^{-},

(82)

where we have omitted the subscript
b
and dropped the phase factor
-i
since it does not change Eq. (
73
).
The probability of photon detection is given by

\displaystyle p=\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\rho}_{c}\hat{L}^{\dagger}\hat{L}]dt=\gamma\braket{\hat{\sigma}^{+}\hat{\sigma}^{-}}_{c}dt.

(83)

We assume that the photon is immediately reset to
\ket{0}_{\mathrm{ph}}
upon detection, since it is absorbed into the photo-detector.
Then, this continuously monitored process is understood as follows [see Fig.
3
(a)]:
a photon is detected with a rate
\gamma\braket{\hat{\sigma}^{+}\hat{\sigma}^{-}}_{c}
, causing the state of the atom to abruptly jump to its ground state.
If no photon is detected, the state is evolved by the non-Hermitian Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{\mathrm{eff}}=\hat{H}_{\mathrm{S}}-\frac{i\gamma}{2}\hat{\sigma}^{+}\hat{\sigma}^{-}
.
The average over all quantum trajectories leads to the GKSL equation

\displaystyle\frac{d\hat{\rho}}{dt}=-i[\hat{H}_{\mathrm{S}},\hat{\rho}]+\gamma\hat{\sigma}^{-}\hat{\rho}\hat{\sigma}^{+}-\frac{\gamma}{2}\{\hat{\rho},\hat{\sigma}^{+}\hat{\sigma}^{-}\}.

(84)

Figure 3:

An example of a quantum trajectory for a single atom system with spontaneous emissions.
(a) If we consider the occupation of the excited state, given as
\frac{\hat{\sigma}^{z}+1}{2}
, it is described by the continuous time evolution and quantum jumps, which reset the state into the ground state.
(b) The total number of jumps
N(t)
until time
t
. It increases by one at times
\tau_{1}
,
\tau_{2}
, and
\tau_{3}
.

There are many other types of models that are expected to phenomenologically describe open quantum systems.
For example, if we consider a two-level atom
\hat{H}_{\mathrm{S}}=\frac{\omega}{2}\hat{\sigma}^{z}
in a finite-temperature bath of photons with an inverse temperature
\beta
, it will exhibit stimulated emission and absorption of photons.
In this case, the averaged dynamics is often modeled
[
7
]
by the GKSL equation with jump operators
\hat{L}_{+}=\sqrt{\gamma_{+}}\hat{\sigma}^{+}
and

\hat{L}_{-}=\sqrt{\gamma_{-}}\hat{\sigma}^{-}
, where the detailed balance condition
\gamma_{+}/\gamma_{-}=e^{-\beta\omega}
is satisfied.
Under such jump operators, the Gibbs state at temperature
\beta^{-1}
becomes the stationary state of the GKSL equation.

As another example, we can think of many-body systems under continuous measurement.
In particular, let us consider the Bose-Hubbard model realized in a cold atomic system in an optical lattice
[
66
]
, whose Hamiltonian is given by

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{\mathrm{BH}}=-\sum_{l,l^{\prime}}J_{ll^{\prime}}(\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{l}\hat{a}_{l^{\prime}}+\mathrm{h.c.})+\frac{U}{2}\sum_{l}\hat{n}_{l}(\hat{n}_{l}-1),

(85)

where
l
and
l^{\prime}
denote the lattice sites,
\hat{a}_{l}
is the annihilation operator of a boson at site
l
, and
\hat{n}_{l}=\hat{a}_{l}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{l}
is the number operator at site
l
.
By adding a suitable laser field, atoms will absorb and spontaneously emit photons, causing the incoherent scattering of photons
[
18
]
.
We can determine the positions of atoms by detecting the scattered photons.
If the wavelength of the incident photons is shorter than the lattice spacing, the measurement will be performed with single-site resolution.
The corresponding jump operators are modeled as
\hat{L}_{l}=\sqrt{\gamma}\hat{n}_{l}
, i.e., the number operator for each site.
The microscopic deviation of this process was discussed in Refs.
[
67
,
18
]
.
Note that such photon scattering from cold atoms has in fact been experimentally realized
[
68
,
69
,
70
]
, while continuous measurement with single-site resolution has yet to be achieved.

3.3
Physical quantities characterizing quantum trajectories

Let us next discuss how to characterize quantum trajectories through physically relevant quantities.
Here we focus on a pure state in the continuous-time case, i.e.,
\ket{\psi_{c}(t)}
in Eq. (
75
), although a similar discussion applies to the discrete-time case in Eq. (
57
).

3.3.1
Nonlinear observables and postselection cost

One may naively consider the expectation value of an observable
\hat{A}
with respect to
\ket{\psi_{c}(t)}
,

\displaystyle\braket{\hat{A}}_{c;t}=\braket{\psi_{c}(t)|\hat{A}|\psi_{c}(t)},

(86)

to characterize quantum trajectories.
However, its ensemble average (average over the quantum trajectories) can also be obtained from the GKSL equation as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\braket{\hat{A}}_{c;t}]=\braket{\hat{A}}_{t}

(87)

with

\displaystyle\braket{\hat{A}}_{t}=\mathrm{Tr}[{\hat{\rho}(t)\hat{A}}],

(88)

where
\hat{\rho}(t)
is given in Eq. (
79
) and we have used
\mathbb{E}[\ket{\psi_{c}(t)}\bra{\psi_{c}(t)}]=\hat{\rho}(t)
.
Therefore, to highlight physics unique to quantum trajectories, we should consider nonlinear quantities in
\hat{\rho}_{c}(t)
, such as
\braket{\hat{A}}_{c;t}^{2}
, whose ensemble average
\mathbb{E}[\braket{\hat{A}}_{c;t}^{2}]
cannot be accessed via the GKSL dynamics.

Another interesting nonlinear quantity, which plays an especially important role in many-body systems, is the entanglement entropy of the state

\displaystyle S_{c,X}(t)=-\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\rho}_{c,X}(t)\ln\hat{\rho}_{c,X}(t)],

(89)

where

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{c,X}(t)=\mathrm{Tr}_{\bar{X}}[\ket{\psi_{c}(t)}\bra{\psi_{c}(t)}]

(90)

is the reduced density matrix of a subsystem
X
.
Here, we have divided the total system into two regions,
X
and its complement
\bar{X}
.
In fact, we find

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[S_{c,X}(t)]\neq-\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\rho}_{X}(t)\ln\hat{\rho}_{X}(t)],

(91)

where
\hat{\rho}_{X}(t)=\mathbb{E}[\hat{\rho}_{c,X}(t)]=\mathrm{Tr}_{\bar{X}}[\hat{\rho}(t)]
.
One of the interesting phenomena concerning this quantity is the measurement-induced entanglement phase transition
[
71
,
72
,
73
]
, which will be detailed in Chapter
7
.
There, we will see that the long-time behavior of
\mathbb{E}[S_{c,X}(t)]
exhibits an intriguing phase transition as the measurement strength is varied, even when
\hat{\rho}(t)
becomes a trivial maximally mixed state at long times regardless of the measurement strength.

As an important remark, the evaluation of these nonlinear quantities requires the postselection of quantum trajectories, whose experimental cost is exponentially large.
For example, precisely estimating
\braket{\hat{A}}_{c;t}
for a single quantum trajectory needs a large number of measurements of an observable
\hat{A}
with respect to
\ket{\psi_{c}(t)}
since we need to suppress intrinsic quantum fluctuations.
However, for this purpose, we must prepare
\ket{\psi_{c}(t)}
multiple times.
Since we cannot clone quantum states
[
74
]
, we need to repeat the time evolution from the initial state
\ket{\psi(0)}
and postselect the states where the jumps occurred according to
\{(\tau_{k},b_{k})\}_{k=1}^{K}
.
As one would expect, the probability of obtaining a target trajectory is exponentially small with respect to time.
If we consider many-body systems, it is also exponentially small with respect to the system size.

To quantify the cost of postselection in a simple setup, let us consider the following model of hardcore bosons on a one-dimensional lattice,

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{\mathrm{HCB}}=-\sum_{l=1}^{V}J(\hat{b}^{\dagger}_{l}\hat{b}_{l+1}+\mathrm{h.c.})+\frac{U}{2}\sum_{l=1}^{V}\hat{n}_{l}\hat{n}_{l+1},

(92)

subject to position measurements
\hat{L}_{l}=\sqrt{\gamma}\hat{n}_{l}
at each site.
Here,
V
is the system size,
\hat{b}_{l}
is the annihilation operator of a hardcore boson, and
\hat{n}_{l}=\hat{b}_{l}^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{l}
.
The number of bosons,
M=\left<\sum_{l}\hat{n}_{l}\right>_{c;t}
, is conserved under the dynamics, which is assumed to scale as
M\propto V
.

In this case, the rate of the jump occurring at one of the sites is given by

\displaystyle\sum_{l}\frac{p_{l}}{dt}=\sum_{l}\braket{\hat{L}_{l}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{l}}_{c;t}=\left\langle\gamma\sum_{l}\hat{n}_{l}\right\rangle_{c;t}=\gamma M,

(93)

which is constant (note that
\hat{n}_{l}^{2}=\hat{n}_{l}
).
In other words, the probability of finding no jumps during the time interval
[0,t]
is given by
e^{-\gamma Mt}
.

Now, we roughly estimate the probability of realizing a quantum trajectory characterized by
\{(\tau_{k},b_{k})\}_{k=1}^{K}
, where we allow some uncertainty
\Delta\tau
for the jump times
\tau_{k}

[
75
]
.
We require that
\Delta\tau
is much smaller than the typical interval of jumps
\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k}\sim(\gamma M)^{-1}
, i.e.,
\epsilon=\gamma M\Delta\tau\ll 1
.
Given an initial state, the probability that there is no jump during
t\in[0,\tau_{1}-\Delta\tau)
and a jump occurs during
t\in[\tau_{1}-\Delta\tau,\tau_{1}+\Delta\tau]
becomes

\displaystyle P_{\tau_{1}}=e^{-\gamma M(\tau_{1}-\Delta\tau)}-e^{-\gamma M(\tau_{1}+\Delta\tau)}\simeq(2\epsilon)e^{-\gamma M\tau_{1}}.

(94)

We can repeat this process for time intervals
\tau_{2}-\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{K}-\tau_{K-1}
, which results in the probability

\displaystyle P_{\tau_{1}}P_{\tau_{2}-\tau_{1}}\cdots P_{\tau_{K}-\tau_{K-1}}=(2\epsilon)^{K}e^{-\gamma M\tau_{K}}.

(95)

Finally, multiplying the probability that there is no jump during
(\tau_{K},t]
, we find

\displaystyle P_{t}(\{\tau_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K})=(2\epsilon)^{K}e^{-\gamma Mt}

(96)

Moreover, for each jump, we have
V
different types of jumps. As a rough estimate, we assume that they occur with equal probabilities.
Then, we find that the probability of realizing a quantum trajectory characterized by
\{(\tau_{k},b_{k})\}_{k=1}^{K}
is

\displaystyle P_{t}(\{(\tau_{k},b_{k})\}_{k=1}^{K})=\left(\frac{1}{V}\right)^{K}(2\epsilon)^{K}e^{-\gamma Mt}\sim\left(\frac{Ve}{2\epsilon}\right)^{-\gamma Mt},

(97)

where we have used that
K\sim\gamma Mt
for a typical trajectory.
Therefore, the cost for the sampling increases as

\displaystyle\left(P_{t}(\{(\tau_{k},b_{k})\}_{k=1}^{K})\right)^{-1}\sim\left(\frac{Ve}{2\epsilon}\right)^{\gamma Mt}.

(98)

This becomes exponentially large with time, whose rate becomes proportional to
\sim V\ln(V/\epsilon)
.

Note that, for the present setup, we can avoid the above postselection and effectively simulate nonlinear quantities by performing suitable experiments in unitary quantum systems
[
75
]
.
This method reduces the cost to
\sim e^{\gamma Mt}
, which is better than Eq. (
98
) by the factor
(V/\epsilon)^{\gamma Mt}
.
While this cost is still exponentially large, we can circumvent the factor concerning the jump-time accuracy
\epsilon
.
We also note that the postselection cost is approximately estimated as
e^{\mathcal{O}(Vt)}
for discrete-time quantum circuits described by Eq. (
57
), although there are several attempts to avoid the postselection
[
76
,
16
,
77
,
78
,
79
,
80
,
81
,
82
,
83
,
84
,
85
]
.

3.3.2
Statistics of quantum jumps

Another important quantity, which is unique to stochastic dynamics, is obtained from the counting variable
N_{b}(t)
of quantum jumps.
Since
N_{b}(t)
is just given by the total number of jumps of type
b\>(\geq 1)
within
[0,t]
, it is directly observed from a single quantum trajectory without postselection.
To be specific, we can consider
[
35
]

\displaystyle N_{\mu}(t)=\sum_{b}\mu_{b}N_{b}(t)

(99)

and its derivative, which is often called the current:

\displaystyle I_{\mu}(t)=\frac{dN_{\mu}(t)}{dt}=\sum_{b}\mu_{b}\frac{dN_{b}(t)}{dt}

(100)

where
\mu=\{\mu_{b}\}_{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{B}
.

If we take the ensemble average of
I_{\mu}(t)
, Eq. (
3.2.3
) leads to

\displaystyle J_{\mu}(t)=\mathbb{E}[I_{\mu}(t)]=\sum_{b}\mu_{b}\frac{\mathbb{E}[dN_{b}(t)]}{dt}=\sum_{b}\mu_{b}\braket{\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{b}}_{t},

(101)

where
\braket{\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{b}}_{t}
is the expectation value of
\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{b}
with respect to
\hat{\rho}(t)
obtained from the GKSL equation in Eq. (
88
).

A more non-trivial question is how the current is correlated in time, which is characterized by

\displaystyle C_{\mu}(t,t+\tau)=\mathbb{E}[I_{\mu}(t)I_{\mu}(t+\tau)]-\mathbb{E}[I_{\mu}(t)]\mathbb{E}[I_{\mu}(t+\tau)].

(102)

We can assume
\tau\geq 0
since
\mathbb{E}[I_{\mu}(t)I_{\mu}(t+\tau)]=\mathbb{E}[I_{\mu}(t+\tau)I_{\mu}(t)]
.
For
\tau>0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[I_{\mu}(t)I_{\mu}(t+\tau)]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{dt^{2}}\mathbb{E}[dN_{\mu}(t)dN_{\mu}(t+\tau)]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{dt^{2}}\sum_{b,b^{\prime}}\mu_{b}\mu_{b^{\prime}}\>\mathrm{Prob}[dN_{b}(t+\tau)=1,dN_{b^{\prime}}(t)=1]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{dt^{2}}\sum_{b,b^{\prime}}\mu_{b}\mu_{b^{\prime}}\>\mathbb{E}_{[0,t)}[\mathrm{Prob}_{[t+\tau,t]}[dN_{b}(t+\tau)=1|dN_{b^{\prime}}(t)=1,\hat{\rho}_{c}(t)]\cdot\mathrm{Prob}_{t}[dN_{b^{\prime}}(t)=1|\hat{\rho}_{c}(t)]],

(103)

where
\mathrm{Prob}_{[t+\tau,t]}[dN_{b}(t+\tau)=1|dN_{b^{\prime}}(t)=1,\hat{\rho}_{c}(t)]
is the conditional probability such that the type-
b
jump occurs at time
t+\tau
when the type-
b^{\prime}
jump occurs at time
t
for
\hat{\rho}_{c}(t)
, and

\displaystyle\mathrm{Prob}_{t}[dN_{b^{\prime}}(t)=1|\hat{\rho}_{c}(t)]=p_{b^{\prime}}^{c}(t)=\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{L}_{b^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{b^{\prime}}\hat{\rho}_{c}(t)]dt.

(104)

Now,
\mathrm{Prob}_{[t+\tau,t]}[dN_{b}(t+\tau)=1|dN_{b^{\prime}}(t)=1,\hat{\rho}_{c}(t)]
is obtained as follows.
If we have a state
\hat{\rho}_{c}(t)
at time
t-0
before the jump, the occurrence of the jump
dN_{b^{\prime}}(t)=1
means that the post-jump state is

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{c}^{\prime}(t)=\frac{\hat{L}_{b^{\prime}}\hat{\rho}_{c}(t)\hat{L}_{b^{\prime}}^{\dagger}}{\braket{\hat{L}_{b^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{b^{\prime}}}_{c;t}}.

(105)

Then, we do not care about the jumps during
(t,t+\tau)
, and so on average, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{c}^{\prime}(t+\tau)=e^{\mathcal{L}\tau}[\hat{\rho}_{c}^{\prime}(t)]

(106)

at time
t+\tau
before the jump.
Finally, we consider the probability of the jump,
\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{b}\hat{\rho}_{c}^{\prime}(t+\tau)]dt
. Combining the above steps, we find

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[I_{\mu}(t)I_{\mu}(t+\tau)]

\displaystyle=\sum_{bb^{\prime}}\mu_{b}\mu_{b^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}_{[0,t)}\left[\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{b}e^{\mathcal{L}\tau}[\hat{L}_{b^{\prime}}\hat{\rho}_{c}(t)\hat{L}_{b^{\prime}}^{\dagger}]]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}[\mathcal{G}_{\mu}\circ e^{\mathcal{L}\tau}\circ\mathcal{G}_{\mu}[\hat{\rho}(t)]],

(107)

where we have introduced the super-operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\mu}[\hat{X}]=\sum_{b}\mu_{b}\hat{L}_{b}\hat{X}\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}.

(108)

Note that
J_{\mu}(t)=\mathrm{Tr}[\mathcal{G}_{\mu}[\hat{\rho}(t)]]
.

Finally, for
\tau=0
, we find

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[I_{\mu}(t)^{2}]=\frac{1}{dt^{2}}\sum_{b}\mu_{b}^{2}\mathbb{E}[dN_{b}(t)]=\frac{1}{dt}\sum_{b}\mu_{b}^{2}\braket{\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{b}}_{t},

(109)

which can be interpreted as
\delta(\tau)\sum_{b}\mu_{b}^{2}\braket{\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{b}}_{t}
.

In conclusion, the correlation function is given by

\displaystyle C_{\mu}(t,t+\tau)=\mathrm{Tr}[\mathcal{G}_{\mu}\circ e^{\mathcal{L}\tau}\circ\mathcal{G}_{\mu}[\hat{\rho}(t)]]+\delta(\tau)\sum_{b}\mu_{b}^{2}\braket{\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{b}}_{t}-J_{\mu}(t)J_{\mu}(t+\tau).

(110)

In particular, if we choose the state
\hat{\rho}
as a stationary state
\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}
, which satisfies

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}[\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}]=0,

(111)

the correlation function reads

\displaystyle C_{\mu}^{\mathrm{ss}}(\tau)=\mathrm{Tr}[\mathcal{G}_{\mu}\circ e^{\mathcal{L}|\tau|}\circ\mathcal{G}_{\mu}[\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}]]+\delta(\tau)\sum_{b}\mu_{b}^{2}\braket{\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{b}}_{\mathrm{ss}}-\mathrm{Tr}[\mathcal{G}_{\mu}[\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}]]^{2},

(112)

where we also allow
\tau<0
and
\braket{\hat{A}}_{\mathrm{ss}}=\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{A}\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}]
.

From the above results, we can obtain the statistics of the counting variable
N_{\mu}(t)
, too.
For simplicity, let us assume that
\hat{\rho}(t)
is a stationary state, although the extension to arbitrary initial states is not difficult. Then, the average of
N_{\mu}(t)
is given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[N_{\mu}(t)]=\int_{0}^{t}d\tau\sum_{b}\mu_{b}\braket{\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{b}}_{\mathrm{ss}}=t\sum_{b}\mu_{b}\braket{\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{b}}_{\mathrm{ss}}.

(113)

Next, the variance of
N_{\mu}(t)
becomes

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}[N_{\mu}(t)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[N_{\mu}(t)^{2}]-\mathbb{E}[N_{\mu}(t)]^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}d\tau\int_{0}^{t}d\tau^{\prime}C_{\mu}(\tau,\tau^{\prime}).

(114)

Taking the time derivative, we have

\displaystyle D(t)=\frac{d\mathbb{V}[N_{\mu}(t)]}{dt}=2\int_{0}^{t}d\tau C_{\mu}(t,\tau)=2\int_{0}^{t}d\tau C_{\mu}(\tau,t)=2\int_{0}^{t}d\tau C_{\mu}^{\mathrm{ss}}(t-\tau)=2\int_{0}^{t}d\tau C_{\mu}^{\mathrm{ss}}(\tau)

(115)

and thus

\displaystyle D(t)=\sum_{b}\mu_{b}^{2}\braket{\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{b}}_{\mathrm{ss}}+2\int_{0}^{t}d\tau(\mathrm{Tr}[\mathcal{G}_{\mu}\circ e^{\mathcal{L}|\tau|}\circ\mathcal{G}_{\mu}[\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}]]-\mathrm{Tr}[\mathcal{G}_{\mu}[\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}]]^{2}).

(116)

If
t
is large, we often encounter that
D(t)
rapidly converges to a constant
D(\infty)
. In that case, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}[N_{\mu}(t)]\sim D(\infty)t.

(117)

We can see that both
\mathbb{E}[N_{\mu}(t)]
and
\mathbb{V}[N_{\mu}(t)]
increases linearly with
t
.
Notably, however, the variance
\mathbb{V}[N_{\mu}(t)]
depends on how the jumps occuring at different times are correlated, as highlighted in the second term in the right-hand side of Eq. (
116
).
Note that, in general, higher-order fluctuations of
N_{\mu}(t)
can be captured by, e.g., its large deviation
[
86
,
87
]
, which we do not explain here.
See Refs.
[
88
,
89
,
35
,
90
]
for a review.

3.3.3
Examples of the statistics of quantum jumps

As an example, let us consider the spontaneous emission from a single-atom system, whose jump operator is given by Eq. (
82
) (see Fig.
3
).
We assume that the atom’s Hamiltonian is simply given by

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{S}=\frac{g}{2}\hat{\sigma}^{x}.

(118)

Furthermore, we assume that the state is prepared in a stationary state
\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}
.
Solving
\mathcal{L}[\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}]
, we find that the stationary state is given by

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}=\frac{1}{s^{2}+2}\begin{pmatrix}1&-is\\
is&1+s^{2}\end{pmatrix},

(119)

where
s=\gamma/g
.

We consider
\mu=1
and
{I}(t)=\frac{dN(t)}{dt}
. Then, its average reads

\displaystyle J^{\mathrm{ss}}=\mathrm{Tr}[\mathcal{G}_{\mu}[\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}]]=\gamma\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\sigma}^{+}\hat{\sigma}^{-}\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}]=\frac{\gamma g^{2}}{\gamma^{2}+2g^{2}}.

(120)

Next, to gain a physical insight for the correlation
C^{\mathrm{ss}}(\tau)
, we assume a small
\tau\>(>0)
regime.
Then, we find

\displaystyle C^{\mathrm{ss}}(\tau)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Tr}[\mathcal{G}_{\mu}^{2}[\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}]]+\tau\mathrm{Tr}[\mathcal{G}_{\mu}\mathcal{L}\mathcal{G}_{\mu}[\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}]]+\frac{1}{2}\tau^{2}\mathrm{Tr}[\mathcal{G}_{\mu}\mathcal{L}^{2}\mathcal{G}_{\mu}[\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}]]-(J^{\mathrm{ss}})^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{g^{4}\gamma^{2}}{(\gamma^{2}+2g^{2})^{2}}{\left\{\frac{(\gamma^{2}+2g^{2})\tau^{2}}{4}-1\right\}},

(121)

where we omit
\circ
between the maps for simplicity.
Importantly, this result shows that
C^{\mathrm{ss}}(\tau)<0
for small
\tau
, indicating the anti-correlation of the two jumps, i.e., spontaneous emissions.
In fact, in the present setup, the detection of the spontaneous emission means that the atom is suddenly changed to the ground state
\ket{0}
.
Since it takes some time for the atom’s state to have an overlap with the excited state
\ket{1}
, the second spontaneous emission is suppressed before that, which explains the anti-correlation of the jumps.

As another example, let us consider a quantum many-body system of hardcore bosons on a one-dimensional lattice, whose Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{\mathrm{HCB}}
is given in Eq. (
92
),
with the position measurement
\hat{L}_{l}=\sqrt{\gamma}\hat{n}_{l}
at each site.
Here, we assume that the system size
V
is even and set the conserved number of particles
M
as
M=V/2
(i.e., half-filling).

Under this setup, let us first consider the total number of jumps in the whole system, which is given by

\displaystyle N_{\mathrm{total}}(t)=\sum_{l=1}^{V}N_{l}(t)

(122)

with
\mu_{l}=1
for all
l
.
If we consider the maximally mixed state
\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}\propto\mathbb{\hat{I}}
in the sector whose particle number is
M
, using
\hat{n}_{l}^{2}=\hat{n}_{l}
for the hardcore bosons, we immediately find

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[N_{\mathrm{total}}(t)]=t\gamma\sum_{l=1}^{V}\braket{\hat{n}_{l}}_{\mathrm{ss}}=\frac{\gamma Vt}{2}

(123)

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}[N_{\mathrm{total}}(t)]=\frac{\gamma Vt}{2},

(124)

where we can confirm that the second term in Eq. (
116
) vanishes
13
13

13

To see this explicitly, we use the fact that
\sum_{l=1}^{V}\hat{n}_{l}=M
, which is regarded as a c-number. Then,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}[\mathcal{G}_{\mu}\circ e^{\mathcal{L}|\tau|}\circ\mathcal{G}_{\mu}[\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}]]

\displaystyle=\gamma\mathrm{Tr}\left[\sum_{l=1}^{V}\hat{n}_{l}^{2}\left(e^{\mathcal{L}|\tau|}\circ\mathcal{G}_{\mu}[\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}]\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\gamma M\mathrm{Tr}\left[{e^{\mathcal{L}|\tau|}\circ\mathcal{G}_{\mu}[\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}]}\right]=\gamma M\mathrm{Tr}\left[{\mathcal{G}_{\mu}[\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}]}\right]=\gamma^{2}M^{2}\mathrm{Tr}\left[{\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}}\right]=\gamma^{2}M^{2},

(125)

and, similarly, we have
\mathrm{Tr}[\mathcal{G}_{\mu}[\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}]]^{2}=\gamma^{2}M^{2}
.

.
This reflects the fact that the statistics of quantum jumps occurring in the total system is Poissonian (
\mathbb{V}[N_{\mathrm{total}}(t)]/\mathbb{E}[N_{\mathrm{total}}(t)]=1
).

However, a more nontrivial feature appears if we instead consider the quantum jumps occurring in the subsystem, say

\displaystyle N_{\mathrm{half}}(t)=\sum_{l=1}^{V/2}N_{l}(t),

(126)

with
\mu_{l}=1\>(1\leq l\leq V/2)
and
\mu_{l}=0\>(V/2+1\leq l\leq V)
.
In fact, Ref.
[
83
]
numerically found that the variance of
N_{\mathrm{half}}(t)
exhibits an anomalous scaling

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}[N_{\mathrm{half}}(t)]=\frac{\gamma Vt}{4}+\gamma^{2}c(\gamma)V^{\alpha}t,

(127)

while
\mathbb{E}[N_{\mathrm{half}}(t)]=\gamma Vt/4
as known from the conservation of the particle number.
Here,
c(\gamma)
is an increasing function of
\gamma
and
\alpha\simeq 2.7
is a universal exponent independent of
U
.
This means that for sufficiently small
\gamma
, which should be
o(V^{0})
, the variance behaves
\mathbb{V}[N_{\mathrm{half}}(t)]\simeq\gamma Vt/4
corresponding to the Poissonian statistics
\mathbb{V}[N_{\mathrm{half}}(t)]/\mathbb{E}[N_{\mathrm{half}}(t)]=1
.
In stark contrast, for
\gamma=\mathcal{O}(V^{0})
, the variance behaves as a super-Poissonian statistics
\mathbb{V}[N_{\mathrm{half}}(t)]\simeq\gamma^{2}c(\gamma)V^{\alpha}t\gg\mathbb{E}[N_{\mathrm{half}}(t)]
.
As seen from this example, statistics of jumps for many-body systems
[
91
,
92
,
93
,
94
,
95
,
96
,
97
]
can be nontrivial.

3.4
Advantage in numerical simulations of open systems

3.4.1
Overview

As we have seen, quantum trajectories have physical meanings in the context of quantum systems under measurement.
Here, we discuss that quantum trajectories are also useful for numerical simulations of open quantum systems, which is in fact one of the motivations for the early works of this concept
[
23
,
24
]
.
Our aim is to simulate the GKSL equation

\displaystyle\frac{d\hat{\rho}}{dt}=-i[\hat{H},\hat{\rho}]+\sum_{b\geq 1}\hat{L}_{b}\hat{\rho}\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\{\hat{\rho},\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{b}\}

(128)

in an efficient way, where we will omit the subscript
\mathrm{S}
for simplicity in the following.

If we directly try to compute the GKSL dynamics without approximation, we need to store
\sim d^{2}
elements of
\hat{\rho}
in the memory of a computer, where
d=\mathrm{dim}[\mathcal{H}]
is the dimension of the Hilbert space.
Moreover, the computational time for the right-hand side is
\sim d^{4}
.
If we consider many-body systems, where
d
grows exponentially with respect to the system size
V
, these scalings are problematic.

In contrast, we have seen that a GKSL equation is obtained from the ensemble average of stochastic quantum trajectories.
Therefore, a GKSL equation can be simulated by sampling many quantum trajectories that reduce to the GKSL equation on average.
This is called the unraveling of the GKSL equation.

Specifically, given Eq. (
128
), we can instead simulate the dynamics of pure states,

\displaystyle d\ket{\psi_{c}}=\left(-i\hat{H}_{\mathrm{eff}}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{b\geq 1}\braket{\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{b}}_{c}\right)\ket{\psi_{c}}dt+\sum_{b\geq 1}\left(\frac{\hat{L}_{b}}{\braket{\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{b}}_{c}}-1\right)\ket{\psi_{c}}dN_{b}

(129)

and take the ensemble average,
\hat{\rho}=\mathbb{E}[\ket{\psi_{c}}\bra{\psi_{c}}]
.
For this case, we need to store only
\sim d
elements of
\ket{\psi_{c}}
to the memory.
The computational time is
\sim d^{2}
for one trajectory, and the net cost is
\sim d^{2}N_{\mathrm{sample}}
, where
N_{\mathrm{sample}}
is the number of samples to obtain
\hat{\rho}
with a sufficient accuracy.
Therefore, compared with the direct simulation, the quantum-trajectory method has a memory advantage and, if
N_{\mathrm{sample}}\ll d^{2}
(which is often the case), an advantage of computational time.

3.4.2
Concrete method

Here, we demonstrate a concrete method to perform the simulation outlined above (see also
[
7
,
35
]
).
As a preliminary, we notice the following method to efficiently sample a random variable
X
, which takes
X_{b}\>(0\leq b\leq B)
with probability
p_{b}
.

1.

Define

\displaystyle Q_{b}=\sum_{b^{\prime}=0}^{b}p_{b^{\prime}},\quad Q_{-1}=0,

(130)

where
Q_{B}=1
from normalization.

2.

Sample
R
randomly from the box distribution in the range
R\in[0,1]
.

3.

Find
b
such that

\displaystyle Q_{b-1}<R\leq Q_{b}.

(131)

4.

Output
X_{b}
.

Why this method works is visually understood from Fig.
4
.

Figure 4:

Sampling of a random variable
X
, which takes
X_{b}\;(0\leq b\leq B)
, with probability
p_{b}
.
Considering the cumulative distribution
Q_{b}
and choosing
R
