is the only eigenvector of
\mathcal{L}
with eigenvalue
0
.
Since
\hat{\varrho}_{0}\succ 0
,
\mathcal{E}_{t}
is irreducible according to (2) of Proposition
8
.
∎

Before closing this section, we provide several examples of irreducible Markovian CPTP maps in two-level systems.
The first example is defined by a GKSL superoperator
\mathcal{L}
in the form of Eq. (
182
) with
[
108
]

\displaystyle\hat{H}=0,\quad\hat{L}_{1}=|0\rangle\langle 1|,\quad\hat{L}_{2}=|1\rangle\langle 0|.

(193)

Since
\{\hat{L}_{1},\hat{L}_{2}\}
is a self-adjoint set and
\{\hat{L}_{1},\hat{L}_{2}\}^{\prime}=\mathbb{C}\hat{\mathbb{I}}
, the Markovian CPTP map
e^{\mathcal{L}t}
is irreducible according to Spohn’s theorem (Theorem
11
).
Indeed, the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{L}
are
\lambda=0
,
-1
,
-1
,
-2
and the eigenvector corresponding to
\lambda=0
is
\hat{X}=|0\rangle\langle 0|+|1\rangle\langle 1|
, which is positive definite.

The second example is taken from Ref.
[
106
]
:

\displaystyle\hat{H}=0,\quad\hat{L}_{1}=|0\rangle\langle 0|+|0\rangle\langle 1|+|1\rangle\langle 1|.

(194)

Here,
\{\hat{L}_{1}\}
is not self-adjoint, and thus Spohn’s theorem does not apply.
On the other hand, we have
2\hat{K}=\hat{L}_{1}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{1}=|0\rangle\langle 0|+|0\rangle\langle 1|+|1\rangle\langle 0|+2|1\rangle\langle 1|
and the algebra generated by
\{\hat{L}_{1},\hat{K}\}
coincides with
\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
.
This is evident from the relations
|0\rangle\langle 1|=\hat{L}_{1}^{2}-\hat{L}_{1}
and
|1\rangle\langle 0|=2\hat{L}_{1}\hat{K}-\hat{L}_{1}-\hat{L}_{1}^{2}
, which allow us to generate all basis oparators.
Thus, the corresponding Markovian CPTP map
e^{\mathcal{L}t}
is irreducible according to Theorem
9
.
We find that the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{L}
are
\lambda=0
,
-1/2
,
(-3\pm i\sqrt{15})/4
and the eigenvector corresponding to
\lambda=0
is
\hat{X}=2|0\rangle\langle 0|-|0\rangle\langle 1|-|1\rangle\langle 0|+|1\rangle\langle 1|
, which is positive definite.

The third example is also from Ref.
[
106
]
:

\displaystyle\hat{H}=(|0\rangle\langle 1|+|1\rangle\langle 0|)/2,\quad\hat{L}_{1}=|0\rangle\langle 1|.

(195)

As before,
\{\hat{L}_{1}\}
is not self-adjoint, so Spohn’s theorem does not apply.
On the other hand, we have
2\hat{K}=2i\hat{H}+\hat{L}_{1}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{1}=i|0\rangle\langle 1|+i|1\rangle\langle 0|+|1\rangle\langle 1|
and the algebra generated by
\{\hat{L}_{1},\hat{K}\}
coincides with
\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
.
This can be verified by observing that
|0\rangle\langle 1|=\hat{L}_{1}
and
|1\rangle\langle 0|=-4i\hat{K}^{2}-2\hat{L}_{1}\hat{K}
.
The spectrum of
\mathcal{L}
is the same as in the second example and the eigenvector corresponding to
\lambda=0
is
\hat{X}=2|0\rangle\langle 0|+i|0\rangle\langle 1|-i|1\rangle\langle 0|+|1\rangle\langle 1|
, which is positive definite.

Various other examples of irreducible and reducible Markovian CPTP maps, including many-body systems, have been discussed in Refs.
[
108
,
106
]
.
In particular, Ref.
[
106
]
pointed out that some previous literature incorrectly used Frigerio’s theorem (Theorem
12
) to predict the presence of a unique steady state, by relying solely on the condition
\{\hat{L}_{b},\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger},\hat{H}\}^{\prime}=\mathbb{C}\hat{\mathbb{I}}
while overlooking the assumption of the existence of a positive definite stationary state.
As discussed in the proof of Theorem
12
, if
\{\hat{L}_{b},\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger},\hat{H}\}^{\prime}
is nontrivial (i.e.,
\mathbb{C}\hat{\mathbb{I}}\subset\{\hat{L}_{b},\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger},\hat{H}\}^{\prime}
), then
\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}
must have degenerate zero eigenvalues, implying the existence of multiple steady states.
However, the converse is not true; the condition
\{\hat{L}_{b},\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger},\hat{H}\}^{\prime}=\mathbb{C}\hat{\mathbb{I}}
alone does not imply the uniqueness of a steady state.

4.5
Miscellaneous topics on the spectra beyond the steady state

In the previous sections, we have detailed the conditions for which the CPTP maps and quantum master equations (i.e., the GKSL equations) have unique stationary states.
In this section, we discuss other spectral properties, such as the spectral gap and statistics, which are less understood than the uniqueness property of a stationary state both physically and mathematically.
While the content in this section is basically not used from next chapters, we here try to
introduce a brief (biased) overview of some relevant topics such that readers can grasp some recent developments of the field.

We first introduce a general structure of eigenvalues and eigenvectors for the CPTP map and the GKSL generator.
For simplicity, we assume that the full diagonalization is possible, unless stated otherwise.
As defined in Sec.
4.1
,
a
th right and left eigenvectors of the CPTP map
\mathcal{E}
satisfy

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}[\hat{\mathsf{R}}_{a}]

\displaystyle=z_{a}\hat{\mathsf{R}}_{a},

(196)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}[\hat{\mathsf{L}}_{a}]

\displaystyle=z_{a}^{*}\hat{\mathsf{L}}_{a}.

(197)

Here,
\hat{\mathsf{R}}_{a}
and
\hat{\mathsf{L}}_{a}
are right and left eigenmodes, respectively, which can be taken to satisfy the bi-orthogonal condition as

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\mathsf{L}}_{a}^{\dagger}\hat{\mathsf{R}}_{b}]\propto\delta_{ab}.

(198)

The eigenvalues
z_{a}\;(a=1,\cdots,d^{2})
with

d=\mathrm{dim}[\mathcal{H}]
are arranged as

\displaystyle 1=z_{1}\geq|z_{2}|\geq\cdots\geq|z_{d^{2}}|.

(199)

Similarly, we can consider the eigenvalues of the GKSL superoperator as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}[\hat{r}_{a}]

\displaystyle=\lambda_{a}\hat{r}_{a},

(200)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}[\hat{l}_{a}]

\displaystyle=\lambda_{a}^{*}\hat{l}_{a},

(201)

where
\hat{{r}}_{a}
and
\hat{{l}}_{a}
are right and left eigenmodes, respectively, satisfying

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{l}_{a}^{\dagger}\hat{r}_{b}]\propto\delta_{ab}.

(202)

The eigenvalues
\lambda_{a}\;(a=1,\cdots,d^{2})
are arranged as

\displaystyle 0=\lambda_{1}\geq\mathrm{Re}[\lambda_{2}]\geq\cdots\geq\mathrm{Re}[\lambda_{d^{2}}].

(203)

For the CPTP dynamics generated by the time-independent GKSL equation,
\mathcal{E}=e^{\mathcal{L}t}
, we have
\hat{\mathsf{R}}_{a}=\hat{r}_{a}
,
\hat{\mathsf{L}}_{a}=\hat{l}_{a}
, and
z_{a}=e^{\lambda_{a}t}
.

Using those eigenvalues and eigenvectors, we can evaluate the state after the repeated CPTP dynamics as

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{n}[\hat{\rho}_{0}]=\sum_{a=1}^{d^{2}}C_{a}z_{a}^{n}\hat{\mathsf{R}}_{a},

(204)

where

\displaystyle C_{a}=\frac{\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\mathsf{L}}_{a}^{\dagger}\hat{\rho}_{0}]}{\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\mathsf{L}}_{a}^{\dagger}\hat{\mathsf{R}}_{a}]}

(205)

is the overlap of the
a
th eigenvector and the initial state
\hat{\rho}_{0}
.
Similarly, for the continuous GKSL dynamics, we have

\displaystyle e^{\mathcal{L}t}[\hat{\rho}_{0}]=\sum_{a=1}^{d^{2}}c_{a}e^{\lambda_{a}t}\hat{r}_{a}

(206)

with

\displaystyle c_{a}=\frac{\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{l}_{a}^{\dagger}\hat{\rho}_{0}]}{\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{l}_{a}^{\dagger}\hat{r}_{a}]}.

(207)

4.5.1
Spectral properties near stationary states and relaxation timescales

Let us first overview relevant topics concerning the spectral gap and relaxation.
Let us assume that the CPTP map is primitive, i.e., the eigenvalue with unit modulus is unique.
Then, Eq. (
204
) is rewritten as

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{n}[\hat{\rho}_{0}]=\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}+\sum_{a=2}^{d^{2}}C_{a}z_{a}^{n}\hat{\mathsf{R}}_{a},

(208)

where
\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}=\hat{\mathsf{R}}_{1}/\mathrm{Tr}(\hat{\mathsf{R}}_{1})
is the unique stationary state of
\mathcal{E}
and we have used
C_{1}=1
because
\hat{\mathsf{L}}_{1}\propto\hat{\mathbb{I}}
in Eq. (
205
).
Since each term in the sum in the right-hand side decays with the factor
z_{a}
when
n
is increased, the asymptotic decay rate of the dynamics is governed by the longest-lived modes.
It is given by the (logarithm of) spectral gap

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathrm{asy}}=-\ln|z_{2}|

(209)

as long as
C_{2}\neq 0
.
This quantity means that the state approaches towards the stationary state following the exponential decay
e^{-\Delta_{\mathrm{asy}}t}
33
33

33

Note that the decay may be accompanied by an oscillation whose frequency is given by
\mathrm{arg}[z_{2}]
.

for asymptotically large
t
with finite
d^{2}
and
C_{2}\neq 0
.
Similarly, for the GKSL case, we have the expansion

\displaystyle e^{\mathcal{L}t}[\hat{\rho}_{0}]=\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}+\sum_{a=2}^{d^{2}}c_{a}e^{\lambda_{a}t}\hat{r}_{a}

(210)

with
\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}=\hat{r}_{1}/\mathrm{Tr}(\hat{r}_{1})
.
Here, we have used
c_{1}=1
because
\hat{l}_{1}\propto\hat{\mathbb{I}}
in Eq. (
207
).
From this expression, we can define

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathrm{asy}}=-\mathrm{Re}[\lambda_{2}]

(211)

as long as
c_{2}\neq 0
, which is called the Liouvillian gap.

One naive expectation is that the inverse of
\Delta_{\mathrm{asy}}
,
\tau_{2}=\Delta_{\mathrm{asy}}^{-1}
, provides the relaxation timescale
\tau_{\mathrm{relax}}
of the dynamics
34
34

34

While the definition of the relaxation time may not be unique, we can take it as, e.g., the mixing time, which is defined as the minimal time
\tau
such that
d(\tau)<\epsilon
. Here,
d(t)=\max_{\hat{\rho}(0)}\|\hat{\rho}(t)-\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}\|_{1}/2
and
\epsilon
is a small but constant cutoff.
Note that
\|\hat{A}\|_{1}=\mathrm{Tr}[\sqrt{\hat{A}^{\dagger}\hat{A}}]
.

.
This is indeed true for many situations, and the relaxation times are evaluated from
\Delta_{\mathrm{asy}}

[
125
]
.
In more general, the timescale when the contribution of the
a
th mode becomes negligible may naively be associated with
\tau_{a}=\Delta_{a}^{-1}
, where

\displaystyle\Delta_{a}=-\ln(|z_{a}|)

(212)

for the CPTP case and

\displaystyle\Delta_{a}=-\mathrm{Re}[\lambda_{a}]

(213)

for the GKSL case. Note that
\Delta_{\mathrm{asy}}=\Delta_{2}
.

In the following, we introduce several topics concerning the spectral gap and relaxation dynamics.
We assume the above relation between the eigenvalues and inverse of the relaxation timescales for the first three topics and then discuss the caveat for this identification in the end.

Mpemba effect

If we choose an initial state with
c_{2}=\cdots=c_{a_{*}-1}=0
but
c_{a_{*}}\neq 0
with
a_{*}\geq 3
,
the relaxation time will become

\displaystyle\tau_{\mathrm{relax}}\sim\tau_{a_{*}}<\tau_{2}

(214)

instead of
\tau_{\mathrm{relax}}\sim\tau_{2}
, where
\Delta_{2}<\Delta_{a_{*}}
is assumed.
This means that the relaxation becomes faster for such initial states than those with
c_{2}\neq 0
.
This fact is recently applied to analyze the “Mpemba effect,” which
originally represents a counter-intuitive observation where initially hotter water can freeze faster than colder water
[
126
]
.
The effect gathers renewed attention in these decades from the community of non-equilibrium statistical mechanics
[
127
,
128
,
129
,
130
,
131
,
132
,
133
,
134
,
135
,
136
,
137
,
138
,
139
]
; in this context, the (classical or quantum) Mpemba effect often indicates a phenomenon where a state initially far from the stationary state relaxes faster than that initially close to the stationary state.
In Markovian open quantum systems described by the GKSL equation, a general mechanism to engineer the quantum Mpemba effect was proposed
[
140
]
.
That is, let us consider states
\hat{\rho}
with
c_{2}\neq 0
and
\hat{\rho}_{0}=\hat{U}\hat{\rho}\hat{U}^{\dagger}
for some unitary matrix
\hat{U}
.
If
\hat{U}
is chosen such that
c_{2}=\cdots=c_{a_{*}-1}=0
and some distance between
e^{\mathcal{L}t}[\hat{\rho}_{0}]
and
\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}
is larger than the distance between
e^{\mathcal{L}t}[\hat{\rho}]
and
\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}
for short times, the quantum Mpemba effect occurs.
This scenario was exemplified for certain situations
[
140
,
141
]
.

Metastability and emergent decoherence-free subspace

Let us consider a situation where
\Delta_{2},\cdots,\Delta_{a_{*}-1}\ll\Delta_{a_{*}}
with
a_{*}\geq 3
.
In this case, for the timescale
t
with
\tau_{a_{*}}\ll t\ll\tau_{a_{*}-1}
, the evolving state effectively stays in a metastable manifold, which is spanned by the eigenmodes
\hat{r}_{1},\cdots,\hat{r}_{a_{*}-1}

[
142
]
.
One important situation is where the dissipation term
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{d}}[\hat{\rho}]=\sum_{b}\hat{L}_{b}\hat{\rho}\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\{\hat{\rho},\hat{L}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{b}\}
is dominant compared with the unitary term
\mathcal{L}_{H}[\hat{\rho}]=-i[\hat{H},\hat{\rho}]
.
If we neglect the unitary part, we can define a stationary (stable) manifold as a manifold spanned by a set of zero modes of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{d}}
.
We assume that there are
a_{*}-1
stationary states created by the existence of a decoherence-free subspace (DFS)
[
143
,
144
]
, where the corresponding projection operator is denoted as
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{DFS}}
; see around Eq. (
238
) for a more detailed discussion.
These zero modes are separated from the longest-living decaying modes by a gap
\Delta_{\mathrm{d},a_{*}}
.
Here,
\Delta_{\mathrm{d},a_{*}}
is defined from the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{d}}
, which is expected to have an order
\sim\|\hat{L}_{b}\|^{2}
.

When small unitary contribution is present, many zero modes for
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{d}}
cease to be exact zero modes for
\mathcal{L}
.
Still, when
J\ll\Delta_{\mathrm{d},a_{*}}
with
J
being the characteristic strength of the Hamiltonian
35
35

35

There is a subtle point in choosing
J
.
For few-level systems,
J
can be chosen as
J=\|\hat{H}\|

[
145
]
.
However, for many-body systems, this choice is not appropriate since
\|\hat{H}\|\ll\Delta_{\mathrm{d},a_{*}}
does not hold for a large system size
V
because
\|\hat{H}\|\propto V
.
Then, it would be natural to take
J
as the microscopic coefficients of the Hamiltonian, e.g., hopping amplitude or the chemical potential, although it is not easy to justify this choice rigorously.

,
we expect that
0\simeq\Delta_{a_{*}-1}\ll\Delta_{a_{*}}\simeq\Delta_{\mathrm{d},a_{*}}
is satisfied, where
\simeq
allows some correction of order of
J
.
Then, there exists the time scale
t
with
\tau_{a_{*}}\ll t\ll\tau_{a_{*}-1}
where the dynamics is effectively constrained within the emergent DFS.
That is, the dynamics is well approximated by a unitary dynamics generated by
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{DFS}}\hat{H}\hat{P}_{\mathrm{DFS}}
, if it is non-vanishing.
This phenomenon, reminiscent of the quantum Zeno effect
[
146
,
144
,
147
,
148
]
, was rigorously justified using the Schrieffer-Wolf transformation
[
149
]
for few-level systems under some assumptions
[
145
]
.
If we assume that the discussion holds for many-body systems
35
, the emergent DFS can be utilized to engineer
[
14
]
exotic many-body dynamics, such as kinetically constrained quantum dynamics
[
150
,
151
]
.

Dissipative phase transition

The spectral analysis can be relevant for understanding dissipative phase transition (DPT) in open quantum maby-body systems with system size
V

[
50
]
.
Let us consider the GKSL dynamics parameterized by
g
.
One of the definitions of the DPT is that, in the thermodynamic limit
V\rightarrow\infty
, an order parameter at the stationary state or its derivatives exhibit singularity when
g
is varied.
For example, Ref.
[
152
]
discussed a second-order transition with
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry breaking
36
36

36

Here, we consider a weak
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry
[
153
,
154
]
satisfying
\hat{G}\mathcal{{L}}[\hat{G}\hat{\rho}\hat{G}]\hat{G}=\mathcal{L}[\hat{\rho}]
.

.
For the symmetry unbroken phase (
g<g_{c}
), the unique stationary state is invariant under the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
operation
\hat{G}
such that
\hat{G}^{2}=\hat{\mathbb{I}}
,
\hat{G}\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}\hat{G}=\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}
.
For the symmetry broken phase (
g>g_{c}
), there are two independent stationary states
\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}^{\pm}
satisfying
\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}^{\mp}=\hat{G}\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}^{\pm}\hat{G}
in the thermodynamic limit
V\rightarrow\infty
.
However, if we consider finite-size systems,
\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}^{\pm}
cannot be the true stationary state.
In this case, for
g<g_{c}
, the eigenmodes
\hat{r}_{1}=\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}
and
\hat{r}_{2}
are separated by a gap that does not vanish for
V\rightarrow\infty
.
In contrast, for
g>g_{c}
, the eigenmodes read
\hat{r}_{1}=\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}\propto\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}^{+}+\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}^{-}
and
\hat{r}_{2}\propto\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}^{+}-\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}^{-}
37
37

37

Note that
\mathrm{Tr}[{\hat{r}_{a}}]=0\;(a\geq 2)
in general due to the trace preservation of the state.
We also note that
\hat{r}_{1}
and
\hat{r}_{2}
actually respect
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry, as
\hat{G}\hat{r}_{1}\hat{G}=\hat{r}_{1}
and
\hat{G}\hat{r}_{2}\hat{G}=-\hat{r}_{2}
.

.
In this case,
\lambda_{2}
is nonzero for finite-size systems but vanishes
\lambda_{2}\rightarrow 0
in the thermodynamic limit.
This means that the symmetry broken modes
\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}^{\pm}
or their linear combination can appear as metastable states depending on the initial state (
\sim(1+c_{2})\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}^{+}+(1-c_{2})\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}^{-}
from Eq. (
210
)).
The lifetime of the metastable state is expected to be
\Delta_{\mathrm{asy}}^{-1}
and grows with
V
.
This picture is consistent with the case in the thermodynamic limit, if we take the limit
\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{V\rightarrow\infty}
38
38

38

If we reverse the order of the two limits,
\lim_{V\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}
, we have a symmetry unbroken unique stationary state even for
g>g_{c}
.

.

Another interesting possibility of the DPT is the dissipative version of time crystals
[
155
,
156
,
157
,
158
,
159
]
, which exhibits a persistent oscillation of an order parameter in the thermodynamic limit with the breaking of time-translation symmetry
[
160
,
161
,
162
]
.
For finite systems, the time crystalline phase has eigenvalues
\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},\cdots
whose real parts vanish but imaginary parts remain finite for
V\rightarrow\infty
.
The corresponding eigenmodes contribute to the persistent oscillation, consistent with the behavior in the thermodynamic limit.
Note that we can have a similar discussion for the CPTP map.
It was found in Ref.
[
155
]
that a periodically driven dissipative Dicke model has a discrete time crystalline phase
[
162
]
;
the corresponding CPTP map has an eigenvalue
z_{2}
, which converges to
-1
in the thermodynamic limit and leads to the period doubling behavior.

Discrepancy between the inverse gap and relaxation timescale

So far, we have assumed that the relaxation timescale
\tau_{\mathrm{relax}}
of systems governed by the GKSL equation is identified as
\tau_{2}
, the inverse of asymptotic decay rate
\Delta_{\mathrm{asy}}=-\mathrm{Re}[\lambda_{2}]
as long as
c_{2}\neq 0
in Eq. (
210
).
However, it is recently widely recognized
39
39

39

The distinction between
\tau_{\mathrm{relax}}
and
\Delta_{\mathrm{asy}}^{-1}
was known before both in classical
[
163
]
and quantum
[
164
,
165
]
systems.
that this identification is not always true
[
166
,
167
,
168
,
169
]
.
This is because the coefficient
c_{a}
, say
c_{2}
, can be exponentially large with respect to the system size
V
, due to the exponential smallness of
\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{l}_{2}^{\dagger}\hat{r}_{2}]
in Eq. (
207
)
40
40

40

Here, we assume the normalization
\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{r}_{a}^{\dagger}\hat{r}_{a}]=\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{l}_{a}^{\dagger}\hat{l}_{a}]=1
.

.
In this case, the amplitude for
a=2
,
c_{2}e^{-\Delta_{\mathrm{asy}}t}
, does not become small until
t\sim\mathcal{O}(V)
, even when
\Delta_{\mathrm{asy}}=\mathcal{O}(V^{0})
.
Namely,
\tau_{\mathrm{relax}}=\mathcal{O}(V)
diverges even for the gapped spectrum.

One of the simplest models for this to occur is the one-dimensional single-particle model with asymmetric dissipative hopping under the open boundary condition
[
166
]
.
Here, the jump operators are assumed to be
\hat{L}_{Ri}=\sqrt{\gamma_{R}}\hat{b}_{i+1}^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{i}\>(1\leq i\leq V-1)
and

\hat{L}_{Li}=\sqrt{\gamma_{L}}\hat{b}_{i+1}^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{i}\>(2\leq i\leq V)

[
170
]
, where
\hat{b}_{i}
is the annihilation operator of the particle at site
i
, and there is no Hamiltonian.
We can easily show that the Liouvillian gap is given by
\Delta_{\mathrm{asy}}=(\sqrt{\gamma_{R}}-\sqrt{\gamma_{L}})^{2}
.
Now, let us start from an initial state where a single particle is placed at the left end and the rest is empty.
If we focus on the number of the particle at the right end,
\hat{n}_{V}=\hat{b}^{\dagger}_{V}\hat{b}_{V}
, as an observable, we can rigorously show that
\braket{\hat{n}_{V}}_{t}
is exponentially suppressed as
\braket{\hat{n}_{V}}_{t}=e^{-\mathcal{O}(V)}
for times
t=\mathcal{O}(V^{0})
, and that the relaxation timescale should be order of
V
or larger
[
171
,
172
]
.
Namely, the relaxation time diverges with respect to
V
, despite the finite Liouvillian gap.
In this case, the left and right eigenmodes with
a=2
exponentially localize at the opposite edges, meaning that their overlap becomes exponentially small as
\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{l}_{2}^{\dagger}\hat{r}_{2}]=e^{-\mathcal{O}(V)}
.
Therefore, the exponentially large coefficient
c_{2}
physically comes from the localization of eigenmodes at the edges of the open boundary condition, which is known as the Liouvillian skin effect
[
166
]
41
41

41

More generally, the localization of eigenmodes of non-Hermitian systems is called the non-Hermitian skin effect
[
173
,
174
,
175
,
176
]
.

.

While we have considered systems described by the GKSL equation, a similar discrepancy between the spectrum and timescale also appears in other contexts
[
177
,
178
,
179
]
.
The general message is that the Liovillian spectrum is not always reliable to evaluate the relaxation timescale.
Instead, it was proposed
[
180
,
181
,
182
]
that the timescale is more characterized by the pseudo-spectrum
[
183
]
, i.e., a set of spectra of slightly perturbed matrices.
As a related fact, let us consider the periodic boundary condition for the above asymmetric hopping model (i.e., the ends at the case with the open boundary condition is perturbed).
Then, the Liouvillian gap becomes vanishing with respect to
L
, where such a sudden change of the spectrum due to the change of the boundary condition is the consequence of the skin effect
[
173
]
.
This gapless feature of the new spectrum is consistent with the divergent relaxation timescale.

4.5.2
Universality of spectral statistics and random matrices

In the previous subsection, we have considered spectral properties near the stationary states, such as the spectral gap, and their relation to relaxation timescales.
In this subsection, we instead consider spectral statistics for the entire spectrum and overview some selected topics, especially focusing on the relevance to random matrix theory.

Entire shape of the spectrum

Let us first consider the entire spectral shape of “typical” GKSL generators.
Before that, we explain the spectral structure of typical non-Hermitian matrices.
According to non-Hermitian random matrix theory,
the so-called circular law
[
184
,
185
,
186
]
holds for the ensemble of matrices whose components follow the independent and identical distribution (iid) with zero mean and a finite variance
[
187
,
188
]
.
Namely, the typical contour of the spectrum on the complex plane asymptotically becomes a circle whose center is at the origin, and the spectral density is uniform within the circle, if we increase the size of the matrix.
In Fig.
6
(a), we show the complex spectrum of a
d^{2}\times d^{2}
complex Ginibre matrix
G
42
42

42

A complex Ginibre matrix is a non-Hermitian random matrix whose components obey iid Gaussian complex variables.

,
normalized by
1/d
such that the spectrum concentrates within the unit circle.
The circular law demonstrates one of the famous examples of the universality of non-Hermitian random matrix theory, i.e., the detailed form of the iid distribution does not matter for its appearance.

Figure 6:

(a) Complex spectrum of a
d^{2}\times d^{2}
Ginibre matrix
G/d
for
d=30
.
(b) Complex spectrum of the GKSL operator
\mathcal{L}
generated by a
(d^{2}-1)\times(d^{2}-1)
random Kossakowski matrix for
d=30
.
The blue circle for (b) has radius
2/d
.

In contrast, if we consider a random GKSL generator
\mathcal{L}
, additional structures lead to distinctions from the circular law for its spectrum
43
43

43

See, e.g., Ref.
[
189
]
for results on random channels, where the Kraus operators are randomly sampled.

, even though
\mathcal{L}
is represented as a non-Hermitian matrix.
To see this, let us consider the GKSL generator in the form of Eq. (
184
).
While it is not trivial how to sample random GKSL generators, Ref.
[
190
]
considered sampling the Kossakowski matrix
C
randomly.
Since the Kossakowski matrix is positive semidefinite, one of the natural ways of sampling is to sample a Ginibre matrix
{G}
and define
C=dGG^{\dagger}/\mathrm{Tr}[GG^{\dagger}]
44
44

44

The coefficient of
GG^{\dagger}
only controls the scale of the spectrum and is not important for the lemon-shape contour, as long as
\hat{H}^{\prime}=0
.

.
As shown in Fig.
6
(b), if there is no unitary part
(\hat{H}^{\prime}=0)
, the spectral shape becomes a lemon-like contour inside a circle with radius
\sim 2/d
and the center at
(-1,0)
45
45

45

This means that the lemon-like contour is gapped from the origin, which corresponds to the stationary-state eigenvalue.

.
Inside the lemon-like contour, the spectral density is non-uniform.
A similar lemon-like contour is obtained for another type of sampling of
C
in the limit of large system sizes, demonstrating the universality of the spectral shape to some extent
46
46

46

It was reported that the lemon-type contour appears in a more physical setting, such as the Lindbladian dynamics of the Sachdev-Ye-Kitaev model
[
191
]
.

.
Reference
[
190
]
also considered the case where
\hat{H}^{\prime}\neq 0
; in this case, the lemon shape is deformed and the contour approaches an ellipse as the strength of
\hat{H}^{\prime}
is increased.

However, if we consider an additional structure of the GKSL equation, it was found that the universality of the above spectral shape no longer holds.
References
[
192
,
193
,
194
]
assumed that
\hat{F}_{i}
are restricted to few-body operators and sampled random
C
appropriately without the unitary part.
In that case, the spectrum is decoupled into distinct clusters, whose decaying timescales are different, rather than the universal lemon-like contour.
Several studies
[
59
,
195
]
considered how the decomposed spectral clusters change due to the unitary effect.
For example, Ref.
[
59
]
investigated physical models (e.g., hardcore bosons) with dissipation and without randomness, finding the decoupled clusters of the GKSL eigenvalues if the dissipation (assumed to be local dephasing) is strong.
When the dissipation is weakened compared with the unitary dynamics, they found that the decoupled clusters touch with one another, manifesting a transition of the spectral shape.
It was shown that this transition physically alters the dynamics of coherence in the system.

Universality of spectral statistics

As discussed above, the entire spectral shape does not show strong universality, i.e., it significantly depends on the structure of many-body systems.
In contrast, certain spectral statistics exhibit the universality predicted by random matrix theory if the system considered is sufficiently “complicated.”
To explain it in more detail, let us first review some well known results for isolated quantum systems and their relation with Hermitian random matrix theory.
We especially focus on the eigenvalue-spacing distribution
P_{H}(s)
, which is the distribution of the properly normalized difference between neighboring energy eigenvalues of the Hamiltonian,
s_{\alpha}\propto E_{\alpha+1}-E_{\alpha}
, in the middle of the spectrum.
If we take a Gaussian random matrix as a Hamiltonian,
P_{H}(s)
becomes close to the Wigner-Dyson distribution,
P_{H}(s)\simeq a_{\beta}s^{\beta}e^{-b_{\beta}s^{2}}
, where
\beta
takes either of the three values
\beta=1,2,
or
4
depending on the time-reversal symmetry
[
196
,
197
]
of matrices, and
a_{\beta},\,b_{\beta}
are constants.
This is in contrast with the Poisson-type spacing distribution
P_{H}(s)=e^{-s}
obtained from random sequences.
Importantly, sufficiently complicated Hamiltonians, which may have structures, e.g., few-body or local interactions, and not be even random, can have
P_{H}(s)
described by random matrix theory with the corresponding time-reversal symmetry.
This has been confirmed in, e.g., the excitation spectrum of nuclei
[
198
]
, semiclassical systems whose classical limit is chaotic
[
199
]
, and non-integrable many-body systems without classical counterparts
[
200
]
47
47

47

Semiclassical systems whose classical limits are chaotic are called quantum chaotic systems.
Non-integrable many-body systems without classical counterparts, whose spectral statistics obey the random matrix theory, are often called quantum many-body chaotic systems.

.
Instead, if the Hamiltonian is integrable,
P_{H}(s)
can display the Poisson statistics
[
201
,
200
]
.
Note that a similar strong universality appears for other spectral statistics
[
202
,
203
,
197
]
and eigenstate statistics
[
204
,
205
]
.
While the universality is confirmed mainly numerically, it is analytically understood even for some physical models
[
206
,
207
,
208
]
.

Recently, there appeared various studies aiming to extend the correspondence between physical systems and random matrix theory to dissipative quantum dynamics
[
209
,
210
,
211
,
212
,
213
,
214
,
215
,
216
,
217
,
218
,
219
,
220
,
221
,
222
,
223
,
224
,
225
,
226
]
.
That is, certain statistics in the middle of the spectrum relevant for sufficiently complicated dynamics were found to be described by non-Hermitian random matrix theory.
This correspondence was first discussed in Ref.
[
227
]
, which argued that the eigenvalue-spacing statistics
P(s)
for the CPTP map describing the periodic kicked tops with damping are described by the universal distribution of non-Hermitian random matrices (the Poisson distribution) when the dynamics is chaotic (integrable) in the classical limit.
Here, we note that the eigenvalue spacing
s_{a}
is defined through the minimal distance of eigenvalues on a complex plane (e.g.,
s_{a}\propto\min_{b}|z_{a}-z_{b}|
for the case of the CPTP map), since eigenvalues are no longer real.
Later, similar universal eigenvalue-spacing statistics for non-Hermitian random matrices were found to appear for non-integrable many-body systems described by non-Hermitian Hamiltonians
[
228
,
210
]
and GKSL generators
[
211
]
.
It was also found that symmetry plays an important role for random matrix theory in open quantum systems
48
48

48

While there are 38 symmetry classes for general non-Hermitian matrices
[
229
]
(in contrast with 10 symmetry classes for Hermitian matrices
[
230
]
), less number of symmetry classes exist for GKSL generators and CPTP maps due to additional constraints
[
231
,
232
,
233
,
234
]
.
.
For example, Ref.
[
212
]
claimed that, among 38 non-Hermitian symmetry classes
[
229
]
, the eigenvalue-spacing distributions
P(s)
take only three different universality classes depending on the transposition symmetries (e.g., the symmetry given as
H=H^{\mathsf{T}}
), instead of the time-reversal symmetry (e.g., the symmetry described as
H=H^{*}
)
49
49

49

Reference
[
212
]
numerically verified three distinct universal classes of
P(s)
(see also Ref.
[
209
]
) with arguments using the degenerate-perturbation theory.
Recently, the existence of the three distinct classes have been studied analytically
[
235
,
236
,
237
]
for different eigenvalue statistics.

.

The universality of non-Hermitian random matrix theory has also been discussed beyond eigenvalue-spacing distributions
P(s)
.
Various statistics, such as the complex-spacing ratio
[
209
,
213
]
, spectral rigidity
[
238
]
, dissipative spectral form factor
[
239
]
, singular-value statistics
[
240
,
241
,
242
]
and eigenstate statistics
[
243
,
244
,
245
,
246
,
247
,
248
,
249
]
were found to exhibit universality.
These facts motivate researchers to characterize the dissipative version of quantum chaos
47
through universal statistics of non-Hermitian random matrix theory.
However, recent studies on dissipative systems with well-defined classical counterparts
50
50

50

Interestingly, the models considered in Ref.
[
227
]
have also been revisited
[
250
]
and found that the correspondence principle fails.

found a caveat to this correspondence: the universal random-matrix statistics in the middle of the spectrum can appear even when the corresponding classical systems do not have a chaotic attractor in the long time
[
251
,
250
]
.
Indeed, since eigenmodes in the middle of the spectrum decay more rapidly than the longest-lived mode for the CPTP or GKSL dynamics, i.e.,
\Delta_{a}\ll\Delta_{\mathrm{asy}}
with
\Delta_{a}
being defined in Eqs. (
212
) and (
213
), the universal statistics are rather expected to be related to transient dynamics
[
243
,
252
,
253
]
.

5
Typical properties of quantum trajectories: ergodicity and purification

Keeping in mind the properties of the averaged dynamics, i.e., the CPTP map or quantum master equations discussed in the previous chapter, we here discuss properties of quantum trajectories.
Since quantum trajectories are determined stochastically, we will seek for properties common to
typical
quantum trajectories, instead of all trajectories.

While analyzing typical properties of quantum trajectories are complicated because we should treat probability measures determined by the Born probability rules, various developments have been made around the last decade
[
26
,
27
,
28
,
29
,
30
,
31
,
32
,
33
,
34
]
by many researchers, including the community of mathematical physics.
In this chapter, we especially discuss two pioneering works by Kümmerer and Maassen, i.e., the ergodicity of linear observables
[
254
,
255
]
and purification
[
256
]
of quantum trajectories.
Our aim in this chapter is to overview their results, which were formulated in a mathematical language, in a physicist-friendly (and less rigorous) way.
In Chapter
6
, we will present more sophisticated recent results, such as the ergodicity of nonlinear quantities and the convergence of the Lyapunov exponents for typical quantum trajectories.

In the following, we basically discuss quantum trajectories for discrete times on the basis of Eq. (
53
), unless stated otherwise.

5.1
Ergodicity of quantum trajectories and linear quantities

While the meaning of ergodicity depends on the context, the ergodicity of quantum trajectories usually refers to the equivalence of the long-time average of a single quantum trajectory and the average over ensembles and long times.
For the simplest case, it is roughly formulated as

\displaystyle\overline{\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}}=\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}=\overline{\mathbb{E}\left[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}\right]}

(215)

for almost all (i.e., typical) trajectories characterized by measurement outcomes
\bm{b}
, where

\displaystyle\overline{f_{n}}=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}f_{n}

(216)

is the long-time average of
f_{n}
and
\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}
is the
\bm{b}
-independent stationary state of the map
\mathcal{E}
.
Equation (
215
) indicates the ergodicity for linear observables, i.e.,

\displaystyle\overline{\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}\hat{A}]}=\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}\hat{A}]=\overline{\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{Tr}(\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}\hat{A})\right]}

(217)

for almost all trajectories.
Equation (
215
) (and its continuous-time version) was first proven in Ref.
[
254
]
under some assumptions.
Its quantum-diffusion-equation version (cf. Eq. (
147
)) was also discussed in Ref.
[
30
]
.

While the above formulation is for quantities linear in
\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}
, the existence of invariant measure, which can be used even to nonlinear quantities in
\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}
, was also derived in Ref.
[
27
]
under suitable assumptions (see Secs.
6.1
and
6.2
).
Moreover, the ergodicity for jump statistics (e.g.,
N_{\mu}(t)
in the continuous-time case), instead of the function of
\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}
, were also proven
[
257
,
258
]
.
Furthermore, beyond ergodicity, fluctuation properties, such as the central limit theorem around the average, have been discussed
[
26
,
31
,
259
]
.

5.1.1
Result by Kümmerer and Maassen

Now, let us present the statement of the ergodicity for linear quantities in
\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}
more formally.
We consider quantum trajectories, which are characterized by a sequence of measurement outcomes
\bm{b}=(b_{1},b_{2},\cdots)
for the time-independent CP-instrument
\{\mathcal{E}_{b}\}
.
The corresponding CPTP map is given by
\mathcal{E}=\sum_{b}\mathcal{E}_{b}
.
The state at time step
n
is given in Eq. (
53
), where the initial state is set to
\hat{\rho}_{0}
.
Then, the following holds
[
255
]
.

1.

Under the above setup,

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}}^{\mathrm{\infty}}:=\overline{\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}}

(218)

exists for any initial state
\hat{\rho}_{0}
almost surely
51
51

51

A sequence of random variables
X_{1},X_{2},\cdots
is said to converge to
X
almost surely if

\displaystyle\mathrm{Prob}\left[\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}X_{n}=X\right]=1.

(219)

The almost-sure convergence is a stronger condition than the convergence in probability, stating that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{Prob}\left[|X_{n}-X|\leq\epsilon\right]=1.

(220)

for all
\epsilon>0
.
As an example
[
188
]
that highlights these two notions, let us pick up a real number
r
uniformly from
[0,1]
. We define

S_{n}\>(n\geq 1)
as a range where the decimal expansion of
r
starts with
n
(e.g.,
S_{418}=[0.418,0.419)
).
Now,
X_{n}
is defined as

\displaystyle X_{n}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&r\in S_{n}\\
0&r\notin S_{n}\end{array}\right.

Then,
X_{n}
converges to
0
in probability. However, since we have infinitely many
n
such that
X_{n}=1
for every
r
,
X_{n}
does not show the almost-sure convergence to
0
.
As mentioned in the introduction, we often use the term “typical” or “almost all” to discuss the behaviors of quantum trajectories, and they basically mean the almost-sure convergence. We sometimes explicitly indicate it to stress the exact meaning.

with respect to the probability measure of quantum trajectories.

2.

\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}}^{\mathrm{\infty}}
is a random variable that satisfies

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}}^{\mathrm{\infty}}]

\displaystyle=\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}}^{\mathrm{\infty}}

(223)

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}}^{\mathrm{\infty}}]=\overline{\mathcal{E}^{n}}[\hat{\rho}_{0}].

(224)

3.

In particular, if
\mathcal{E}
has only one stationary state
\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}
, the ergodicity holds, i.e.,

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}}^{\mathrm{\infty}}=\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}={\mathbb{E}\left[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}}^{\mathrm{\infty}}\right]}

(225)

for any initial state
\hat{\rho}_{0}
almost surely with respect to the probability measure of quantum trajectories.

Let us explain intuitive meanings of this result, as schematically shown in Fig.
7
.
(1) means that while
\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}\hat{A}]
temporarily fluctuates, its long-time average converges for almost all trajectories.
(3) means that if there is a unique stationary state for the CPTP map
\mathcal{E}
, whose sufficient condition has already been detailed in Chapter
4
, the long-time average
\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}}^{\mathrm{\infty}}
is described by it, i.e., the ergodicity in Eq. (
225
) (or Eq. (
217
)) holds; see Fig.
7
(a).
Note that non-decaying oscillatory eigenmodes corresponding to the peripheral spectrum with
|z_{a}|=1
and
z_{a}\neq 1
(see Eq. (
157
)), which may exist in CPTP maps that are not primitive, vanish in the long-time average in the right-hand side of Eq. (
225
).
If the CPTP map is primitive, such modes are absent and thus Eq. (
225
) becomes

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}}^{\mathrm{\infty}}=\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}\right].

(226)

(2) is related to the situation where
\mathcal{E}
has multiple stationary states and the ergodicity breaks down, as shown in Fig.
7
(b).
Instead, each quantum trajectory averaged over long times is probabilistically determined and becomes a fixed point of the CPTP map.
If we average each long-time trajectory over all measurement outcomes, we obtain a stationary state of the CPTP map that depends on the initial state
\hat{\rho}_{0}
.

Figure 7:
Schematic figures representing (non-)ergodicity of quantum trajectories for linear observables. The expectation value of an observable
\hat{A}
for each quantum trajectory
\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}
(blue and red) temporally fluctuates. However, the temporal average of
\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}
exists for almost all trajectories. (a) For ergodic cases, the temporal average for almost all trajectories coincides with that for ensemble averaged dynamics. (b) If the ergodicity is broken, the temporal average becomes a random variable that is different from the ensemble-averaged one in general.

The proof of the ergodicity explained above is given in Appendix
C
.
There, we basically follow Ref.
[
255
]
but try to illustrate it in a physicist-friendly way, at the cost of mathematical rigor.
The mathematical trick to show the almost-sure convergence is to employ the martingale convergence theorem
[
260
,
261
]
.

While the above result is for the discrete-time case, a similar result holds for quantum trajectories under continuous-time measurement
[
254
]
.
In this case, the ergodicity of quantum trajectories requires the unique stationary state for the GKSL generator
\mathcal{L}
.

5.1.2
Examples

We explain some examples to intuitively understand the meaning of ergodicity and its breakdown.
The first two examples are simple toy examples, and the third example is the ergodicity breaking of quantum diffusion recently demonstrated in Ref.
[
262
]
.

Example 1.
Let us consider a four-level system, where each level is denoted by
\ket{0},\ket{1},\ket{2},\ket{3}
.
We assume that the measurement operators are given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{M}_{0}&=\ket{0}\bra{0},\\
\hat{M}_{1}&=\ket{1}\bra{1},\\
\hat{M}_{2}&=\ket{1}\bra{3}+\ket{0}\bra{2}.\end{split}

(227)

We can easily confirm that
\sum_{b}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b}=\hat{\mathbb{I}}
and that
\mathcal{E}
has multiple stationary states, namely

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}[c\ket{0}\bra{0}+(1-c)\ket{1}\bra{1}]=c\ket{0}\bra{0}+(1-c)\ket{1}\bra{1}

(228)

for arbitrary
c\in[0,1]
.
Indeed, the corresponding CPTP map is not irreducible (see Theorem
2
), as
\mathbb{K}
does not include, e.g.,
\ket{2}\bra{2}

or
\ket{3}\bra{3}
and thus
\mathbb{K}\neq\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
.

Figure 8:
Transitions of the state
\hat{\rho}_{0}
for
Example 1
. The state becomes
\ket{0}\bra{0}
and
\ket{1}\bra{1}
with probabilities
\gamma/2+(1-\gamma)\alpha
and
\gamma/2+(1-\gamma)(1-\alpha)
, respectively. This means that the ergodicity is broken, while the purification occurs.

Let us consider quantum trajectories starting from an initial state

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{0}=\gamma\ket{+}\bra{+}+(1-\gamma)(\alpha\ket{2}\bra{2}+(1-\alpha)\ket{3}\bra{3}),

(229)

where
\gamma,\alpha\in[0,1]
and
\ket{+}=\frac{\ket{0}+\ket{1}}{\sqrt{2}}
.
The transitions and their probabilities are illustrated in Fig.
8
.
After the first measurement,
\hat{\rho}_{0}

is mapped to

\ket{0}\bra{0}\>(b_{1}=0),\ket{1}\bra{1}\>(b_{1}=1),
or
\alpha\ket{0}\bra{0}+(1-\alpha)\ket{1}\bra{1}\>(b_{1}=2)
with the probabilities
\gamma/2
,
\gamma/2
, and
1-\gamma
, respectively.
The states
\ket{0}\bra{0}
and
\ket{1}\bra{1}
become unchanged after subsequent measurements.
On the other hand, the state
\alpha\ket{0}\bra{0}+(1-\alpha)\ket{1}\bra{1}
becomes
\ket{0}\bra{0}\>(b_{2}=0)
or
\ket{1}\bra{1}\>(b_{2}=1)
with probabilities
\alpha
and
1-\alpha
, respectively, after the second measurement.

Therefore, we find

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n(\geq 2)}=\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}}^{\infty}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\ket{0}\bra{0}&\quad\mathrm{Prob}:\frac{\gamma}{2}+(1-\gamma)\alpha,\\
\ket{1}\bra{1}&\quad\mathrm{Prob}:\frac{\gamma}{2}+(1-\gamma)(1-\alpha).\end{array}\right.

We also find that

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{E}^{n}}[\hat{\rho}_{0}]=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{E}^{n}[\hat{\rho}_{0}]=\left(\frac{\gamma}{2}+(1-\gamma)\alpha\right)\ket{0}\bra{0}+\left(\frac{\gamma}{2}+(1-\gamma)(1-\alpha)\right){\ket{1}\bra{1},}

(232)

which means that
the ergodicity breaks down due to multiple stationary states for
\mathcal{E}
.
In contrast, we can confirm (2) above, i.e.,
\mathcal{E}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}}^{\infty}]=\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}}^{\infty}
and

\mathbb{E}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}}^{\infty}]=\overline{\mathcal{E}^{n}}[\hat{\rho}_{0}]
.

Finally, we note that the purification of the quantum state, which will be detailed in Sec.
5.2
, occurs in this case.
That is, while
\hat{\rho}_{0}
is originally a mixed state,
\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}}^{\infty}
becomes a pure state.

Example 2.

We next consider a four-level system with the measurement operators

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{M}_{0}&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\ket{0}\bra{0},\\
\hat{M}_{1}&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\ket{1}\bra{1},\\
\hat{M}_{2}&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\ket{1}\bra{0}+\ket{0}\bra{1}),\\
\hat{M}_{3}&=\ket{1}\bra{3}+\ket{0}\bra{2},\end{split}

(233)

which satisfy
\sum_{b}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b}=\hat{\mathbb{I}}
.
In this case, the CPTP map
\mathcal{E}
has only one stationary state,

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}=\frac{1}{2}(\ket{0}\bra{0}+\ket{1}\bra{1}).

(234)

Note that the corresponding CPTP map is not irreducible in this case.
Indeed, while the stationary state is unique, it is not positive definite.

Let us again start from an initial state given in Eq. (
229
).
We first observe that in the long run, the measurement outcomes with
b=0
or
b=1
are obtained, after which the state always becomes
\ket{0}\bra{0}
or
\ket{1}\bra{1}
.
If the state is
\ket{0}\bra{0}
(
\ket{1}\bra{1}
), the following measurement leads to the state
\ket{0}\bra{0}
(
\ket{1}\bra{1}
) with
b=0
(
b=1
) or the state

\ket{1}\bra{1}
(
\ket{0}\bra{0}
) with
b=2
.
These possibilities occur with the same probability, as schematically shown in Fig.
9
.

Figure 9:
Transitions of the state
\hat{\rho}_{0}
for
Example 2
. After long times, typical trajectories become pure and they change into
\ket{0}\bra{0}
or
\ket{1}\bra{1}
with equal probabilities. In this case, the purification and ergodicity for almost all trajectories hold true.

Consequently, after sufficiently long times, we find that
\ket{0}\bra{0}
and
\ket{1}\bra{1}
appear with the same frequency for typical trajectories.
Therefore, the long-time average becomes

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}}^{\infty}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\ket{0}\bra{0}+\ket{1}\bra{1}\right)=\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}},

(235)

meaning that the ergodicity holds true.
We can also see that purification occurs for typical trajectories, since
\ket{0}\bra{0}
and
\ket{1}\bra{1}
are pure states.

We note that long-time average of rare trajectories can have different properties.
For example, if we find
b=0
for all of the outcomes,
\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}}^{\infty}=\ket{0}\bra{0}\neq\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}
.
As another example,
if we find
b=2
for all of the outcomes, we find

\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}}^{\infty}=\ket{+}\bra{+}\neq\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}
.

Example 3.

The final example in this subsection is the ergodicity breakdown for continuous-time quantum trajectories discussed in Ref.
[
262
]
.
We especially focus on the diffusion-type stochastic equation in Eq. (
147
) with the case of an initial pure state,
\hat{L}=\hat{L}^{\dagger}
(only one jump type), and
\theta=0
for simplicity.
Then, it turns out that the corresponding stochastic equation for the pure state is given by

\displaystyle d\ket{\psi_{c}}=\left(-i\hat{H}-\frac{\hat{L}^{2}}{2}+\hat{L}\braket{\hat{L}}_{c}-\frac{\braket{\hat{L}}_{c}^{2}}{2}\right)\ket{\psi_{c}}dt+\left(\hat{L}-\braket{\hat{L}}_{c}\right)\ket{\psi_{c}}dW.

(236)

Now, consider eigenvalues of
\hat{L}
as

\displaystyle\hat{L}\ket{q_{k,m}}=q_{k}\ket{q_{k,m}},

(237)

where
m
is the label for possible degeneracy.
We then define

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{DFS}}^{(k)}=\mathrm{Span}\{\ket{q_{k,m}}\}_{m}.

(238)

If
\hat{H}\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{DFS}}^{(k)}
for all
\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{DFS}}^{(k)}
,

\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{DFS}}^{(k)}
is called a decoherence-free subspace (DFS)
[
143
,
144
]
.
In this case, for
\ket{\psi_{c}(t)}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{DFS}}^{(k)}
, we find
(\hat{L}-\braket{\hat{L}}_{c;t})\ket{\psi_{c}(t)}=0
and thus

\displaystyle d\ket{\psi_{c}(t)}=-i\hat{H}\ket{\psi_{c}(t)}dt.

(239)

Namely, the effect of the dissipation vanishes, and the state essentially evolves via the unitary dynamics within
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{DFS}}^{(k)}
.

Under this situation, the GKSL equation obtained by the average over outcomes,

\displaystyle\frac{d\hat{\rho}}{dt}=-i[\hat{H},\hat{\rho}]+\hat{L}\hat{\rho}\hat{L}-\frac{1}{2}\{\hat{L}^{2},\hat{\rho}\},

(240)

have multiple stationary states.
To see this, let us consider a projected Hamlitonian onto the DFS,

\hat{H}_{\mathrm{DFS}}^{(k)}=\hat{P}_{\mathrm{DFS}}^{(k)}\hat{H}\hat{P}_{\mathrm{DFS}}^{(k)}
, where
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{DFS}}^{(k)}
is the projection operator to
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{DFS}}^{(k)}
.
Then, it is easy to confirm that
\ket{E_{\mathrm{DFS}}^{(k)}}\bra{E_{\mathrm{DFS}}^{(k)}}
is a stationary state for the GKSL equation, where
\ket{E_{\mathrm{DFS}}^{(k)}}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{DFS}}^{(k)}
is an eigenstate of
\hat{H}_{\mathrm{DFS}}^{(k)}
.
In contrast, we also have a trivial stationary state
\frac{\hat{\mathbb{I}}}{\mathrm{dim}[\mathcal{H}]}
, which acts on the entire Hilbert space and thus different from
\ket{E_{\mathrm{DFS}}^{(k)}}\bra{E_{\mathrm{DFS}}^{(k)}}
.

The existence of multiple stationary states indicates that the ergodicity of quantum trajectories breaks down for this system, which we will explicitly demonstrate below.
To consider the time evolution of
\ket{\psi_{c}(t)}
,
we first decompose it as
[
262
]

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{c}(t)}=\sum_{\ket{q_{m}}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{DFS}}^{(k)}}c_{m}(t)\ket{q_{m}}+\sum_{\ket{p_{l}}\in\mathcal{H}\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{DFS}}^{(k)}}d_{l}(t)\ket{p_{l}}

(241)

Here, since we will consider a fixed DFS in the following, we simply omit the subscript
k
for quantities such as
\ket{q_{m}}
.

If we define

\displaystyle|c(t)|^{2}=\braket{\psi_{c}(t)|\left(\sum_{\ket{q_{m}}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{DFS}}^{(k)}}\ket{q_{m}}\bra{q_{m}}\right)|\psi_{c}(t)}=\sum_{\ket{q_{m}}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{DFS}}^{(k)}}|c_{m}(t)|^{2}=\sum_{\ket{q_{m}}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{DFS}}^{(k)}}|\braket{q_{m}|\psi_{c}(t)}|^{2},

(242)

we find

\displaystyle d(|c(t)|^{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ket{q_{m}}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{DFS}}^{(k)}}\braket{q_{m}|\left(\ket{d\psi_{c}(t)}\bra{\psi_{c}(t)}+\ket{\psi_{c}(t)}\bra{d\psi_{c}(t)}+\ket{d\psi_{c}(t)}\bra{d\psi_{c}(t)}\right)|q_{m}}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{\ket{q_{m}}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{DFS}}^{(k)}}c_{m}^{*}(t)\braket{q_{m}|\hat{L}-\braket{\hat{L}}_{c;t}|\psi_{c}(t)}+\mathrm{c.c.}\right)dW

\displaystyle=\left[2|c(t)|^{2}\left(q(1-|c(t)|^{2})-\sum_{\ket{p_{l}},\ket{p_{l^{\prime}}}\in\mathcal{H}\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{DFS}}^{(k)}}d_{l^{\prime}}^{*}(t)d_{l}(t)\braket{p_{l^{\prime}}|\hat{L}|p_{l}}\right)\right]dW,

(243)

where we have used
dtdW=0
,
dW^{2}=dt
, and the fact that
\braket{q_{m}|\hat{H}|p_{l}}=0
since
\hat{H}\ket{q_{m}}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{DFS}}^{(k)}
.
Note that we write
q_{k}
just as
q
, i.e.,
\hat{L}\ket{q_{m}}=q\ket{q_{m}}
.

Now, we ask what is the stationary distribution for
|c(t)|^{2}
, starting from the initial state

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{0}}=\sum_{\ket{q_{m}}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{DFS}}^{(k)}}c_{m}(0)\ket{q_{m}}+\sum_{\ket{p_{l}}\in\mathcal{H}\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{DFS}}^{(k)}}d_{l}(0)\ket{p_{l}}.

(244)

For this purpose, we first notice

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[d|c(t)|^{2}\right]\propto\mathbb{E}[dW]=0,

(245)

from which we can conclude

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|c(t)|^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[|c(0)|^{2}\right]=|c(0)|^{2}.

(246)

Next, we notice that there are two solutions for
d|c(t)|^{2}=0
,

|c(t)|^{2}=1
and
|c(t)|^{2}=0
, where we have used the normalization condition

\displaystyle|c(t)|^{2}+\sum_{\ket{p_{l}}\in\mathcal{H}\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{DFS}}^{(k)}}|d_{l}(t)|^{2}=1.

(247)

If we take generic
\hat{H}
and
\hat{L}
within our setting, we expect that there are no other solutions for
d|c(t)|^{2}=0
, so we assume this in the following.

Then, the stationary probability distribution for
|c(t)|^{2}
with respect to the ensemble of quantum trajectories
52
52

52

We first notice that
|c(t)|^{2}
converges to a stationary value because of the Martingale convergence theorem
[
260
]
since
\mathbb{E}[d|c(t)|^{2}]=0
and
\mathbb{E}[|c(t)|^{2}]
is bounded. Then, by the assumption,
|c(t)|^{2}
takes a stationary value 0 or 1, i.e., the stationary distribution is given by a sum of
\delta(|c(t)|^{2}-1)
and
\delta(|c(t)|^{2})
. Their weights are determined from the condition in Eq. (
246
).

should take the form

\displaystyle(1-|c(0)|^{2})\delta(|c(t)|^{2})+|c(0)|^{2}\delta(|c(t)|^{2}-1),

(248)

i.e.,
|c(t)|^{2}
becomes 0 and 1 with probabilities
1-|c(0)|^{2}
and
|c(0)|^{2}
, respectively.
Now, let us assume
0<|c(0)|<1
. In this case, since

\displaystyle\overline{|c(t)|^{2}}=(1\>\>\text{or}\>\>0)\neq|c(0)|^{2}=\overline{\mathbb{E}\left[|c(t)|^{2}\right]},

(249)

we find that the ergodicity of quantum trajectories actually breaks down
53
53

53

Note that
|c(t)|^{2}
is a linear observable with respect to
\ket{\psi_{c}(t)}\bra{\psi_{c}(t)}
, which is understood from Eq. (
242
).
.

Intuitively, trajectories with
|c(t)|^{2}\rightarrow 0
are noisy trajectories due to
dW
, since it is out of DFS.
In contrast, trajectories with
|c(t)|^{2}\rightarrow 1
are noiseless trajectories, which may exhibit coherent dynamics, since it is fully in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{DFS}}
. These trajectories are schematically shown in Fig.
10
.

Figure 10:
Schematic illustration of evolution for a quantum trajectory in
Example 3
(see Fig. 1 of Ref.
[
262
]
for actual time evolution). With a probability
|c(0)|^{2}\>(1-|c(0)|^{2})
, the long-time trajectories exhibit coherent (incoherent) dynamics, where
|c(t)|^{2}
approaches one (zero).

In Ref.
[
262
]
, the authors considered concrete models showing such breakdown of the ergodicity.
For example, they considered a one-dimensional XY model with a magnetic field and on-site dephasing.
For certain system sizes, the DFSs appear, in which a local spin coherently synchronizes (or anti-synchronizes) with other local spins
[
263
]
.
If we start from some initial state with
0<|c(0)|^{2}<1
, one actually finds that some trajectories (anti-)synchronize because they fall into the DFS, while there also exist trajectories that exhibit only noisy dynamics.
This also demonstrates the breakdown of the ergodicity in this system.
While this example focuses on the relation between the ergodicity breaking and the DFS,
a more general mathematical discussion for the probability and time for a quantum trajectory to converge into one of the subspaces has recently been discussed
[
264
]
.

5.2
Purification of quantum trajectories

As another important property of quantum trajectories, we next discuss their purification.
Let us take an initially mixed state
\hat{\rho}_{0}
.
If we perform a projective measurement with a rank-one operator, we immediately obtain a pure state,
\hat{P}_{\eta}\hat{\rho}_{0}\hat{P}_{\eta}{\propto}\ket{\eta}\bra{\eta}
.
While the indirect measurement does not immediately change an initial mixed state to a pure state, the purity
\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\rho}^{2}]
of the state
\hat{\rho}
is expected not to decrease after the measurement.
This can be mathematically understood from the inequality by Nielsen
[
265
]
54
54

54

While this inequality is intuitive, the proof in Ref.
[
265
]
is rather complicated, where the majorization technique is used. Here, instead of a general proof, we give a simple proof available only for
k=2
. Using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality twice, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\rho}^{2}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{b,b^{\prime}}\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\rho}\hat{M}_{b^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b^{\prime}}\hat{\rho}]\leq\sum_{b,b^{\prime}}|\mathrm{Tr}[\sqrt{\hat{\rho}}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b}\sqrt{\hat{\rho}}\sqrt{\hat{\rho}}\hat{M}_{b^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b^{\prime}}\sqrt{\hat{\rho}}]|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{b,b^{\prime}}\sqrt{\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\rho}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\rho}]\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{M}_{b^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b^{\prime}}\hat{\rho}\hat{M}_{b^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b^{\prime}}\hat{\rho}]}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{b}\sqrt{p_{b}}\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\rho}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\rho}]}{p_{b}}}\right)^{2}\leq\sum_{b}\frac{\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\rho}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\rho}]}{p_{b}}=\sum_{b}p_{b}\mathrm{Tr}[(\hat{\rho}_{b}^{\prime})^{2}].

(250)

,

\displaystyle\sum_{b}p_{b}\mathrm{Tr}[(\hat{\rho}_{b}^{\prime})^{k}]\geq\mathrm{Tr}[(\hat{\rho})^{k}],

(251)

where
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
p_{b},\hat{\rho}_{b}^{\prime}
are given in Eq. (
14
).
This means that the average purity
(k=2)
of post-measurement states becomes larger than or unchanged from the original purity.

Therefore, we expect that repeating the measurement will eventually lead to the purification, i.e.,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}\;\;\text{becomes a pure state,}

(252)

or, it is rephrased as

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}\right)^{2}\right]=1.

(253)

Such a purification property recently attracts attention in the context of measurement-induced phase transitions
[
266
]
.

We are especially interested in whether quantum trajectories become purified almost surely after sufficiently long times, focusing on finite-dimensional systems.
One of the equivalent conditions for the almost sure purification of the trajectories is the one discussed in Refs.
[
256
,
27
]
, which is stated as follows:
if there exist an orthogonal projector
\hat{\mathcal{Q}}
and constants
\zeta_{\bm{b}_{n}}
such that

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{Q}}\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n}^{\dagger}\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n}\hat{\mathcal{Q}}=\zeta_{\bm{b}_{n}}\hat{\mathcal{Q}}

(254)

for any
n
and all
\bm{b}_{n}
55
55

55

While
\bm{b}
represents a sequence of measurement outcomes in most part of this review, the meaning of
\bm{b}
will be extended in Chapter
7
.
There,
\bm{b}
involves not only discrete measurement outcomes but also continuous variables, such as random unitaries chosen from a classical probability measure.
In this case,
“all
\bm{b}_{n}
” should be rephrased as “almost all
\bm{b}_{n}
”.
While Ref.
[
256
]
only considers discrete
\bm{b}
, Ref.
[
27
]
also considers continuous
\bm{b}
.

,
then
\mathcal{\hat{Q}}
satisfies
\mathrm{rank}[\mathcal{\hat{Q}}]<2
.
Note that
\hat{\mathcal{Q}}
should be independent of
\bm{b}
.

To understand one direction of the equivalence, let us assume that a projector
\hat{\mathcal{Q}}
with
\mathrm{rank}[\hat{\mathcal{Q}}]\geq 2
exists such that Eq. (
254
) holds true for any
n
and all
\bm{b}_{n}
.
Then, if we consider an initial state
\hat{\rho}_{0}=\hat{\mathcal{Q}}/\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\mathcal{Q}}]
,
the realizable state at step
n
becomes
\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}={\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n}\hat{\mathcal{Q}}\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n}^{\dagger}}/\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n}\hat{\mathcal{Q}}\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n}^{\dagger}]
, where
\bm{b}
is restricted to the ones for which
\zeta_{\bm{b}_{n}}\neq 0
.
Then, the purity at time-step
n
reads

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}\right)^{2}\right]=\frac{\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n}\hat{\mathcal{Q}}\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n}^{\dagger}\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n}\hat{\mathcal{Q}}\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n}^{\dagger}]}{\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n}\hat{\mathcal{Q}}\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n}^{\dagger}]^{2}}=\frac{1}{\mathrm{Tr}[\mathcal{\hat{Q}}]}<1,

(255)

where we have used Eq. (
254
).
Therefore, purification indeed does not occur in this case.

While we skip the complete proof of the other side of the equivalence,
we discuss one-step and two-step versions of the condition in detail in Sec.
5.2.1
.
The discussions in that section correspond to sufficient conditions for which the purification holds true for almost all quantum trajectories.
The proof for the one-step version is given in Appendix
D
.
We then provide concrete examples of the presence or absence of purification in Sec.
5.2.2
.

We also note that the condition in Eq. (
254
) is related to the matrix rank of
\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n}
, while we do not explain mathematical details here. Instead, in Sec.
5.2.3
, we explain the relation between the purification of
\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}
and the rank of
\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n}
in a physicist-friendly manner.

5.2.1
Sufficient condition of purification

Here, we discuss some sufficient conditions for the purification of typical quantum trajectories on the basis of Ref.
[
256
]
by Maassen and Kümmerer.
By considering
n=1
case for Eq. (
254
),
one finds that a quantum trajectory
\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}
purifies
almost surely with respect to the probability measure of quantum trajectories,
unless
the following situation occurs:

(
\star
)

There exist an orthogonal projection
\hat{\mathcal{Q}}
with
\mathrm{rank}[\hat{\mathcal{Q}}]\geq 2
and
\zeta_{b}\>(\geq 0)
such that

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{Q}}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\mathcal{Q}}=\zeta_{b}\hat{\mathcal{Q}}

(256)

for all
b
.
In other words, if we consider the space projected by
\hat{\mathcal{Q}}
,
\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b}
acts as the identity (times
\zeta_{b}
). Note that
\hat{\mathcal{Q}}
should be independent of
b
.

The proof is given in Appendix
D
.

As a corollary of this result, we can say the following:
if
\mathrm{dim}[\mathcal{H}]=2
, a quantum trajectory
\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}
purifies almost surely, unless
\hat{M}_{b}
becomes proportional to unitary for all
b
.
We note that (
\star
) above is a necessary but not sufficient condition for the breakdown of purification
(see the discussion later)
56
56

56

Note that the statements that “(
\star
) is a necessary condition for the breakdown of purification for almost all trajectories” and “purification for almost all trajectories holds unless (
\star
) occurs” are equivalent (contraposition with each other).
.

While the above result is for the discrete-time case, a similar property can be discussed for quantum trajectories under continuous-time measurement
[
267
,
30
]
.
For example, for the case with the quantum jump process in Eq. (
73
), the purification of quantum trajectories occurs almost surely unless a similar condition with above (
\star
), where
\hat{M}_{b}
is replaced with
\hat{L}_{b}
, holds.

As a stronger version of the above statement, we can consider a two-step version (
n=2
) for Eq. (
254
) and obtain the following consequence:
a quantum trajectory
\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}
purifies
almost surely with respect to the probability measure of quantum trajectories,
unless
the following situation occurs:

(
\star\star
)

There exist an orthogonal projection
\hat{\mathcal{Q}}
with
\mathrm{rank}[\hat{\mathcal{Q}}]\geq 2
and
\zeta_{bb^{\prime}}\>(\geq 0)
such that

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{Q}}\hat{M}_{b^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b}\hat{M}_{b^{\prime}}\hat{\mathcal{Q}}=\zeta_{bb^{\prime}}\hat{\mathcal{Q}}

(257)

for all
b,b^{\prime}
. Note that
\hat{\mathcal{Q}}
should be independent of
{b}
and
b^{\prime}
.

Again, this is a necessary but not sufficient condition for the breakdown of purification.

5.2.2
Examples

Let us discuss some examples. In Sec.
5.1.2
, we already saw that
Examples 1
and
2
show purification for almost all trajectories, i.e., the time-evolving state approaches
\ket{0}\bra{0}
or
\ket{1}\bra{1}
.
For these examples, we find that
(\star\star)
does not hold true, whereas
(\star)
holds.

Let us especially revisit
Example 1.
, which is given in Eq. (
227
).
In this case, while purification occurs, we can find that
(\star)
holds.
Indeed, if we take
\hat{\mathcal{Q}}=\ket{2}\bra{2}+\ket{3}\bra{3}
with
\mathrm{rank}[\mathcal{\hat{Q}}]=2
, Eq. (
256
) holds with
\zeta_{0}=\zeta_{1}=0
and
\zeta_{2}=1
.
This fact illustrates that
(\star)
is not a sufficient condition for the breakdown of purification.
However, we also find that
(\star\star)
is not satisfied in this case;
if we consider
b=1
and
b^{\prime}=2
,
\hat{\mathcal{Q}}=\ket{2}\bra{2}+\ket{3}\bra{3}
no longer satisfies Eq. (
257
).
We can then conclude that no projector
\hat{\mathcal{Q}}
with
\mathrm{rank}[\hat{\mathcal{Q}}]\geq 2
exists, and therefore the purification for typical quantum trajectories is justified.
A similar discussion holds for
Example 2.

Next, we introduce additional two examples where the purification does not occur.

Example 4.

Let us again consider a four level system, where each level is denoted by
\ket{0},\ket{1},\ket{2},
and
\ket{3}
.
We assume that the measurement operators are given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{M}_{0}&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\ket{0}\bra{0}-\ket{1}\bra{1}),\\
\hat{M}_{1}&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\ket{0}\bra{0}+\ket{1}\bra{1}),\\
\hat{M}_{2}&=\ket{1}\bra{3}+\ket{0}\bra{2}.\end{split}

(258)

We can easily confirm that
\sum_{b}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b}=\hat{\mathbb{I}}
and that
\mathcal{E}
has multiple stationary states, namely

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}[c\ket{0}\bra{0}+(1-c)\ket{1}\bra{1}]=c\ket{0}\bra{0}+(1-c)\ket{1}\bra{1}

(259)

for arbitrary
c\in[0,1]
.

Let us consider quantum trajectories starting from an initial state

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{0}=\gamma\ket{+}\bra{+}+(1-\gamma)(\alpha\ket{2}\bra{2}+(1-\alpha)\ket{3}\bra{3}),

(260)

where
\gamma,\alpha\in[0,1]
and
\ket{\pm}=\frac{\ket{0}\pm\ket{1}}{\sqrt{2}}
.
The transition paths are illustrated in Fig.
11
.
After the first step, the state becomes
\ket{-}\bra{-}
,
\ket{+}\bra{+}
, and
{\alpha\ket{0}\bra{0}+(1-\alpha)\ket{1}\bra{1}}
for the outcomes
b_{1}=0,1,
and
2
, respectively.
The corresponding probabilities read
p_{0}=p_{1}=\gamma/2
and
p_{2}=1-\gamma
.
After that, we will measure either
b=0
or
b=1
with equal probabilities at each time step.
For the case with
b_{1}=0
or
1
, the measurement with
b=1
keeps the state invariant, while
b=0
changes
\ket{\pm}\bra{\pm}
into
\ket{\mp}\bra{\mp}
.
In this case,
\ket{\pm}\bra{\pm}
and
\ket{\mp}\bra{\mp}
appear with the same frequency in a quantum trajectory in the long run.
In contrast, for the case with
b_{1}=2
, the state is kept invariant irrespective of subsequent measurement outcomes
b=0
or
1
.
Therefore, we find
that the purification for almost all trajectories does not hold when
\alpha\neq 0,1
and
\gamma\neq 1
, i.e., the state for the case with
b_{1}=2
remains mixed.
Note that the absence of purification is understood from the fact that there exists the projection, e.g.,
\mathcal{Q}=\ket{0}\bra{0}+\ket{1}\bra{1}
, with which Eq. (
254
) holds, where
\zeta_{\bm{b}_{n}}=1/2^{n}
when
b_{l}\neq 2\;(1\leq l\leq n)
and
\zeta_{\bm{b}_{n}}=0
otherwise.

Moreover, we find that

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}}^{\infty}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{1}{2}(\ket{0}\bra{0}+\ket{1}\bra{1})&\quad\mathrm{Prob}:\gamma,\\
{\alpha\ket{0}\bra{0}+(1-\alpha)\ket{1}\bra{1}}&\quad\mathrm{Prob}:1-\gamma.\end{array}\right.

and

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{E}^{n}}[\hat{\rho}_{0}]=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{E}^{n}[\hat{\rho}_{0}]=\left(\frac{\gamma}{2}+(1-\gamma)\alpha\right)\ket{0}\bra{0}+\left(\frac{\gamma}{2}+(1-\gamma)(1-\alpha)\right){\ket{1}\bra{1},}

(263)

which means that
the ergodicity also breaks down due to multiple stationary states for
\mathcal{E}
.
In contrast, we can confirm (2) above, i.e.,
\mathcal{E}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}}^{\infty}]=\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}}^{\infty}
and

\mathbb{E}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}}^{\infty}]=\overline{\mathcal{E}^{n}}[\hat{\rho}_{0}]
.
We note that there is no peripheral spectrum in the CPTP map considered here, which is the reason why the long-time average can be removed in Eq. (
263
).

Figure 11:
Transitions of the state
\hat{\rho}_{0}
for
Example 4
. Trajectories transition

\ket{+}\bra{+}
and
\ket{-}\bra{-}
with equal probabilities for
b_{1}=0
and
1
. In contrast, the state becomes invariant from

\alpha\ket{0}\bra{0}+(1-\alpha)\ket{1}\bra{1}
after subsequent measurements for
b_{1}=2
. Then, the ergodicity and the purification for almost all trajectories do not hold true.

Example 5.

As a next example, let us consider the four-level system whose measurement operators read

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{M}_{0}&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\ket{0}\bra{0}-\ket{1}\bra{1}),\\
\hat{M}_{1}&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\ket{0}\bra{1}+\ket{1}\bra{0}),\\
\hat{M}_{2}&=\ket{1}\bra{3}+\ket{0}\bra{2}.\end{split}

(264)

In this case, we find that there is only one stationary state,

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}=\frac{1}{2}(\ket{0}\bra{0}+\ket{1}\bra{1}).

(265)

Figure 12:
Transition of the state
\hat{\rho}_{0}
for
Example 5
. Trajectories transition between

\ket{+}\bra{+}
and
\ket{-}\bra{-}
with equal probabilities for
b_{1}=0
and
1
. Likewise, they transition between

\alpha\ket{0}\bra{0}+(1-\alpha)\ket{1}\bra{1}
and
\alpha\ket{1}\bra{1}+(1-\alpha)\ket{0}\bra{0}
with equal probabilities for
b_{1}=2
. Then, the ergodicity holds true for almost all quantum trajectories, while the purification for
b_{1}=2
breaks down.

We again start from the same initial state

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{0}=\gamma\ket{+}\bra{+}+(1-\gamma)(\alpha\ket{2}\bra{2}+(1-\alpha)\ket{3}\bra{3}).

(266)

Then, after the first measurement, the state becomes
\ket{-}\bra{-}
,
\ket{+}\bra{+}
, and
{\alpha\ket{0}\bra{0}+(1-\alpha)\ket{1}\bra{1}}
for the outcomes
b_{1}=0,1,
and
2
, respectively.
The corresponding probabilities read
p_{0}=p_{1}=\gamma/2
and
p_{2}=1-\gamma
.
After that, we will measure either
b=0
or
b=1
with equal probabilities at each time step.
For the case with
b_{1}=0
or
1
, the measurement with
b=1
keeps the state invariant, while
b=0
changes
\ket{\pm}\bra{\pm}
into
\ket{\mp}\bra{\mp}
.
In this case,
\ket{\pm}\bra{\pm}
and
\ket{\mp}\bra{\mp}
appear with the same frequency in a quantum trajectory in the long run.
In contrast, for the case with
b_{1}=2
, the measurement with
b=0
keeps the state invariant, while
b=1
changes
\alpha\ket{0}\bra{0}+(1-\alpha)\ket{1}\bra{1}
into
\alpha\ket{1}\bra{1}+(1-\alpha)\ket{0}\bra{0}
and vice versa.
For this case,
\alpha\ket{0}\bra{0}+(1-\alpha)\ket{1}\bra{1}
and
\alpha\ket{1}\bra{1}+(1-\alpha)\ket{0}\bra{0}
appear with the same frequency in the long run.
Therefore, for both cases, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}}^{\infty}=\frac{1}{2}(\ket{0}\bra{0}+\ket{1}\bra{1})=\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}.

(267)

This means that, while the ergodicity holds true, the purification does not hold true for almost all trajectories when
\gamma\neq 1
and
\alpha\neq 0,1
, i.e., the state remains mixed for
b_{1}=2
(see Fig.
12
).
Again, the absence of purification is consistent with the existence of the projection
\mathcal{Q}=\ket{0}\bra{0}+\ket{1}\bra{1}
, with which Eq. (
254
)
holds.

As known from
Examples 1.-5.
, the ergodicity and purification of quantum trajectories are independent concepts in general.
However, we note that purification is often assumed to show the existence of invariant measure of quantum trajectories
[
27
]
, which is related to the ergodicity property of nonlinear quantities, as will be detailed in Sec.
6.2
.

5.2.3
Relation to the product of Kraus operators

Here, we discuss a relation between the purification of
\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}
and the matrix rank of
\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n}
on the basis of Ref.
[
27
]
.
We try to explain the relation in a physicist-friendly manner.
See Ref.
[
27
]
for a rigorous treatment.
We consider matrices depending on the sequence of measurement outcomes
\bm{b}
and perform the singular-value decomposition of it,

\displaystyle\hat{Y}_{\bm{b};n}=\frac{\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n}}{\sqrt{\mathrm{Tr}\left(\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n}^{\dagger}\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n}\right)}}=\sum_{i}\Lambda_{i,\bm{b};n}\ket{\Psi_{i,\bm{b};n}}\bra{\Phi_{i,\bm{b};n}},

(268)

where
\{\Lambda_{i,\bm{b};n}\}_{i}
are singular values of
\hat{Y}_{\bm{b};n}
arrayed as
\Lambda_{i,\bm{b};n}\geq\Lambda_{i+1,\bm{b};n}
.
The states
\ket{\Psi_{i,\bm{b};n}}
and
\ket{\Phi_{i,\bm{b};n}}
are eigenstates of
\hat{Y}_{\bm{b};n}\hat{Y}_{\bm{b};n}^{\dagger}
and
\hat{Y}_{\bm{b};n}^{\dagger}\hat{Y}_{\bm{b};n}
, respectively, corresponding to the
i
th eigenvalue
\Lambda_{i,\bm{b};n}^{2}
.
These states are orthonormalized as
\langle\Psi_{i,\bm{b};n}|\Psi_{j,\bm{b};n}\rangle=\langle\Phi_{i,\bm{b};n}|\Phi_{j,\bm{b};n}\rangle=\delta_{ij}
.

We can easily understand that, if
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{rank}\left(\hat{Y}_{\bm{b};n}\right)=1
, the purification of
\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}
occurs, owing to
\mathrm{rank}\left(\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}\right)=\mathrm{rank}\left(\hat{Y}_{\bm{b};n}\hat{\rho}_{0}\hat{Y}_{\bm{b};n}^{\dagger}\right)\leq\mathrm{rank}\left(\hat{Y}_{\bm{b};n}\right)
57
57

57

Note that
\mathrm{rank}(\hat{A}\hat{B})\leq\mathrm{rank}(\hat{A})
and
\mathrm{rank}(\hat{A}\hat{B})\leq\mathrm{rank}(\hat{B})
are always satisfied.
. In particular, if
\hat{Y}_{\bm{b};n}
typically exhibits
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{rank}\left(\hat{Y}_{\bm{b};n}\right)=1
, it is obvious that Eq. (
252
) is satisfied in typical trajectories.
To see the converse, i.e., the typical purification of
\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}
indicates
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{rank}\left(\hat{Y}_{\bm{b};n}\right)=1
for typical
\hat{Y}_{\bm{b};n}
, we consider the limiting behavior of a matrix,

\displaystyle\hat{Z}_{\bm{b};n}=\hat{Y}_{\bm{b};n}^{\dagger}\hat{Y}_{\bm{b};n}=\frac{\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n}^{\dagger}\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n}}{\mathrm{Tr}\left(\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n}^{\dagger}\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n}\right)}=\sum_{i}\Lambda_{i,\bm{b};n}^{2}\ket{\Phi_{i,\bm{b};n}}\bra{\Phi_{i,\bm{b};n}}.

(269)

If we take the initial state as
\hat{\rho}_{0}=\hat{\mathbb{I}}/d
, we can show that the function
\hat{Z}_{\bm{b};n}
is a martingale
[
260
]
.
That is, the expectation value of
\hat{Z}_{\bm{b};n+1}
under the condition that the measurement outcomes from
1
to
n
steps are
\bm{b}_{n}=(b_{1},b_{2},\cdots,b_{n})
becomes

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{I}/d}\left(\hat{Z}_{\bm{b};n+1}\middle|\bm{b}_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{b_{n+1}}\hat{Z}_{\bm{b};n+1}\mathrm{Tr}\left(\hat{M}_{b_{n+1}}\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}\hat{M}_{b_{n+1}}^{\dagger}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{b_{n+1}}\frac{\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b_{n+1}}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b_{n+1}}\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n}}{\mathrm{Tr}\left(\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n+1}^{\dagger}\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n+1}\right)}\frac{\mathrm{Tr}\left(\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n+1}\frac{\hat{\mathbb{I}}}{d}\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n+1}^{\dagger}\right)}{\mathrm{Tr}\left(\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n}\frac{\hat{\mathbb{I}}}{d}\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};n}^{\dagger}\right)}

\displaystyle=\hat{Z}_{\bm{b};n},

(270)

where
\sum_{b_{n+1}}\hat{M}_{b_{n+1}}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b_{n+1}}=\hat{\mathbb{I}}
is used.
Since
\hat{Z}_{\bm{b};n}
is a positive semidefinite matrix satisfying
\mathrm{Tr}\left(\hat{Z}_{\bm{b};n}\right)=1
, coordinates of
\hat{Z}_{\bm{b};n}
satisfy
58
58

58

For a positive semidefinite matrix
\hat{A}
,
|A_{ij}|=|\braket{i|\sqrt{\hat{A}}\sqrt{\hat{A}}|j}|\leq\sqrt{A_{ii}A_{jj}}
is satisfied.
Combining this with
A_{ii}\leq\sum_{i}A_{ii}=1
if
\mathrm{Tr}(\hat{A})=1
, we obtain
|A_{ij}|\leq 1
.

\displaystyle\left|\left[\hat{Z}_{\bm{b};n}\right]_{ij}\right|\leq 1.

(271)

Using Eqs. (
270
) and (
271
), we can apply the martingale convergence theorem
[
260
]
to each element of
\hat{Z}_{\bm{b};n}
.
Then, we find that the limit

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\hat{Z}_{\bm{b};n}=\hat{Z}_{\bm{b}}

(272)

exists, almost surely with respect to
\bm{b}
.
Equation (
272
) means that
\Lambda_{i,\bm{b};n}
and
\ket{\Phi_{i,\bm{b};n}}
asymptotically become independent of
n
, and thus
\hat{Y}_{\bm{b};n}
can be written as

\displaystyle\hat{Y}_{\bm{b};n}\simeq\sum_{i}\Lambda_{i,\bm{b}}\ket{\Psi_{i,\bm{b};n}}\bra{\Phi_{i,\bm{b}}}

(273)

in the long-time regime, where
\Lambda_{i,\bm{b}}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\Lambda_{i,\bm{b};n}
and
\bra{\Phi_{i,\bm{b}}}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\bra{\Phi_{i,\bm{b};n}}
59
59

59

When
\{\Lambda_{i,\bm{b}}\}_{i}
are degenerated, there is some ambiguity on how to determine
\{\ket{\Phi_{i,\bm{b}}}\}_{i}
. However, such ambiguity has no effect on the discussions below.
.
Therefore, if
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{rank}\left(\hat{Y}_{\bm{b};n}\right)=\tilde{d}\geq 2
and thus
\Lambda_{\tilde{d},\bm{b}}\neq 0
are satisfied in a set
\mathfrak{B}\subseteq\{\bm{b}\}
of measurement outcomes, the initially maximally mixed state
\hat{\rho}_{0}=\hat{\mathbb{I}}/d
does not purify.
Indeed, in this case, the matrix rank of
\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}\propto\hat{Y}_{\bm{b};n}\hat{\rho}_{0}\hat{Y}_{\bm{b};n}^{\dagger}=\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{d}}\Lambda_{i,\bm{b}}^{2}\ket{\Psi_{i,\bm{b};n}}\bra{\Psi_{i,\bm{b};n}}
is larger than one due to
\Lambda_{\tilde{d},\bm{b}}\neq 0
.

From Eq. (
272
), we can also understand that the breakdown of the purification is related to the existence of dark subspaces.
A space
\mathcal{D}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
of quantum pure states is referred to as the dark subspace if, for any
\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{D}
, there exist unitary operators
\{\hat{U}_{\bm{b};m}\}_{\bm{b},m}
such that

\displaystyle\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};m}\ket{\psi}\propto\hat{U}_{\bm{b};m}\ket{\psi}

(274)

is satisfied for arbitrary
\bm{b}
and
m
.
If there is a
\tilde{d}
-dimensional dark subspace with
\tilde{d}\geq 2
, Eq. (
254
) is satisfied with
\hat{\mathcal{Q}}=\hat{P}_{\mathcal{D}}
which is the projector onto the dark subspace.
To see the relation between the dark subspace and the breakdown of the purification, we compare
\hat{Z}_{\bm{b};n+m}
and
\hat{Z}_{\bm{b};n}
.
Since
\hat{Y}_{\bm{b};n+m}
satisfies
\hat{Y}_{\bm{b};n+m}\propto\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\vartheta^{n}\bm{b};m}\hat{Y}_{\bm{b};n}
, the former becomes

\displaystyle\hat{Z}_{\bm{b};n+m}=\hat{Y}_{\bm{b};n+m}^{\dagger}\hat{Y}_{\bm{b};n+m}

\displaystyle\propto\sum_{ij}\Lambda_{i,\bm{b}}\Lambda_{j,\bm{b}}\ket{\Phi_{i,\bm{b}}}\bra{\Psi_{i,\bm{b};n}}\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\vartheta^{n}\bm{b};m}^{\dagger}\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\vartheta^{n}\bm{b};m}\ket{\Psi_{j,\bm{b};n}}\bra{\Phi_{j,\bm{b}}},

(275)

with
\vartheta^{n}\bm{b}=(b_{n+1,},b_{n+2},\cdots)
[see also Eq. (
280
)].
Owing to Eq. (
272
),
\hat{Z}_{\bm{b};n+m}=\hat{Z}_{\bm{b};n}
should be satisfied for sufficiently large
n
.
Here, we consider the situation where
\Lambda_{\tilde{d},\bm{b}}\neq 0
is satisfied with
\tilde{d}\geq 2
and
\Lambda_{i>\tilde{d},\bm{b}}=0
. This means that the quantum trajectory corresponding to
\bm{b}
does not purify.
In this case, comparing Eqs. (
269
) and (
275
), we realize that

\displaystyle\bra{\Psi_{i,\bm{b};n}}\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\vartheta^{n}\bm{b};m}^{\dagger}\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\vartheta^{n}\bm{b};m}\ket{\Psi_{j,\bm{b};n}}\propto\delta_{ij}

(276)

should be satisfied for any
m
and
i,j\leq\tilde{d}
. This means that
\ket{\Psi_{i,\bm{b};n}}
resides in the
\tilde{d}
-dimensional dark subspace.
