\Delta
is the spectral gap introduced in Eq. (
336
).

We consider
\tau_{\mathrm{RC}}=\infty
in the following discussion to avoid the divergence of the second Lyapunov exponent
\varepsilon_{2}
, which would lead to an ill-defined purification time.

Let us now consider the following non-local quantum circuit on a system of
L
qubits.
At each timestep, a global Haar-random unitary
\hat{U}\in U(2^{L})
is applied, followed by projective measurements at exactly
pL
sites chosen randomly.
This measurement projects the system onto a subspace of dimension
2^{(1-p)L}
.
Due to the strong scrambling effect of the global unitaries, this model is always in a mixed phase and does not exhibit a measurement-induced transition.
The rank of the state is fixed to be
r_{\bm{b};t}=2^{(1-p)L}
for all
t\geq 1
and almost all unitaries, meaning that the rank collapse time is infinite,
\tau_{\mathrm{RC}}=\infty
.
This allows us to safely study the purification time
\tau_{\mathrm{P}}
.

We can show that
\tau_{\mathrm{P}}
is exponentially long in the system size using RMT.
A key insight is that, due to the Haar randomness of the unitaries, the singular value statistics of the process becomes independent of the specific measurement outcomes.
We can therefore analyze the process for a fixed sequence of projections, effectively bypassing the Born rule average.
The Kraus operators for a
t
-step evolution become equivalent to a product of
t
random matrices:

\displaystyle\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{t}\sim(\hat{P}\hat{U}_{t})(\hat{P}\hat{U}_{t-1})\cdots(\hat{P}\hat{U}_{1}),

(372)

where
\hat{P}
is a fixed projector onto a
2^{(1-p)L}
-dimensional subspace and each
\hat{U}_{t}
is an independent Haar-random unitary.
The Lyapunov exponents
\varepsilon_{j}
for such a product of random matrices are known from RMT:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{j}=-\frac{1}{2}[\psi(2^{L}-j+1)-\psi(2^{(1-p)L}-j+1)],

(373)

where
\psi(x)
is the digamma function
72
72

72

The digamma function is the logarithmic derivative of the gamma function
\Gamma(x)
:
\psi(x)=\frac{d}{dx}\ln\Gamma(x)=\Gamma(x)^{\prime}/\Gamma(x)
.
.
Using this exact result, we can calculate the purification time:

\displaystyle\tau_{\mathrm{P}}=\frac{1}{2(\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{1})}=\left(\frac{1}{2^{(1-p)L}-1}-\frac{1}{2^{L}-1}\right)^{-1}

(374)

For large
L
, this gives a purification time that is exponentially long in the system size
\tau_{\mathrm{P}}\sim 2^{(1-p)L}
.

The RMT analysis can be extended to a similar non-local model, but with measurement layers comprising weak measurements.
A single-qubit weak measurement operator can be written as
\hat{M}_{l,\pm}=(\hat{\mathbb{I}}\pm\eta\hat{\sigma}^{z}_{l})/\sqrt{2(1+\eta^{2})}
, where
0\leq\eta\leq 1
quantifies the measurement strength.
This ensures
\tau_{\mathrm{RC}}=\infty
unless
\eta=1
and
p=1
.
The Kraus operators corresponding to the whole time evolution
\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};t}
73
73

73

The Kraus operator for one-step evolution is given as explained in the footnote
67
.
can be analyzed using similar techniques as the projective case.
Note that since the randomness of unitaries and measurements included in
\bm{b}
does not affect the singular value statistics of the Kraus operators, we drop the explicit
\bm{b}
-dependence for simplicity.

To analyze the singular value statistics, we focus on the evolution of
\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{t}=2^{2pLt}\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{t}\hat{\mathsf{M}}^{\dagger}_{t}
and its eigenvalues
x_{j}(t)
.
Under an assumption of
pL\eta^{2}\ll 1
, by performing a perturbative expansion in the measurement strength
\eta
and averaging over the Haar-random unitaries, one obtains a Langevin equation for variables
w_{j}(t)=(\ln x_{j}(t)+\Gamma Nt/4)/2
with
N=2^{L}
.
This equation describes the motion of
w_{j}(t)
as being driven by random noise while also repelling each other:

\displaystyle w_{j}(t+1)-w_{j}(t)=\frac{1}{2}\xi_{j}(t)+\frac{\Gamma}{4}\sum_{i\neq j}\coth(w_{j}(t)-w_{i}(t)),

(375)

where
\xi_{j}(t)
is a Gaussian white noise with variance
\langle\xi_{i}(t_{1})\xi_{j}(t_{2})\rangle=\Gamma\delta_{ij}\delta_{t_{1}t_{2}}
and the parameter
\Gamma=e^{-\mathcal{O}(L)}
for
L\gg 1
sets the strength of both noise and repulsive interaction.

In the continuous time limit of the Langevin equation, we reach a Fokker-Planck equation of the joint probability distribution
P(\vec{w},s)
of the variables
\vec{w}(t)=(w_{1}(t),w_{2}(t),\ldots)
at time
s=\Gamma t/8
:

\displaystyle\partial_{s}P(\vec{w},s)=\sum_{j=1}^{N}(-\partial_{w_{j}}(D_{j}(\vec{w})P(\vec{w},s))+\partial_{w_{j}}^{2}P(\vec{w},s)),

(376)

where the drift term is
D_{j}(\vec{w})=2\sum_{i(\neq j)}\coth(w_{j}-w_{i})
.
This equation is a direct analogue of the Dorokhov-Mello-Pereyra-Kumar equation, which describes universal conductance fluctuations in disordered mesoscopic wires
[
291
,
292
]
.

Remarkably, this Fokker-Planck equation is exactly solvable due to its connection to Calogero-Sutherland models
[
293
,
294
,
273
]
.
The explicit solution for
P(\vec{w},s)
is known, allowing for a complete, analytical description of the purification dynamics.
In particular, at late times
t\gg\Gamma^{-1}
, the Lyapunov exponents are given by

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{j}=-pL\ln 2+\frac{\Gamma}{8}(2^{L}+2-4j),

(377)

from which we can read off the purification time as

\displaystyle\tau_{\mathrm{P}}=\Gamma^{-1}=e^{\mathcal{O}(L)}

(378)

for this model.

Universal fluctuations in the mixed phase of fermionic Gaussian systems

In Ref.
[
295
]
, the Fokker-Planck equation for the joint probability distribution of Lyapunov exponents was derived for monitored quantum dynamics of fermionic Gaussian states

74
74

74

“Gaussian states” refer to states whose density matrix can be written as the exponential of a quadratic form of fermionic operators, so that all higher-order correlations factorize according to Wick’s theorem.
See Ref.
[
296
]
for a general review and Ref.
[
297
]
for measurements that preserve the Gaussianity.

.
They obtained exact solutions for the Fokker-Planck equation for the mixed phase, which leads to a universal entropy fluctuation.

The system consists of fermions on
L
sites and starts from the maximally mixed state.
The state is evolved by a unitary operator
\hat{U}_{t}
, which is generated by a time-dependent random Hamiltonian quadratic in the fermion operators.
Meanwhile, the particle number
\hat{n}_{l}=\hat{c}_{l}^{\dagger}\hat{c}_{l}
at each site
l
is continuously measured where
\hat{c}_{l}
(
\hat{c}_{l}^{\dagger}
) are fermionic annihilation (creation) operators.
This measurement is described by

\hat{P}_{t}=\exp[\sum_{l}\{(\hat{n}_{l}-\langle\hat{n}_{l}\rangle_{c;t})\sqrt{\gamma}dW_{t,l}-(\hat{n}_{l}-\langle\hat{n}_{l}\rangle_{c;t})^{2}\gamma dt\}]

where
\gamma
is the measurement strength and
dW_{t,l}
is the standard Wiener process (see also Sec.
3.5
).

We now discretize the time as
t=N\Delta t
so that the unitary dynamics
\hat{U}_{n\Delta t}
occurs in the interval
((n-1)\Delta t,n\Delta t)
and the measurement
\hat{P}_{n\Delta t}
is applied at time
n\Delta t
.
Due to Gaussianity, the information of the state is fully encoded in a single-particle matrix
\mathsf{M}_{\bm{b};t}=P_{t}U_{t}\cdots P_{\Delta t}U_{\Delta t}
where
P_{t}
and
U_{t}
satisfy
\hat{P}_{t}\hat{c}_{l}^{\dagger}\hat{P}_{t}^{-1}=\sum_{m}(P_{t})_{lm}\hat{c}_{m}^{\dagger}
and
\hat{U}^{\dagger}_{t}\hat{c}_{l}^{\dagger}\hat{U}_{t}=\sum_{m}(U_{t})_{lm}\hat{c}_{m}^{\dagger}
, respectively.
Here, the symbols without hats denote the corresponding single-particle matrices, not the many-body operators.
For this dynamics, we construct the effective single-particle Hamiltonian as
H_{\bm{b};t}=-\ln(\mathsf{M}_{\bm{b};t}\mathsf{M}^{\dagger}_{\bm{b};t})/2t
.
The snapshot single-particle Lyapunov exponents
z_{l,\bm{b};t}
, which are the eigenvalues of
H_{\bm{b};t}
, determine the mixed-state entropy, but we here consider a variable
\zeta_{l,\bm{b};t}=-z_{l,\bm{b};t}t
instead.

For a simple analytical treatment, the single-particle unitary matrix
U_{t}
is modeled as a Haar random
U(L)
matrix.
We consider the dynamics in an infinitesimal interval
(t,t+\Delta t)
that renormalizes the probability distribution function
p(\vec{\zeta}_{\bm{b};t},t)
.
Performing a perturbative analysis, we find that the Fokker-Planck equation for
p(\vec{\zeta}_{\bm{b};t},t)
is derived as

\displaystyle\frac{L+1}{\gamma}\frac{\partial p(\vec{\zeta}_{\bm{b};t},t)}{\partial t}=-\sum_{l=1}^{L}\frac{\partial[(\mu_{l,\bm{b};t}+\nu_{l,\bm{b};t})p(\vec{\zeta}_{\bm{b};t},t)]}{\partial\zeta_{l,\bm{b};t}}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{l,m=1}^{L}\frac{\partial^{2}[(1+\delta_{lm})p(\vec{\zeta}_{\bm{b};t},t)]}{\partial\zeta_{l,\bm{b};t}\partial\zeta_{m,\bm{b};t}},

(379)

where
\mu_{l,\bm{b};t}=\sum_{m\neq l}\coth(\zeta_{l,\bm{b};t}-\zeta_{m,\bm{b};t})
and
\nu_{l,\bm{b};t}=\sum_{m}(1+\delta_{lm})\tanh\zeta_{m,\bm{b};t}
.
In order to find its solution, we first need to consider the case of “forced measurements” where the probability to obtain a measurement outcome is independent of the pre-measurement state so that
\nu_{l,\bm{b};t}=0
.
In this case, we find the exact solution to the Fokker-Plank equation as

\displaystyle p_{F}(\vec{\zeta}_{\bm{b};t},t)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(t)\left(\prod_{l<m}(\zeta_{l,\bm{b};t}-\zeta_{m,\bm{b};t})\sinh(\zeta_{l,\bm{b};t}-\zeta_{m,\bm{b};t})\right)

\displaystyle\times\exp\left(-\frac{L+1}{2\gamma t}\sum_{l,m}\zeta_{l,\bm{b};t}\left(-\frac{1}{L+1}+\delta_{lm}\right)\zeta_{m,\bm{b};t}\right),

(380)

where
\mathcal{N}(t)
is a normalization constant.
Next, we consider the case where the measurement outcomes obey the Born rule.
Using the fact that the Born probability for the trajectory
\bm{b}_{t}
to be realized is proportional to
\prod_{l}\cosh\zeta_{l,\bm{b};t}

[
298
]
, we can show that the Fokker-Planck equation (
379
) has the exact solution,

\displaystyle p_{B}(\vec{\zeta}_{\bm{b};t},t)=e^{-L\gamma t/2}\left(\prod_{l}\cosh\zeta_{l,\bm{b};t}\right)p_{F}(\vec{\zeta}_{\bm{b};t},t).

(381)

Next, we consider the Rényi mixed-state entropy.
For free fermion systems, it is decomposed into a sum
S_{\alpha}(\bm{b};t)=\sum_{l=1}^{L}f_{\alpha}(\zeta_{l,\bm{b};t})
with

\displaystyle f_{\alpha}(\zeta)=\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\ln\left[\frac{1}{(1+e^{2\zeta})^{\alpha}}+\frac{1}{(1+e^{-2\zeta})^{\alpha}}\right].

(382)

Using the exact solution to the Fokker-Planck equation, we can uncover the universal behavior of the entropy in the large-
L
and short-
t
limit
1\ll\gamma t\ll L
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[S_{\alpha}(\bm{b};\!t)]\simeq\frac{L}{2\gamma t}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f_{\alpha}(\zeta)d\zeta=\frac{\pi^{2}L}{24\gamma t}\left(1+\frac{1}{\alpha}\right),

(383)

and the universal entropy fluctuations,

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}[S_{\alpha}(\bm{b};\!t)]\equiv\mathbb{E}[(S_{\alpha}(\bm{b};\!t))^{2}]-\mathbb{E}[(S_{\alpha}(\bm{b};\!t))]^{2}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dq\frac{|q|(1-e^{-\pi|q|})}{4\pi^{2}}\left(\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f_{\alpha}(\zeta)e^{-iq\zeta}d\zeta\right)^{2}.

(384)

Specifically, we have
\mathbb{V}[S_{2}(\bm{b};\!t)]=10\ln 2-6\ln\pi
, which was numerically confirmed to appear in other settings, such as those where the unitary evolution is generated by a local Hamiltonian, and those where the measurements are replaced by projective ones
[
295
]
.
Reference
[
295
]
also argued that the saturating value of the entropy fluctuation is independent of microscopic details, but depends only on fundamental symmetries of the system.

7.3
Lyapunov spectrum and measurement-induced transitions

While the previous section focused on analytically tractable models where the Lyapunov spectrum or the mixed-state entropy can be precisely treated, we now shift our focus to the connection between the Lyapunov spectrum and measurement-induced phase transitions, whose analytical treatment remains challenging, primarily through numerical investigations.

This section will explore this connection by addressing two central questions:
(1) How can the universal data of the MIPT be extracted from the Lyapunov spectrum?
(2) How does the qualitative behavior of the Lyapunov spectrum serve as a distinct signature for the measurement-induced phases, and how does this behavior change across the transition?
By addressing these questions, we aim to demonstrate how the Lyapunov spectrum acts as a powerful tool to probe the measurement-induced phases and the underlying universality class.

7.3.1
Extracting critical data from the Lyapunov spectrum

The universal properties of an MIPT at its critical point can be revealed by numerically analyzing the Lyapunov spectrum
[
269
,
272
,
271
,
270
]
.
We here particularly focus on its application to the (1+1)-dimensional monitored RQC under the periodic boundary conditions in the spatial direction, as depicted in Fig.
14
(a).

For an initial state
\hat{\rho}_{0}
, the probability to obtain a trajectory labeled by
\bm{b}_{t}
is given by
p_{\bm{b};t}=\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};t}\hat{\rho}_{0}\hat{\mathsf{M}}^{\dagger}_{\bm{b};t}]
.
Using the singular value decomposition of
\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};t}
in Eq. (
337
), this can be written as

\displaystyle p_{\bm{b};t}=\sum_{i}e^{-2\varepsilon_{i,\bm{b};t}t}\langle\Phi_{i,\bm{b};t}|\hat{\rho}_{0}|\Phi_{i,\bm{b};t}\rangle

(385)

where
\varepsilon_{i,\bm{b};t}
are the Lyapunov exponents and
\ket{\Phi_{i,\bm{b};n}}
are the corresponding right singular vectors.
As discussed in Sec.
6.3
, under the assumption that both irreducibility and purification conditions hold for the CPTP map associated with the monitored RQC, it is dominated by the leading Lyapunov exponent at late times,

\displaystyle p_{\bm{b};t}\simeq e^{-2\varepsilon_{1,\bm{b};t}t}\langle\Phi_{1,\bm{b};t}|\hat{\rho}_{0}|\Phi_{1,\bm{b};t}\rangle.

(386)

The core idea of Ref.
[
269
]
is to identify each trajectory as a two-dimensional classical statistical mechanics model, which is defined through the “partition function”
Z_{\bm{b};t}\equiv p_{\bm{b};t}
75
75

75

Writing the full circuit evolution as
\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};t}=\hat{M}_{b_{t}}\cdots\hat{M}_{b_{2}}\hat{M}_{b_{1}}
, each time slice
\hat{M}_{b_{n}}
may be viewed as a transfer matrix for a two-dimensional statistical mechanics model with quenched randomness drawn from
\{\bm{b}\}
.
The Born probability
p_{\bm{b};t}=\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};t}\hat{\rho}_{0}\hat{\mathsf{M}}^{\dagger}_{\bm{b};t}]
represents two layers of transfer matrix evolution, one with forward evolution by
\hat{M}_{b_{n}}
and another with backward evolution by
\hat{M}_{b_{n}}^{\dagger}
, which are glued at one side by
\hat{\rho}_{0}
while summed over all possible states at the other side.
Such objects are regarded as the partition functions of statistical mechanics models in the literature (see, e.g., Ref.
[
299
]
).
One may also view
p_{\bm{b};t}
as a partition function in the Keldysh formalism
[
300
]
, although the evolution does not preserve the trace of
\hat{\rho}_{0}
.

.
The free energy of this classical model is given by

\displaystyle F_{\bm{b};t}=-\ln Z_{\bm{b};t}\simeq 2\varepsilon_{1,\bm{b};t}t-\ln\langle\Phi_{1,\bm{b};t}|\hat{\rho}_{0}|\Phi_{1,\bm{b};t}\rangle.

(387)

As discussed in Sec.
5.2.3
, since the right singular vectors
\ket{\Phi_{i,\bm{b};t}}
asymptotically become
t
-independent, one can safely neglect the second term of Eq. (
387
) for a sufficiently long time.
Averaging the free energy
F_{\bm{b};t}
over the trajectories yields,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[F_{\bm{b};t}]=\sum_{\bm{b}_{t}}p_{\bm{b};t}F_{\bm{b};t}=-\sum_{\bm{b}_{t}}p_{\bm{b};t}\ln p_{\bm{b};t},

(388)

which is nothing but the Shannon entropy of the trajectories and behaves as
\mathbb{E}[F_{\bm{b};t}]\simeq 2\sum_{\bm{b}_{t}}p_{\bm{b};t}\varepsilon_{1,\bm{b};t}t
at late times.

According to CFT, the free energy density
f_{1}(L)\equiv\mathbb{E}[F_{\bm{b};t}]/A
with an effective spacetime area
A=\alpha Lt
, where
\alpha
is an anisotropy parameter
76
76

76

The anisotropy parameter
\alpha
in the effective area
A
relates the scales of space and time at the critical point.
It can be determined by finding the time
t_{*}
at which spatial and temporal correlations become equal, which leads to the relation
\alpha=(L/\pi t_{*})\ln(1+\sqrt{2})
.
A numerical procedure to determine
\alpha
using the mutual information was proposed in Refs.
[
269
,
272
]
.

,
is predicted to obey the following finite-size scaling form at the critical point,

\displaystyle f_{1}(L)=f_{1}(L=\infty)-\frac{\pi c_{\mathrm{eff}}}{6L^{2}}+\cdots.

(389)

Here,
c_{\mathrm{eff}}
is a universal number called the effective central charge.
We can also consider generalizations of the free energy density to the higher Lyapunov exponents
\varepsilon_{i,\bm{b};t}
with
i\geq 2
, which are given by
f_{i}(L)\equiv 2\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{i,\bm{b};t}]/(\alpha L)
.
These free energy densities are expected to obey the finite-size scaling form,

\displaystyle f_{i}(L)-f_{1}(L)=\frac{2\pi x_{i}^{\mathrm{typ}}}{L^{2}},

(390)

from which we can extract the scaling dimensions
x_{i}^{\mathrm{typ}}
of operators in the underlying CFT.

We end this section with several caveats.
When the CPTP map corresponding to the monitored RQC obeys the irreducibility condition, we do not actually need the average over the trajectories to compute the free energy densities; as discussed in Sec.
6.3.1
, the Lyapunov exponents converge to values independent of
\bm{b}
for a typical trajectory.
However, if we consider a finite-size system of length
L
subject to projective measurements at each site with probability
p
, which is a common setup for the MIPT, there is always the possibility that the Kraus operator
\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};t}
becomes rank one during the evolution and all higher Lyapunov exponents
\varepsilon_{i,\bm{b};t}
with
i\geq 2
diverge thereafter [see also the discussion around Eq. (
370
)].
A typical situation is that all sites are measured in a single time step, which happens with probability
p^{L}
.
Although such events may only occur with probability exponentially small in
L
, they can be obstacles in computing higher Lyapunov exponents, as they often require a long convergence time.
In order to successfully perform their finite-size scaling analysis as described above, the higher Lyapunov exponents must converge in a time long enough but much shorter than
e^{\mathcal{O}(L)}
.
For these cases, the average over the trajectories may help to improve the convergence.
As we will see in the next section, we need not be worried about the timescale for the convergence when we consider weak measurements.

7.3.2
Spectral behavior in measurement-induced phases and transitions

The Lyapunov spectrum probes not only the universal properties of the MIPT, but also the phases themselves.
Reference
[
274
]
studied the behaviors of the Lyapunov spectrum within the measurement-induced phases for a
(1+1)
-dimensional RQC with local Haar unitary gates and generalized measurements of local qubits, as depicted in Fig.
18
(a).
The Kraus operator corresponding to the single-site generalized measurement with an outcome
b=\pm 1
is given by
\hat{M}_{l,b}=(\hat{\mathbb{I}}+b\eta\hat{\sigma}^{z}_{l})/\sqrt{2(1+\eta^{2})}
, where the parameter
0\leq\eta\leq 1
tunes the strength of the measurement.
The Kraus operator representing the whole monitored circuit is denoted by
\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};t}
77
77

77

\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};t}
is constructed as in the footnote
67
with
p=1
.

, where the
\eta
-dependence is omitted for ease of notation.
We write the singular value decomposition of the Kraus operator as
\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};t}=\sum_{i=1}^{2^{L}}\Lambda_{i,\bm{b};t}|\Psi_{i,\bm{b};t}\rangle\langle\Phi_{i,\bm{b};t}|
, where
\Lambda_{i,\bm{b};t}
are the singular values arranged as
\Lambda_{1,\bm{b};t}\geq\Lambda_{2,\bm{b};t}\geq\cdots\geq 0
and
\ket{\Psi_{i,\bm{b};t}}
and
\ket{\Phi_{i,\bm{b};t}}
are the corresponding left and right singular vectors, respectively [see also Eq. (
337
)].

In the long-time limit, an initial state
\ket{\psi_{0}}
converges to the left singular vector corresponding to the leading singular value
\Lambda_{1,\bm{b};t}
,
\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};t}\ket{\psi_{0}}\sim\Lambda_{1,\bm{b};t}\ket{\Psi_{1,\bm{b};t}}
, if we assume
\Lambda_{1,\bm{b};t}>\Lambda_{2,\bm{b};t}
78
78

78

We can prove that this monitored circuit satisfies the purification condition discussed in Sec.
5.2
unless
\eta=0
, so that
\Lambda_{1,\bm{b};t}>\Lambda_{2,\bm{b};t}
holds for
t\to\infty
according to the results in Sec.
6.3.2
.
If we decompose the full Kraus operator as
\hat{\sf{M}}_{\bm{b};t}=\hat{M}_{b_{t}}\cdots\hat{M}_{b_{2}}\hat{M}_{b_{1}}
, each time slice
\{\hat{M}_{b_{i}}\}_{b_{i}}
, which consists of one layer of random two-site unitary gates
\hat{U}_{l,l+1}
and another layer of generalized measurements
\hat{M}_{l,b}
, is also a set of Kraus operators.
By successively taking the sums over the measurement outcomes at later times, one can reduce condition (
254
) for
\hat{\sf{M}}_{\bm{b};t}
to that for the first time slice
\hat{M}_{b_{1}}
.
Now, the time slice
\hat{M}_{b_{1}}
is a tensor product of
\hat{X}_{b,b^{\prime},U}=\hat{M}_{1,b}\hat{M}_{2,b^{\prime}}\hat{U}_{1,2}
.
For any
\hat{U}_{1,2}
,
\hat{X}^{\dagger}_{b,b^{\prime},U}\hat{X}_{b,b^{\prime},U}
can be easily diagonalized, and their eigenvalues are given by
(1\pm b\eta)^{2}(1\pm b^{\prime}\eta)^{2}/(2(1+\eta^{2}))^{2}
, with
b,b^{\prime}\in\{+1,-1\}
.
While each
\hat{M}_{b_{1}}
has degenerate eigenvalues, the set of
\hat{M}_{b_{1}}
over all possible measurement outcomes has no common eigenspace with dimension greater than one for
0<\eta\leq 1
.
This implies that an orthogonal projection operator
\hat{\mathcal{Q}}
that satisfies
\hat{\mathcal{Q}}\hat{M}_{b_{1}}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b_{1}}\hat{\mathcal{Q}}=\zeta_{b_{1}}\hat{\mathcal{Q}}
with some
\zeta_{b_{1}}\geq 0
for almost all
b_{1}
cannot have
\mathrm{rank}[\hat{\mathcal{Q}}]\geq 2
.
A similar argument also applies to the monitored Kitaev-Majorana circuit discussed in Sec.
7.4
.

.
The long-time state
\ket{\Psi_{1,\bm{b};t}}
can be regarded as the “ground state” of the effective Hamiltonian

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{\bm{b};t}=-\frac{1}{2t}\ln\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};t}\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};t}^{\dagger}

(391)

introduced in Eq. (
319
), with the ground state energy
\varepsilon_{1,\bm{b};t}=-\ln(\Lambda_{1,\bm{b};t})/t
, which is nothing but the first Lyapunov exponent.
We note that these monitored systems are irreducible, i.e., (3) of Theorem
2
is satisfied
[
277
]
for each Haar and Clifford case.
Therefore, the Lyapunov exponents are independent of
\bm{b}
and
\ket{\psi_{0}}
, as discussed in Sec.
6.3
.

Figure 18:
(a) A monitored circuit with generalized measurements.
The two-site Haar random unitary gates are arranged in a brickwork manner.
The generalized measurements with strength
\eta
are applied at all sites followed by a unitary layer.
(b) Schematic of the long-time values of the first Lyapunov gap
\Delta(L\to\infty)
against the measurement strength
\eta
.
The location of the purification transition is denoted by
\eta_{c}
.
The figures were created based on Ref.
[
274
]
.

Since the rank of
\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};t}
never becomes one except for the projective case
\eta=1
, the higher Lyapunov exponents
\varepsilon_{i,\bm{b};t}=-\ln(\Lambda_{i,\bm{b};t})/t
can be safely computed by the numerical procedure in Sec.
6.3.4
.
Since the Lyapunov spectrum converges to values independent of the trajectory
\bm{b}
for sufficiently long times almost surely, they can, in practice, be computed by running the monitored circuit only once for each
\eta
.
Hence, we drop the subscript
\bm{b};t
from
\varepsilon_{i,\bm{b};t}
hereafter and focus on the converged values of the Lyapunov exponents
\varepsilon_{1}(L)\leq\varepsilon_{2}(L)\leq\cdots
for finite-size systems of length
L
.

Our main focus is the first Lyapunov gap,
\Delta(L)=\varepsilon_{2}(L)-\varepsilon_{1}(L)
.
From the numerical results, the Lyapunov gap shows a transition from a gapless phase where the gap exponentially closes in the system size
L
,
\Delta(L)=\exp[-\mathcal{O}(L)]
, to a gapped phase where the gap becomes almost independent of
L
,
\Delta(L)=\mathcal{O}(L^{0})
as
\eta
increases:

\displaystyle\Delta(L)=\begin{cases}e^{-\mathcal{O}(L)}&(\eta<\eta_{c})\\
\mathcal{O}(L^{0})&(\eta>\eta_{c})\end{cases}.

(392)

The behavior of
\Delta(L\to\infty)
is schematically shown in Fig.
18
(b), and it exhibits the gap closing/opening transition.
In the gapless phase with
\eta<\eta_{c}
, the low-lying spectrum also exhibits the exponential decay
\varepsilon_{i}(L)-\varepsilon_{1}(L)=\exp[-\mathcal{O}(L)]
, which was numerically confirmed up to
i=10
.
Regarding the entire spectrum, it was analytically shown that the spectral width always exhibits the bound
\varepsilon_{2^{L}}(L)-\varepsilon_{1}(L)\leq\mathcal{O}(L)
, which means that level spacings
\varepsilon_{i+1}(L)-\varepsilon_{i}(L)
are exponentially narrow with respect to
L
for almost all
i

[
274
]
.

Notably, this spectral transition in fact captures the purification transition.
Remember that different initial states typically yield the same long-time Lyapunov spectrum, owing to the irreducibility.
This means that the first Lyapunov gap is
\Delta(L)
even when the initial state is the maximally mixed state

\hat{\rho}_{0}=\hat{\mathbb{I}}/2^{L}
.
In this case, the gap
\Delta(L)
gives the purification time by
\tau_{\mathrm{P}}^{-1}(L)=2\Delta(L)
as in Eq. (
371
).
Then, the spectral transition can be regarded as the transition of the purification time
\tau_{\mathrm{P}}(L)
between a mixed phase with
\tau_{\mathrm{P}}(L)=\exp[\mathcal{O}(L)]
and a purified phase with
\tau_{\mathrm{P}}(L)=\mathcal{O}(L^{0})
.
Numerically, the location of the spectral transition turns out to be almost the same as that of the entanglement transition, which is consistent with the conjecture that the entanglement transition is the same as the purification transition in the general monitored RQCs.
As pointed out in Ref.
[
274
]
, the coincidence between the entanglement and the spectral transition is analogous to what has been found at the ground-state quantum phase transition in equilibrium systems
[
301
,
302
]
79
79

79

In the ground state of a Hamiltonian with short-range interactions in one dimension, a gapped phase exhibits a finite energy gap
\Delta(L)=\mathcal{O}(L^{0})
and area-law entanglement
S_{X}=\mathcal{O}(|X|^{0})
, whereas a gapless phase typically exhibits a polynomially decaying energy gap
\Delta(L)=\mathcal{O}[1/\mathrm{poly}(L)]
and logarithmic violation of the area-law entanglement
S_{X}=\mathcal{O}(\ln|X|)
.
The latter should be contrasted with the gapless phase in the monitored systems where
\Delta(L)=\exp[-\mathcal{O}(L)]
and
S_{X}=\mathcal{O}(|X|)
.
This difference might stem from possible long-range interactions and inhomogeneity in the effective Hamiltonian defined through Eq. (
391
).

.
This coincidence was confirmed even when the unitary gates may possess spacetime translation symmetries
[
277
]
.

7.4
Measurement-induced topology

Topology is one of the most fundamental concepts for characterizing stable phases of matter
[
303
]
.
For local Hamiltonians with a finite excitation gap, ground states belonging to different topological phases cannot be adiabatically deformed into one another without closing the gap, a distinction maintained by their discrete topological invariants
[
304
]
.

A key manifestation of this occurs in symmetry-protected topological (SPT) phases
[
305
,
306
,
307
,
308
,
309
,
310
]
, where non-trivial bulk topology guarantees the presence of gapless boundary states.
These states are robust against any perturbations that preserve the system’s symmetry as long as the bulk gap is open.
This phenomenon, known as the bulk-edge correspondence, profoundly influences the physical properties of topological phases and has been intensively researched over the past decades
[
311
,
312
,
313
,
314
,
315
]
.

Despite significant advancements in equilibrium systems, the role of topology in out-of-equilibrium systems driven by environmental interactions remains not fully understood.
Specifically, how topology manifests itself in monitored quantum systems is still elusive.

Concerning this issue, previous studies have investigated measurement-induced topological phase transitions separating trivial and topological area-law phases, for example, in spin systems with
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry
[
316
,
317
,
318
,
319
]
and in fermion systems with particle-hole symmetry
[
320
,
321
,
322
,
323
,
324
,
325
,
326
,
327
,
275
,
328
,
276
]
.
For one-dimensional monitored systems under open boundary conditions (OBC), the existence of a topological phase can be probed by the topological entanglement entropy or the purification dynamics; the latter can be characterized by the exponentially-long-time survival of the entanglement entropy of an ancilla system initially entangled with the main system.
They capture the quantum information encoded in the edge states, if any, that are protected by the bulk topology.

Figure 19:
(a) A weakly monitored Kitaev Majorana circuit.
The purple (green) two-site gate represents the generalized measurement of the parity of the neighboring Majorana pair on an odd (even) bond.
The orange gate is the unitary evolution by a local Hamiltonian that preserves particle-hole symmetry, say, the unitary evolution by the Kitaev chain Hamiltonian.
We switch off the unitary evolution when we consider the measurement-only circuit.
(b) The single-particle Lyapunov exponents
z_{l}
for the same circuit with unitary evolution by the Kitaev chain Hamiltonian under the OBC.
The red lines highlight the two middle exponents
z_{L}
and
z_{L+1}
, and the gray bands correspond to other exponents.
The locations of the transition from a topological area-law phase to an intermediate subvolume-law phase and the one from the intermediate phase to a trivial area-law phase are marked by
\eta_{\mathrm{topo}}
and
\eta_{\mathrm{triv}}
, respectively.
The inset depicts the squared amplitude of the averaged single-particle wave function associated with the zero modes
z_{L}
and
z_{L+1}
in the topological phase, showing their localization at edges.
(c) The bulk dynamical topological invariant at
t=\mathcal{O}(L)
averaged over trajectories for sufficiently large
L
.
The figures were created based on Ref.
[
276
]
.

While the above signatures, e.g., topological entanglement entropy, are suggestive, these are not responsible for the underlying symmetry protection of the topological phase.
Reference
[
276
]
addressed this issue.
It considered a weakly monitored variant of the Kitaev-Majorana chain as shown in Fig.
19
(a).
The circuit is defined on a one-dimensional chain of
2L
Majorana fermions
\hat{\gamma}_{l}=\hat{\gamma}_{l}^{\dagger}
, which obey the anticommutation relations
\{\hat{\gamma}_{l},\hat{\gamma}_{l^{\prime}}\}=2\delta_{ll^{\prime}}
.
The Majorana fermions are subject to repeated weak measurements of the parity of Majorana pairs on odd and even bonds with the strength
0\leq\eta_{o}<1
and
\eta_{e}=1-\eta_{o}
, which are described by the Kraus operators,

\displaystyle\hat{M}_{l,b}^{o}=\frac{e^{-ib\theta_{o}\hat{\gamma}_{2l-1}\hat{\gamma}_{2l}}}{\sqrt{2\cosh 2\theta_{o}}},\quad\hat{M}_{l,b}^{e}=\frac{e^{-ib\theta_{e}\hat{\gamma}_{2l}\hat{\gamma}_{2l+1}}}{\sqrt{2\cosh 2\theta_{e}}},\qquad\mathrm{with}\quad\theta_{o/e}=\tanh^{-1}(\eta_{o/e}),

(393)

respectively.
Here,
b=\pm 1
is the measurement outcome randomly determined through the Born probability
p_{l,b}^{o/e}=\left\|\hat{M}_{l,b}^{o/e}\ket{\psi}\right\|^{2}
for the normalized pre-measurement state
\ket{\psi}
.
The unitary time evolution in one time step is described by
\hat{U}_{\mathrm{Kitaev}}=e^{i\hat{H}_{\mathrm{Kitaev}}}
with
\hat{H}_{\mathrm{Kitaev}}=iJ\sum_{l=1}^{2L-1}\hat{\gamma}_{l}\hat{\gamma}_{l+1}+iJ^{\prime}\hat{\gamma}_{2L}\hat{\gamma}_{1}
, where
J
and
J^{\prime}
are real coupling constants.
The open (OBC), periodic (PBC), and antiperiodic (APBC) boundary conditions are specified by
J^{\prime}=0
,
J
, and
-J
, respectively.
This circuit preserves the particle-hole symmetry
\hat{\gamma}_{l}\to-\hat{\gamma}_{l}
, as both measurements and unitary evolution are described by operators bilinear in the Majorana fermions.

Similar monitored circuits with Majorana fermions have been studied in the literature
[
320
,
321
,
322
,
323
,
324
,
325
,
326
]
.
It has been shown that there is a direct transition between two different area-law phases in the absence of unitary evolution:
one exhibits nontrivial topological signatures and is realized when the measurements on even bonds are dominant.
The other is topologically trivial and realized when the measurements on odd bonds are dominant.
When we add unitary dynamics that preserves the particle-hole symmetry and Gaussianity (see below), such as
\hat{U}_{\mathrm{Kitaev}}
, a subvolume-law entanglement phase where the entanglement entropy scales as
(\ln L)^{2}
emerges between the two area-law phases, as predicted by an effective field theory using a non-linear sigma model
[
326
]
.
To summarize, in the circuit model described above, we have three distinct measurement-induced phases; a topological area-law phase (
\eta_{o}<\eta_{\mathrm{topo}}
), a subvolume-law entanglement phase (
\eta_{\mathrm{topo}}<\eta_{o}<\eta_{\mathrm{triv}}
), and a trivial area-law phase (
\eta_{\mathrm{triv}}<\eta_{o}
).

Reference
[
276
]
investigated the measurement-induced topological phase transition in the above model, not only from the entanglement-wise quantities but also from the spectrum, bulk SPT invariant, and Majorana edge modes.
For a trajectory up to time
t
and the corresponding Kraus operator
\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};t}
, we can define the effective Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{\bm{b};t}=-\ln(\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};t}\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};t}^{\dagger})/2t
, as introduced in Eq. (
319
).
In the current model, the circuit preserves the Gaussianity so that the full information of the dynamics is encoded in a single-particle matrix
80
80

80

The circuit preserves the Gaussianity when it maps a Gaussian state
\hat{\rho}\propto\exp(-i\vec{\gamma}^{\mathsf{T}}G\vec{\gamma})
to another Gaussian state
\hat{\rho}^{\prime}\propto\exp(-i\vec{\gamma}^{\mathsf{T}}G^{\prime}\vec{\gamma})
with real antisymmetric matrices
G
and
G^{\prime}
.
In the current model, this is ensured because the corresponding Kraus operator
\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};t}
is generated by operators bilinear in the Majorana fermions.

.
Hence, we define the effective single-particle Hamiltonian matrix
H_{\bm{b};t}
through

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{\bm{b};t}=-i\vec{\gamma}^{\mathsf{T}}H_{\bm{b};t}\vec{\gamma},

(394)

where
H_{\bm{b};t}
is the
2L\times 2L
real antisymmetric matrix written in the basis of Majorana fermions
\vec{\gamma}=(\hat{\gamma}_{1},\ldots\hat{\gamma}_{2L})
.
This enables us to efficiently compute the single-particle Lyapunov spectrum
z_{1}\leq z_{2}\leq\ldots\leq z_{2L}
from the eigenvalues of
H_{\bm{b};t}
.
For the bulk SPT invariant, Ref.
[
276
]
proposed the monitored version of Kitaev’s
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
invariant
[
329
]
:

\displaystyle\chi_{\bm{b};t}=P(\hat{H}_{\bm{b};t}^{\mathrm{PBC}})P(\hat{H}_{\bm{b};t}^{\mathrm{APBC}}),

(395)

where
P(\hat{H})
is the ground-state parity of the Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
.
Here,
\hat{H}_{\bm{b};t}^{\mathrm{PBC}/\mathrm{APBC}}
are the effective Hamiltonians along with the trajectory label
\bm{b}_{t}
under the PBC and APBC, respectively, and defined as follows.
First, we evolve the system by circuit
\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};t}
under the PBC with the Born rule, which gives the effective Hamiltonian
\hat{H}^{\mathrm{PBC}}_{\bm{b};t}
.
Next, we make an identical copy of the PBC circuit by postselecting all measurement outcomes but flipping the outcomes of the copied circuit only at the boundary bond between
l=1
and
2L
; this yields the APBC circuit and the corresponding effective Hamiltonian
\hat{H}^{\mathrm{APBC}}_{\bm{b};t}
.
In this way, we can realize the APBC circuit
with respect to
the given PBC circuit
81
81

81

Note that, independent evolution of the two circuits with the PBC and APBC by the Born rule is meaningless since the measurement histories become different between them, yielding the unrelated Hamiltonians; we cannot make a precise meaning and distinction of PBC or APBC in this way.

.
Then, cutting off the circuits up to time
t
, the dynamical topological invariant is obtained by comparing the ground state parities of
\hat{H}_{\bm{b};t}^{\mathrm{PBC}}
and
\hat{H}_{\bm{b};t}^{\mathrm{APBC}}
82
82

82

The basic ideas presented here are generally applicable to many-body systems, whereas the numerical calculations in Ref.
[
276
]
employed a slightly different method that exploits the Gaussianity of free fermion systems.
See that reference for details.

.

Figure
19
(b) schematically shows that the single-particle Lyapunov spectrum of a typical trajectory under the OBC exhibits a bulk gap in both area-law phases.
In the topological area-law phase, however, two Majorana zero modes as
z_{L}\sim z_{L+1}\to 0
for
L\to\infty
appear.
It was numerically confirmed that these zero modes are localized at the edges, as schematically shown in the inset of Fig.
19
(b), similarly to the ground state in the topological phase of the static Kitaev chain.
On the other hand, the bulk gap closes in the subvolume-law phase.
For the spectra under the PBC, the numerical results show that the two area-law phases are both gapped with the finite-size gap scaling
\Delta(L)=\mathcal{O}(L^{0})
, while the two transition points
\eta_{o}=\eta_{\mathrm{topo}}
and
\eta_{\mathrm{triv}}
both exhibit the critical scaling
\Delta(L)=\mathcal{O}(L^{-1})
.
Interestingly, the gap decays faster than
L^{-1}
inside the subvolume-law phase, meaning that this phase is gapless but has no straightforward counterpart in the ground states of local Hamiltonians with the particle-hole symmetry alone.

Turning to the topological invariant
\chi_{\bm{b};t}
, it typically converges to
-1
in the topological area-law phase for
\eta_{o}<\eta_{\mathrm{topo}}
and
+1
in the trivial area-law phase for
\eta_{o}>\eta_{\mathrm{triv}}
within a short time
t=\mathcal{O}(L)
,

\displaystyle\chi_{\bm{b};t}=\begin{cases}-1&(\eta_{o}<\eta_{\mathrm{topo}})\\
+1&(\eta_{o}>\eta_{\mathrm{triv}})\end{cases}.

(396)

This numerically ensures the bulk-edge correspondence in the gapped measurement-induced topological phases, i.e., the non-trivial (trivial) topological number
\chi_{\bm{b};t}=-1
(
\chi_{\bm{b};t}=+1
) leads to the presence (absence) of topological Majorana zero modes.

The gapless subvolume-law phase requires a careful discussion since, at least for finite size systems, the dynamical topological invariant flows towards
\pm 1
in time for general parameters even inside the gapless phase.
Here, we focus on the fact that the convergence time of the dynamical topological invariant
\chi_{\bm{b};t}
respects the relaxation time scale
\tau_{\mathrm{P}}(L)\sim 1/\Delta(L)
.
Therefore, the convergence time of
\chi_{\bm{b};t}
is less than
\mathcal{O}(L)
for the gapped area-law phases and greater than
\mathcal{O}(L)
for the gapless subvolume-law phase.
Then, the trajectory-averaged dynamical topological invariant
\mathbb{E}[\chi_{\bm{b};t}]
at time
t=\mathcal{O}(L)
is expected to separate the three phases.
In fact, it takes
-1
in the topological area-law phase for
\eta_{o}<\eta_{\mathrm{topo}}
,
+1
in the trivial area-law phase for
\eta_{o}>\eta_{\mathrm{triv}}
, and
0
in the subvolume-law phase for
\eta_{\mathrm{topo}}<\eta_{o}<\eta_{\mathrm{triv}}
in a sufficiently large system.
The results are schematically shown in Fig.
19
(c).

The model studied above is a fermionic Gaussian dynamics, and in the convention of Ref.
[
275
]
, it belongs to class D in the tenfold Altland-Zirnbauer (AZ) symmetry class
[
230
]
.
The reference
[
275
]
performed the AZ classification for monitored free fermionic systems based on the symmetries of the single-particle time evolution operators.
The classification scheme varies depending on which operators are considered and how the relevant symmetries are defined.
In other works, based on the correspondence between a monitored free fermion dynamics and a static Anderson localization problem
[
330
,
326
,
331
]
, the AZ classification was instead performed by analyzing the symmetries of the single-particle transfer matrix that describes the corresponding Anderson localization problem
[
327
,
328
]
.
In the latter convention, the above model belongs to class DIII.
Reference
[
328
]
further proposed a general framework to treat symmetries in many-body systems and demonstrated that it consistently reproduces the tenfold AZ symmetry classification in the free fermion limit.
A comprehensive classification of topological phases in interacting monitored dynamics, however, remains elusive.

8
Conclusion and outlook

We reviewed the basics and various aspects of monitored quantum systems, focusing on typical behaviors, spectral properties, and many-body phases.
Regarding the CPTP dynamics averaged over measurement outcomes, we provided pedagogical introductions about various representations, discrete- and continuous-time descriptions, spectral features, and properties of steady states.
We explained that long-time behaviors of quantum states are profoundly related to the irreducibility and primitivity of the averaged dynamics, and these properties can be examined through the algebra of Kraus operators in the discrete dynamics or jump operators in the continuous dynamics.
Regarding quantum trajectories, where outcomes of all measurements are recorded, we discussed that the jump statistics, nonlinear observables, ergodicity, purification, and Lyapunov spectrum capture intriguing features of the random trajectories that may not be seen in the averaged CPTP dynamics.

The aforementioned features of the CPTP dynamics have huge effects on the corresponding quantum trajectories.
For example, quantum trajectories exhibit the ergodicity of linear observables if the steady state for the averaged dynamics is unique due to, e.g., irreducibility.
In addition, the irreducibility of the CPTP dynamics, combined with purification of typical trajectories, leads to the ergodicity of nonlinear observables and typical convergence of the Lyapunov spectrum.
We also introduced measurement-induced phase transitions as representative phenomena that occur in quantum trajectories but are usually invisible in the averaged CPTP dynamics; we explained that the Lyapunov spectrum, purification timescales, and nonlinear observables such as the entanglement entropy, computed only from quantum trajectories, are good indicators to detect measurement-induced phase transitions.

There are several future directions that should be intriguing.
One direction is to explore the behaviors of quantum trajectories and the CPTP dynamics in intermediate timescales:
On one hand, the ground states of the effective Hamiltonians describe the behaviors of quantum trajectories in the long-time regime
t\gg 1/\Delta
where
\Delta
is the Lyapunov spectral gap, as discussed in Sec.
6.3
.
On the other hand, the steady states of the CPTP dynamics describe the behaviors of averaged quantum states in the long-time regime
t\gg 1/\Delta^{\prime}
where
\Delta^{\prime}
is the spectral gap of the corresponding CPTP map, as discussed in Chapter
4
.
However, it is highly nontrivial how other excited states and the corresponding spectrum contribute to their behaviors in intermediate timescales
t\ll 1/\Delta
or
t\ll 1/\Delta^{\prime}
.
For example, exploring the relations between the excitation spectrum and the convergent behaviors of time averages of observables should be interesting.

The second direction is to study the physical consequences of breaking “nice” conditions, such as the irreducibility, ergodicity, and/or typical purification of quantum trajectories.
It is known that in reducible monitored quantum systems, the Hilbert space may be decomposed into several orthogonal subspaces, such as the decaying subspace and decoherence-free subspace
[
332
]
.
Exploring the relations between the spectral features and the decompositions in such reducible quantum systems should be intriguing.
It should also be interesting to study the dynamical behaviors of the Lyapunov spectrum and nonlinear observables in quantum trajectories of reducible, nonergodic, and/or unpurifying monitored systems.

The third direction is to explore how to detect distinctions between quantum trajectories and the corresponding CPTP dynamics.
If we focus on linear observables, their time average in one quantum trajectory and ensemble average in the CPTP dynamics become the same when the system is ergodic, as discussed in Sec.
5.1
.
This means that it is difficult to find the distinctions on the basis of linear observables.
While we can reveal unique features of quantum trajectories absent in the CPTP dynamics through focusing on nonlinear observables, there is a postselection problem as discussed in Sec.
3.3.1
.
Finding ways to circumvent these problems should be important in understanding monitored quantum systems and also in probing measurement-induced phase transitions.
One possible approach is to study statistics of quantum jumps, which is free from the postselection problem.
Another approach will be to perform feedback operations depending on measurement outcomes
83
83

83

Although we have entirely omitted quantum feedback operations in this review, there are several references to this subject; see, e.g,
[
7
,
333
,
334
,
335
,
35
,
336
]
.
so that certain properties of quantum trajectories become visible even for the averaged dynamics.

Appendix A
Some important mathematical theorems on positive maps

A.1
Russo-Dye theorem

Here, we provide a proof of the Russo-Dye theorem for a positive unital linear map
\mathcal{T}:\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{H})\to\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{H})
, which is used in Sec.
4.1
.
The proof follows Ref.
[
337
]
.
To do so, we first prove the following lemma.

Lemma 13
.

Let
\hat{A}\in\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{H})
.
Then
\hat{A}
satisfies
\lVert\hat{A}\rVert\leq 1
(i.e.,
\hat{A}
is contractive) if and only if
\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\mathbb{I}}&\hat{A}\\
\hat{A}^{\dagger}&\hat{\mathbb{I}}\end{pmatrix}
is positive semidefinite.

Proof.

Let
d=\dim(\mathcal{H})
.
Let
\hat{A}=\hat{U}\hat{\Lambda}\hat{V}
be the singular value decomposition of
\hat{A}
, where
\hat{U}
and
\hat{V}
are unitary matrices and
\hat{\Lambda}=\mathrm{diag}(\Lambda_{1},\cdots,\Lambda_{d})
is a diagonal matrix with singular values
\Lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\Lambda_{d}\geq 0
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\mathbb{I}}&\hat{A}\\
\hat{A}^{\dagger}&\hat{\mathbb{I}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\mathbb{I}}&\hat{U}\hat{\Lambda}\hat{V}\\
\hat{V}^{\dagger}\hat{\Lambda}\hat{U}^{\dagger}&\hat{\mathbb{I}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\hat{U}&0\\
0&\hat{V}^{\dagger}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\mathbb{I}}&\hat{\Lambda}\\
\hat{\Lambda}&\hat{\mathbb{I}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\hat{U}^{\dagger}&0\\
0&\hat{V}\end{pmatrix}.

(397)

This matrix is unitarily equivalent to the following matrix,

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}1&\Lambda_{1}\\
\Lambda_{1}&1\end{pmatrix}\oplus\cdots\oplus\begin{pmatrix}1&\Lambda_{d}\\
\Lambda_{d}&1\end{pmatrix}.

(398)

Since each
2\times 2
block matrix has eigenvalues
\{1\pm\Lambda_{j}\}
, this matrix is positive semidefinite if and only if
\lVert\hat{A}\rVert=\Lambda_{1}\leq 1
.
∎

We are now ready to prove the Russo-Dye theorem.

Theorem 14
.

If
\mathcal{T}
is a positive unital linear map, then its operator norm satisfies
\lVert\mathcal{T}\rVert=1
.

Proof.

We first observe that the operator norm
\lVert\mathcal{T}\rVert
is equivalently written as

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{T}\rVert=\sup_{\lVert\hat{X}\rVert\leq 1}\lVert\mathcal{T}[\hat{X}]\rVert.

(399)

Suppose
\hat{X}\in\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{H})
has
\lVert\hat{X}\rVert\leq 1
.
Let
\hat{X}=\hat{U}^{\prime}\hat{\Lambda}\hat{V}^{\prime}
be the singular value decomposition of
\hat{X}
, where
\hat{U}^{\prime}
and
\hat{V}^{\prime}
are unitary matrices and
\hat{\Lambda}=\mathrm{diag}(\Lambda_{1},\cdots,\Lambda_{d})
is a diagonal matrix with singular values
\Lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\Lambda_{d}\geq 0
.
Since
\Lambda_{1}\leq 1
, we can write
\hat{\Lambda}
as

\displaystyle\hat{\Lambda}=\begin{pmatrix}\Lambda_{1}&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&\Lambda_{d}\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}e^{i\theta_{1}}&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&e^{i\theta_{d}}\end{pmatrix}+\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}e^{-i\theta_{1}}&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&e^{-i\theta_{d}}\end{pmatrix}

(400)

with some
\theta_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
.
Thus,
\hat{X}
can be written as
\hat{X}=\frac{1}{2}(\hat{U}_{+}+\hat{U}_{-})
with unitary matrices
\hat{U}_{+}
and
\hat{U}_{-}
.
We then find

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{T}[\hat{X}]\rVert=\frac{1}{2}\lVert\mathcal{T}[\hat{U}_{+}+\hat{U}_{-}]\rVert\leq\frac{1}{2}\left(\lVert\mathcal{T}[\hat{U}_{+}]\rVert+\lVert\mathcal{T}[\hat{U}_{-}]\rVert\right).

(401)

We now wish to show
\lVert\mathcal{T}[\hat{U}]\rVert\leq 1
for unitary
\hat{U}
.
Using the spectral decomposition
\hat{U}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}e^{i\phi_{j}}\hat{P}_{j}
with
\phi_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
and rank-one projectors
\hat{P}_{j}
, we find

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\mathbb{I}}&\mathcal{T}[\hat{U}]\\
\mathcal{T}[\hat{U}]^{\dagger}&\hat{\mathbb{I}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\mathbb{I}}&\sum_{j}e^{i\phi_{j}}\mathcal{T}[\hat{P}_{j}]\\
\sum_{j}e^{-i\phi_{j}}\mathcal{T}[\hat{P}_{j}]&\hat{\mathbb{I}}\end{pmatrix}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\begin{pmatrix}1&e^{i\phi_{j}}\\
e^{-i\phi_{j}}&1\end{pmatrix}\otimes\mathcal{T}[\hat{P}_{j}],

(402)

where we have used
\sum_{j}\mathcal{T}[\hat{P}_{j}]=\hat{\mathbb{I}}
at the last equality.
Since this matrix is positive semidefinite, by the above lemma we obtain
\lVert\mathcal{T}[\hat{U}]\rVert\leq 1
.
This implies
\lVert\mathcal{T}[\hat{X}]\rVert\leq 1
for
\lVert\hat{X}\rVert\leq 1
and thus
\lVert\mathcal{T}\rVert\leq 1
.
On the other hand, since
\mathcal{T}
is unital, we have
\lVert\mathcal{T}[\hat{\mathbb{I}}]\rVert=\lVert\hat{\mathbb{I}}\rVert=1
and therefore
\lVert\mathcal{T}\rVert=1
.
∎

A.2
Existence of a stationary state for CPTP maps

Here, we provide a proof that any CPTP map
\mathcal{E}:\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]\to\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
has at least one stationary state
\hat{\varrho}_{0}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
, i.e., a density operator satisfying
\mathcal{E}[\hat{\varrho}_{0}]=\hat{\varrho}_{0}
.
This can be understood as a consequence of Brouwer’s fixed-point theorem, which states that any continuous map
\mathcal{F}
from a nonempty compact convex set
\mathbb{S}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
to itself has a fixed point
\hat{x}_{0}\in\mathbb{S}
so that
\mathcal{F}[\hat{x}_{0}]=\hat{x}_{0}
.
Since we consider a finite-dimensional Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
with
d=\dim(\mathcal{H})
, any Hermitian operator
\hat{X}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
can be represented as a
d\times d
Hermitian matrix and is isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{d^{2}}
.
The set of density operators is nonempty (since there exists
\hat{\rho}=\hat{\mathbb{I}}/d
), convex (since
s\hat{\rho}_{1}+(1-s)\hat{\rho}_{2}
with
0\leq s\leq 1
is a density operator if
\hat{\rho}_{1}
and
\hat{\rho}_{2}
are density operators), and compact (since boundedness and closedness are ensured by
\mathrm{Tr}(\hat{\rho})=1
and
\hat{\rho}\succeq 0
).
Since
\mathcal{E}
is linear, it is also continuous for finite-dimensional
\mathcal{H}
.
Thus, the existence of a stationary state is ensured by Brouwer’s fixed-point theorem.

However, since we only consider linear CPTP maps and any CPTP map is a contraction for the operator norm, we can explicitly construct a stationary state by adapting the proof of Theorem 4.4.1 in Ref.
[
6
]
.

Theorem 15
.

For finite-dimensional
\mathcal{H}
, any CPTP map
\mathcal{E}:\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]\to\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
has at least one density operator
\hat{\varrho}_{0}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
such that
\mathcal{E}[\hat{\varrho}_{0}]=\hat{\varrho}_{0}
.

Proof.

We explicitly construct such a density operator.
Taking any density operator
\hat{\rho}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
, we consider its time average,

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{\textrm{av}}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\mathcal{E}^{n}[\hat{\rho}],

(403)

where
\mathcal{E}^{0}[\hat{\rho}]=\hat{\rho}
.
Since
\mathcal{E}
is CPTP, each
\hat{\rho}_{n}=\mathcal{E}^{n}[\hat{\rho}]
is a density operator and their convex sum
(1/N)\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\hat{\rho}_{n}
for a finite
N
is also a density operator.
Since the set of density operators is compact, the limit
\hat{\rho}_{\textrm{av}}
exists within the set and is a density operator.
Since
\mathcal{E}
is a continuous linear map for finite-dimensional
\mathcal{H}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}[\hat{\rho}_{\textrm{av}}]=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\mathcal{E}[\mathcal{E}^{n}[\hat{\rho}]]=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathcal{E}^{n}[\hat{\rho}].

(404)

We then find

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}[\hat{\rho}_{\textrm{av}}]-\hat{\rho}_{\textrm{av}}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\mathcal{E}^{N}[\hat{\rho}]-\hat{\rho}}{N}.

(405)

Since
\|\mathcal{E}^{N}[\hat{\rho}]-\hat{\rho}\|\leq\|\mathcal{E}^{N}[\hat{\rho}]\|+\|\hat{\rho}\|\leq\|\mathcal{E}\|^{N}\|\hat{\rho}\|+\|\hat{\rho}\|\leq 2
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\|\mathcal{E}^{N}[\hat{\rho}]-\hat{\rho}\|}{N}=0

(406)

and therefore

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\mathcal{E}^{N}[\hat{\rho}]-\hat{\rho}}{N}=0.

(407)

Thus, we find
\mathcal{E}[\hat{\rho}_{\textrm{av}}]=\hat{\rho}_{\textrm{av}}
.
∎

A.3
Burnside’s theorem on matrix algebras

A subset
\mathbb{A}\subseteq\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
is called an algebra if it is closed under scalar multiplication, addition, and multiplication.
A set
\mathbb{S}\subseteq\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
is said to be irreducible if the only subspaces of
\mathcal{H}
invariant under the action of
\mathbb{S}
are
\{0\}
and
\mathcal{H}
.
Then, Burnside’s theorem on matrix algebras specialized to
\mathbb{A}\subseteq\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
is stated as follows.

Theorem 16

(Burnside’s theorem on matrix algebras)
.

An algebra
\mathbb{A}\subseteq\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
is irreducible if and only if
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
.

Proof.

Since the if part is trivial, we prove the only if part following Ref.
[
338
]
.

We first show that
\mathbb{A}
contains a rank-1 operator.
Let
\hat{T}_{0}\in\mathbb{A}
be a nonzero operator with the smallest rank
r
among all elements in
\mathbb{A}
.
Suppose
r>1
.
Let
\ket{x_{1}},\ket{x_{2}}\in\mathcal{H}
be such that
\hat{T}_{0}|x_{1}\rangle
and
\hat{T}_{0}|x_{2}\rangle
are linearly independent.
Since
\mathbb{A}|x\rangle=\mathcal{H}
for any nonzero
\ket{x}\in\mathcal{H}
and
\hat{T}_{0}|x_{1}\rangle\neq 0
, there exists
\hat{A}\in\mathbb{A}
such that
\hat{A}\hat{T}_{0}|x_{1}\rangle=|x_{2}\rangle
.
Then,
\hat{T}_{0}\hat{A}\hat{T}_{0}|x_{1}\rangle
and
\hat{T}_{0}|x_{1}\rangle
are linearly independent, so that
\hat{T}_{0}\hat{A}\hat{T}_{0}-\lambda\hat{T}_{0}
is nonzero for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
.
When restricted to
\hat{T}_{0}\mathcal{H}
,
\hat{T}_{0}\hat{A}
has an eigenvector
\ket{y_{0}}\in\hat{T}_{0}\mathcal{H}
with eigenvalue
\lambda_{0}
, and hence
\hat{T}_{0}\hat{A}-\lambda_{0}\hat{\mathbb{I}}
has a rank lower than
\dim(\hat{T}_{0}\mathcal{H})=r
.
This contradicts the assumption that
\hat{T}_{0}
has the lowest rank, and thus
r
must be 1.

We next show that
\mathbb{A}
contains all rank-1 operators.
Since
\hat{T}_{0}\in\mathbb{A}
has rank 1, we can write
\hat{T}_{0}=|u_{0}\rangle\langle v_{0}|
with some
|u_{0}\rangle,|v_{0}\rangle\in\mathcal{H}
.
Since
\mathbb{A}|u_{0}\rangle=\mathbb{A}|v_{0}\rangle=\mathcal{H}
, for any
|u\rangle,|v\rangle\in\mathcal{H}
there exist
\hat{B},\hat{C}\in\mathbb{A}
such that
|u\rangle=\hat{B}|u_{0}\rangle
and
|v\rangle=\hat{C}^{\dagger}|v_{0}\rangle
.
Since
\mathbb{A}
is an algebra,
\hat{B}\hat{T}_{0}\hat{C}\in\mathbb{A}
for any
\hat{B},\hat{C}\in\mathbb{A}
.
In this way, we can construct
\hat{B}\hat{T}_{0}\hat{C}=|u\rangle\langle v|\in\mathbb{A}
for any pairs of
\ket{u},\ket{v}\in\mathcal{H}
.
Since any
\hat{X}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
can be written as a sum of rank-1 operators
|u\rangle\langle v|
, we prove
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
.
∎

A.4
CP unital map is a Schwarz map

Here, we give a proof that a CP unital map
\mathcal{T}:\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]\to\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
is a Schwarz map, i.e., a map satisfying the Kadison-Schwarz inequality
\mathcal{T}[\hat{A}^{\dagger}\hat{A}]\succeq\mathcal{T}[\hat{A}^{\dagger}]\mathcal{T}[\hat{A}]
for any
\hat{A}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
.
To begin with, we first prove the following lemma:

Lemma 17
.

Let
\hat{A}
,
\hat{B}
,
\hat{C}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
.
If a block matrix
\hat{M}:=\begin{pmatrix}\hat{A}&\hat{B}^{\dagger}\\
\hat{B}&\hat{C}\end{pmatrix}
is positive semidefinite and
\hat{C}
is positive definite, then
\hat{A}\succeq\hat{B}^{\dagger}\hat{C}^{-1}\hat{B}
.

Proof.

Let
\ket{x}\in\mathcal{H}
.
Since
\hat{C}
is invertible, we can define
v=\begin{pmatrix}\ket{x}\\
-\hat{C}^{-1}\hat{B}\ket{x}\end{pmatrix}
.
Since
\hat{M}\succeq 0
,
v^{\dagger}\hat{M}v\geq 0
and thus

\displaystyle 0\leq v^{\dagger}\hat{M}v=\begin{pmatrix}\bra{x}&-\bra{x}\hat{B}^{\dagger}\hat{C}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\hat{A}&\hat{B}^{\dagger}\\
\hat{B}&\hat{C}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\ket{x}\\
-\hat{C}^{-1}\hat{B}\ket{x}\end{pmatrix}=\bra{x}\left(\hat{A}-\hat{B}^{\dagger}\hat{C}^{-1}\hat{B}\right)\ket{x}.

(408)

Since this must hold for any
\ket{x}\in\mathcal{H}
, we have
\hat{A}\succeq\hat{B}^{\dagger}\hat{C}^{-1}\hat{B}
.
∎

We then prove the original proposition.

Theorem 18
.

If a map
\mathcal{T}:\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]\to\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
is CP and unital,
\mathcal{T}
is also a Schwarz map.

Proof.

Let us consider a block matrix
\hat{X}:=\begin{pmatrix}\hat{A}^{\dagger}\hat{A}&\hat{A}^{\dagger}\\
\hat{A}&\hat{\mathbb{I}}\end{pmatrix}
with any
\hat{A}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
.
This matrix is obviously positive semidefinite as it can be written as
\hat{X}=\begin{pmatrix}\hat{A}^{\dagger}\\
\hat{\mathbb{I}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\hat{A}&\hat{\mathbb{I}}\end{pmatrix}
.
Since
\mathcal{T}
is CP, it is also 2-positive
84
84

84

As given in Eq. (
29
), a linear map
\mathcal{T}:\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]\to\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
is CP if
(\mathcal{T}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{n})[\hat{R}]\succeq 0
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and for
\hat{R}\succeq 0
on
\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{n}]
, where
\mathcal{H}_{n}
is an auxiliary
n
-dimensional Hilbert space and
\mathcal{I}_{n}:\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}_{n}]\to\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}_{n}]
is the identity map.
When this property holds for a specific
n
,
\mathcal{T}
is called
n
-positive.

and thus

\displaystyle\hat{M}:=(\mathcal{T}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{2})[\hat{X}]=\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{T}[\hat{A}^{\dagger}\hat{A}]&\mathcal{T}[\hat{A}^{\dagger}]\\
\mathcal{T}[\hat{A}]&\mathcal{T}[\hat{\mathbb{I}}]\end{pmatrix}\succeq 0.

(409)

Since
\mathcal{T}
is unital, we have

\displaystyle\hat{M}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{T}[\hat{A}^{\dagger}\hat{A}]&(\mathcal{T}[\hat{A}])^{\dagger}\\
\mathcal{T}[\hat{A}]&\hat{\mathbb{I}}\end{pmatrix}\succeq 0,

(410)

where we have used the Hermiticity preserving property of
\mathcal{T}
,
\mathcal{T}[\hat{A}^{\dagger}]=(\mathcal{T}[\hat{A}])^{\dagger}
.
Using the above lemma, we find the Kadison-Schwarz inequality
\mathcal{T}[\hat{A}^{\dagger}\hat{A}]\succeq\mathcal{T}[\hat{A}^{\dagger}]\mathcal{T}[\hat{A}]
.
∎

A.5
Schur’s lemma on self-adjoint sets

We call
\mathbb{S}\subseteq\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
a self-adjoint set if
\hat{S}^{\dagger}\in\mathbb{S}
for every
\hat{S}\in\mathbb{S}
.
We say that
\mathbb{S}\subseteq\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
is irreducible if the only subspaces of
\mathcal{H}
invariant under the action of
\mathbb{S}
are
\{0\}
and
\mathcal{H}
.
The commutant of
\mathbb{S}
is defined as a set of operators commuting with all elements of
\mathbb{S}
, that is,

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}^{\prime}=\{\hat{T}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]:[\hat{T},\hat{S}]=0\textrm{ for all }\hat{S}\in\mathbb{S}\}.

(411)

Then, we have the following theorem known as Schur’s lemma in the context of operator algebra (see, e.g., Theorem 5.1.6 of Ref.
[
339
]
).

Theorem 19

(Schur’s lemma)
.

Let
\mathbb{S}\subseteq\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
be a self-adjoint set.
Then
\mathbb{S}^{\prime}=\mathbb{C}\hat{\mathbb{I}}
if and only if
\mathbb{S}
is irreducible.

Proof.

Let
\hat{T}\in\mathbb{S}^{\prime}
.
For any
\hat{S}\in\mathbb{S}
, we have
[\hat{T},\hat{S}]=0
and
[\hat{T}^{\dagger},\hat{S}^{\dagger}]=[\hat{S},\hat{T}]^{\dagger}=0
.
Since
\hat{S}^{\dagger}\in\mathbb{S}
,
\hat{T}^{\dagger}
is also an element of
\mathbb{S}^{\prime}
(this means that
\mathbb{S}^{\prime}
is also self-adjoint).
Since any
\hat{T}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
can be decomposed into a sum of two Hermitian operators
\hat{T}_{1}=\frac{1}{2}(\hat{T}+\hat{T}^{\dagger})
and
\hat{T}_{2}=\frac{1}{2i}(\hat{T}-\hat{T}^{\dagger})
as
\hat{T}=\hat{T}_{1}+i\hat{T}_{2}
, we can assume
\hat{T}^{\dagger}=\hat{T}
without loss of generality.

Suppose that
\mathbb{S}
is irreducible.
Let
\hat{T}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lambda_{i}|\psi_{i}\rangle\langle\psi_{i}|
be the spectral decomposition of
\hat{T}
with eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
and eigenvectors
\ket{\psi_{i}}\in\mathcal{H}
.
Let
\mathcal{V}_{\lambda}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
be an eigenspace spanned by the eigenvectors of
\hat{T}
with the eigenvalue
\lambda
.
For any
\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{V}_{\lambda}
and
\hat{S}\in\mathbb{S}
, we have
\hat{T}\hat{S}|\psi\rangle=\hat{S}\hat{T}|\psi\rangle=\lambda\hat{S}|\psi\rangle
.
Since
\hat{S}|\psi\rangle\in\mathcal{V}_{\lambda}
,
\mathcal{V}_{\lambda}
is an invariant subspace of
\mathcal{H}
under
\mathbb{S}
.
However, such a subspace must be
0
or
\mathcal{H}
by assumption, and thus
\mathcal{V}_{\lambda}=\mathcal{H}
for any
\lambda
.
This implies
\lambda_{i}=\lambda
and hence
\hat{T}=\lambda\hat{\mathbb{I}}
.
This proves
\mathbb{S}^{\prime}=\mathbb{C}\hat{\mathbb{I}}
.

Suppose that
\mathbb{S}^{\prime}=\mathbb{C}\hat{\mathbb{I}}
.
Let
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
be an invariant subspace of
\mathcal{H}
under
\mathbb{S}
and
\mathcal{V}_{\perp}
be its orthogonal complement.
For any
\ket{v}\in\mathcal{V}
,
\ket{u}\in\mathcal{V}_{\perp}
, and
\hat{S}\in\mathbb{S}
, we have
\hat{S}^{\dagger}|v\rangle\in\mathcal{V}
and
\langle v|\hat{S}|u\rangle=(\hat{S}^{\dagger}|v\rangle)^{\dagger}|u\rangle
= 0.
This implies
\hat{S}|u\rangle\in\mathcal{V}_{\perp}
and that
\mathcal{V}_{\perp}
is also an invariant subspace of
\mathcal{H}
under
\mathbb{S}
.
Then, let
\hat{P}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
be an orthogonal projection operator to
\mathcal{V}
.
Since any
\ket{x}\in\mathcal{H}
can be written as
\ket{x}=\ket{v}+\ket{u}
with some
\ket{v}\in\mathcal{V}
and
\ket{u}\in\mathcal{V}_{\perp}
, we have
\hat{P}\hat{S}|x\rangle=\hat{S}|v\rangle=\hat{S}\hat{P}|x\rangle
.
Thus, we have
[\hat{P},\hat{S}]=0
for any
\hat{S}\in\mathbb{S}
.
By assumption,
\hat{P}
must be proportional to the identity operator, i.e.
\hat{P}=\mu\hat{\mathbb{I}}
with some
\mu\in\mathbb{C}
.
Since
\hat{P}^{2}=\hat{P}
, we have
\mu=0
or
1
and therefore
\mathcal{V}=\{0\}
or
\mathcal{H}
.
∎

Appendix B
Proof of Proposition
1

Here we provide a proof of Proposition
1
for the irreducibility of CPTP maps
\mathcal{E}
.

Proof.

The proof for (1)
\Leftrightarrow
(2) follows Ref.
[
104
]
, while that for (1)
\Leftrightarrow
(3) follows Refs.
[
100
,
36
]
.

(1)
\Rightarrow
(2):
Suppose that there exists an orthogonal projection operator
\hat{P}\notin\{0,\hat{\mathbb{I}}\}
such that
\hat{P}\preceq\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}[\hat{P}]
.
For any positive semidefinite
\hat{\rho}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{P}\hat{\rho}\hat{P}]\leq\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{P}\hat{\rho}\hat{P}\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}[\hat{P}]]=\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{P}\mathcal{E}[\hat{P}\hat{\rho}\hat{P}]\hat{P}]\leq\mathrm{Tr}[\mathcal{E}[\hat{P}\hat{\rho}\hat{P}]]=\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{P}\hat{\rho}\hat{P}],

(412)

which yields the equality
\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{P}\mathcal{E}[\hat{P}\hat{\rho}\hat{P}]\hat{P}]=\mathrm{Tr}[\mathcal{E}[\hat{P}\hat{\rho}\hat{P}]]
.
Since
\mathcal{E}[\hat{P}\hat{\rho}\hat{P}]\succeq 0
,
this implies
\mathcal{E}[\hat{P}\hat{\rho}\hat{P}]=\hat{P}\mathcal{E}[\hat{P}\hat{\rho}\hat{P}]\hat{P}\in\hat{P}\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]\hat{P}
85
85

85

Let
\hat{A}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
be a positive semidefinite operator and
\hat{P}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
be an orthogonal projection operator.
If
\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{A}]=\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{P}\hat{A}]
, then
\hat{A}=\hat{P}\hat{A}\hat{P}
.

Proof
. Let
\hat{A}=\sum_{n}\lambda_{n}\ket{\psi_{n}}\bra{\psi_{n}}
be the spectral decomposition of
\hat{A}
with
\lambda_{n}\geq 0
,
\ket{\psi_{n}}\in\mathcal{H}
, and
\||\psi_{n}\rangle\|=1
.
Using
\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{A}]=\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{P}\hat{A}]
, we have
\sum_{n}\lambda_{n}(1-\|\hat{P}|\psi_{n}\rangle\|^{2})=\sum_{n}\lambda_{n}\|(\hat{\mathbb{I}}-\hat{P})|\psi_{n}\rangle\|^{2}=0
, which implies
\lambda_{n}=0
or
(\hat{\mathbb{I}}-\hat{P})\ket{\psi_{n}}=0
.
Then, we have
(\hat{\mathbb{I}}-\hat{P})\hat{A}=\hat{A}(\hat{\mathbb{I}}-\hat{P})=0
and thus
\hat{A}=\hat{P}\hat{A}=\hat{A}\hat{P}=\hat{P}\hat{A}\hat{P}
.

.
Moreover,
\mathcal{E}[\hat{P}\hat{X}\hat{P}]\in\hat{P}\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]\hat{P}
holds for any (not necessarily positive semidefinite)
\hat{X}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
, since
\mathcal{E}
is linear and
\hat{X}
can always be decomposed into a linear combination of positive semidefinite operators
86
86

86

Any
\hat{X}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
can be decomposed as
\hat{X}=\hat{H}_{+}+i\hat{K}_{+}-(\hat{H}_{-}+i\hat{K}_{-})
with some positive semidefinite operators
\hat{H}_{\pm},\hat{K}_{\pm}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
.

.
We therefore find
\hat{P}\notin\{0,\hat{\mathbb{I}}\}
such that
\mathcal{E}[\hat{P}\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]\hat{P}]\subseteq\hat{P}\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]\hat{P}
.

(2)
\Rightarrow
(1):
Suppose that there exists an orthogonal projection operator
\hat{P}\notin\{0,\hat{\mathbb{I}}\}
such that
\mathcal{E}[\hat{P}\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]\hat{P}]\subseteq\hat{P}\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]\hat{P}
.
For any
\hat{X}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
, the following holds

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{X}\hat{P}]=\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{P}\hat{X}\hat{P}]=\mathrm{Tr}[\mathcal{E}[\hat{P}\hat{X}\hat{P}]]=\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{P}\mathcal{E}[\hat{P}\hat{X}\hat{P}]\hat{P}]=\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{X}\hat{P}\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}[\hat{P}]\hat{P}].

(413)

Here, the second equality comes from TP of
\mathcal{E}
and the third equality from
\mathcal{E}[\hat{P}\hat{X}\hat{P}]\in\hat{P}\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]\hat{P}
by the assumption, which implies
\mathcal{E}[\hat{P}\hat{X}\hat{P}]=\hat{P}\mathcal{E}[\hat{P}\hat{X}\hat{P}]\hat{P}
.
From Eq. (
413
), we obtain
\hat{P}=\hat{P}\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}[\hat{P}]\hat{P}
.
Let
\hat{Q}=\hat{\mathbb{I}}-\hat{P}
be the projection operator to the complement of
\hat{P}
.
Since
\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}
is unital, i.e.,
\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}[\hat{\mathbb{I}}]=\hat{\mathbb{I}}
, we have
\hat{P}\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}[\hat{Q}]\hat{P}=\hat{P}(\hat{\mathbb{I}}-\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}[\hat{P}])\hat{P}=0
.
Since
\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}[\hat{Q}]\succeq 0
,
this implies
\hat{P}\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}[\hat{Q}]=\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}[\hat{Q}]\hat{P}=0
87
87

87

Let
\hat{A}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
be a positive semidefinite operator and
\hat{P}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
be an orthogonal projection operator.
If
\hat{P}\hat{A}\hat{P}=0
, then
\hat{P}\hat{A}=\hat{A}\hat{P}=0
.

Proof.
Since
\hat{A}\succeq 0
, there is a unique decomposition
\hat{A}=\hat{A}^{1/2}\hat{A}^{1/2}
by some positive semidefinite operator
\hat{A}^{1/2}=(\hat{A}^{1/2})^{\dagger}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
.
Since
\langle\psi|\hat{P}\hat{A}\hat{P}|\psi\rangle=\|\hat{A}^{1/2}\hat{P}\ket{\psi}\|^{2}=0
holds for any
\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{H}
, we have
\hat{A}^{1/2}\hat{P}=0
and thus
\hat{A}^{1/2}\hat{A}^{1/2}\hat{P}=\hat{A}\hat{P}=0
.
Its Hermitian conjugate gives
(\hat{A}\hat{P})^{\dagger}=\hat{P}\hat{A}=0
.

and thus
\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}[\hat{Q}]=(\hat{P}+\hat{Q})\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}[\hat{Q}](\hat{P}+\hat{Q})=\hat{Q}\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}[\hat{Q}]\hat{Q}
.
Finally, we find that
\hat{P}\notin\{0,\hat{\mathbb{I}}\}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}[\hat{P}]=\hat{\mathbb{I}}-\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}[\hat{Q}]=\hat{\mathbb{I}}-\hat{Q}\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}[\hat{Q}]\hat{Q}=\hat{P}+\hat{Q}\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}[\hat{P}]\hat{Q}\succeq\hat{P}.

(414)

(1)
\Rightarrow
(3):
We first show that the irreducibility in the sense of (1) implies that
(\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{E})^{d-1}[\hat{\rho}]\succ 0
for any nonzero
\hat{\rho}\succeq 0
in
\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
, which gives another definition of irreducibility when
\mathcal{H}
has a finite dimension
d
.
Here,
(\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{E})[\hat{X}]=\hat{X}+\mathcal{E}[\hat{X}]
.
We then show that it further implies (3).

Let
\hat{\rho}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
be nonzero and positive semidefinite.
Assume that (1) holds for
\mathcal{E}
.
Since
\mathcal{E}[\hat{\rho}]\succeq 0
,
\ker((\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{E})[\hat{\rho}])\subseteq\ker(\hat{\rho})
88
88

88

Let
\ket{\psi}\in\ker(\hat{\rho}+\mathcal{E}[\hat{\rho}])
.
Then,
\langle\psi|(\hat{\rho}+\mathcal{E}[\hat{\rho}])|\psi\rangle=0
and thus
\langle\psi|\hat{\rho}|\psi\rangle=-\langle\psi|\mathcal{E}[\hat{\rho}]|\psi\rangle
.
Since
\hat{\rho}\succeq 0
and
\mathcal{E}[\hat{\rho}]\succeq 0
, this implies
\hat{\rho}\ket{\psi}=\hat{\mathcal{E}}[\hat{\rho}]\ket{\psi}=0
.
As this holds for any
\ket{\psi}\in\ker(\hat{\rho}+\mathcal{E}[\hat{\rho}])
, we have
\ker(\hat{\rho}+\mathcal{E}[\hat{\rho}])\subseteq\ker(\hat{\rho}),\ker(\mathcal{E}[\hat{\rho}])
.
[We can actually prove
\ker(\hat{\rho}+\mathcal{E}[\hat{\rho}])=\ker(\hat{\rho})\cap\ker(\mathcal{E}[\hat{\rho}])
].
.
Suppose that the equality
\ker((\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{E})[\hat{\rho}])=\ker(\hat{\rho})
holds, that is
\ker(\mathcal{E}[\hat{\rho}])\supseteq\ker(\hat{\rho})
.
Let
\hat{P}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
be an orthogonal projection operator onto the image of
\hat{\rho}
.
We then introduce a set of positive semidefinite operators whose images lie in the image of
\hat{\rho}
:
\mathbb{F}_{P}:=\{\hat{\sigma}\succeq 0:\ker(\hat{\sigma})\supseteq\ker(\hat{P})\}
.
Obviously,
\hat{\rho}\in\mathbb{F}_{P}
and
\mathcal{E}[\hat{\rho}]\in\mathbb{F}_{P}
.
Since
\hat{\rho}
is strictly positive (
\hat{\rho}\succ 0
) in the space
\hat{P}\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]\hat{P}
, there exists
c>0
such that
\hat{\sigma}\preceq c\hat{\rho}
for any
\hat{\sigma}\in\mathbb{F}_{P}
89
89

89

If
\hat{A}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
is positive definite and
\hat{B}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
is positive semidefinite, then there exists
c>0
such that
\hat{B}\preceq c\hat{A}
.

Proof
. Since
\hat{A}
is invertible, we can introduce
\hat{B}^{\prime}=\hat{A}^{-1/2}\hat{B}\hat{A}^{-1/2}
.
Since
\hat{B}^{\prime}\succeq 0
and
\hat{B}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
, we have
0\preceq\hat{B}^{\prime}\preceq\|\hat{B}^{\prime}\|\hat{\mathbb{I}}
or equivalently
\langle\psi|\hat{B}^{\prime}|\psi\rangle\leq\|\hat{B}^{\prime}\|\langle\psi|\psi\rangle
for any
\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{H}
.
This can be rewritten by
\ket{\phi}=\hat{A}^{-1/2}\ket{\psi}
as
\langle\phi|\hat{B}|\phi\rangle\leq\|\hat{B}^{\prime}\|\langle\phi|\hat{A}|\phi\rangle
.
Since this holds for any
\ket{\phi}\in\mathcal{H}
, we have
\hat{B}\preceq\|\hat{B}^{\prime}\|\hat{A}
.
Setting
c=\|\hat{B}^{\prime}\|
proves the claim.
This obviously holds even when
\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
is restricted to a subspace
\hat{P}\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]\hat{P}
.

.
Since
\mathcal{E}
is positive, this gives
\mathcal{E}[\hat{\sigma}]\preceq c\mathcal{E}[\hat{\rho}]
and thus
\ker(\mathcal{E}[\hat{\sigma}])\supseteq\ker(\mathcal{E}[\hat{\rho}])\supseteq\ker(\hat{\rho})
90
90

90

Let
\hat{A},\hat{B}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
be positive semidefinite operators.
If there exists
c>0
such that
\hat{B}\preceq c\hat{A}
, then
\textrm{ker}(\hat{A})\subseteq\textrm{ker}(\hat{B})
.

Proof
. Let
\ket{\psi}\in\textrm{ker}(\hat{A})
, then
\hat{A}\ket{\psi}=0
.
Since
\hat{B}\preceq c\hat{A}
, we have
0\leq\langle\psi|(c\hat{A}-\hat{B})|\psi\rangle=-\langle\psi|\hat{B}|\psi\rangle
.
Since
\hat{B}\succeq 0
, this implies
\langle\psi|\hat{B}|\psi\rangle=0
and thus
\hat{B}\ket{\psi}=0
.
As this holds for any
\ket{\psi}\in\textrm{ker}(\hat{A})
, we have
\textrm{ker}(\hat{A})\subseteq\textrm{ker}(\hat{B})
.

.
This establishes
\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{F}_{P})\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{P}\subseteq\hat{P}\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]\hat{P}
.
Since
\mathcal{E}
is linear and
\hat{P}\hat{X}\hat{P}
for any
\hat{X}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
can be decomposed into a linear combination of operators in
\mathbb{F}_{P}
, we have
\mathcal{E}[\hat{P}\hat{X}\hat{P}]\in\hat{P}\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]\hat{P}
and hence
\mathcal{E}[\hat{P}\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]\hat{P}]\subseteq\hat{P}\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]\hat{P}
.
However, (1) implies that such
\hat{P}
must be
\hat{P}=\hat{\mathbb{I}}
and
\hat{\rho}\succ 0
(i.e., full rank), which leads to
(\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{E})^{d-1}[\hat{\rho}]\succ 0
.
In constrast, when
\hat{\rho}
is not full rank, we must instead have
\ker((\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{E})[\hat{\rho}])\subset\ker(\hat{\rho})
and thus
\textrm{rank}((\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{E})[\hat{\rho}])>\textrm{rank}(\hat{\rho})
.
Therefore, even in that case,
\hat{\rho}
becomes full rank at least after
(d-1)
times applications of
\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{E}
, that is
(\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{E})^{d-1}[\hat{\rho}]\succ 0
.

By expanding
(\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{E})^{d-1}[\hat{\rho}]
and
\exp(s\mathcal{E})[\hat{\rho}]
for
s>0
in powers of
\mathcal{E}
, we find that all terms are positive semidefinite in both expansions.
Since all powers appearing in
(\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{E})^{d-1}[\hat{\rho}]
are also present in
\exp(s\mathcal{E})[\hat{\rho}]
, there exists some
c>0
such that
\exp(s\mathcal{E})[\hat{\rho}]\succeq c(\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{E})^{d-1}[\hat{\rho}]
.
This proves
\exp(s\mathcal{E})[\hat{\rho}]\succ 0
for any
s>0
.

(3)
\Rightarrow
(1):
Suppose that there exists an orthogonal projection operator
\hat{P}\notin\{0,\hat{\mathbb{I}}\}
such that
\mathcal{E}[\hat{P}]=\hat{P}\hat{\sigma}\hat{P}
for some positive semidefinite operator
\hat{\sigma}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
.
This implies
\mathcal{E}[\hat{P}]=\hat{P}\hat{\sigma}\hat{P}\preceq c\hat{P}
for some
c>0
91
91

91

If
\hat{P}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
is an orthogonal projection operator and
\hat{A}\in\mathbb{B}[\mathcal{H}]
is a positive semidefinite operator, then there exists
c>0
such that
\hat{P}\hat{A}\hat{P}\preceq c\hat{P}
.

Proof
. For any
\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{H}
, we have
\langle\psi|\hat{P}\hat{A}\hat{P}|\psi\rangle\leq\|\hat{P}|\psi\rangle\|\|\hat{A}\hat{P}|\psi\rangle\|
by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.
Then,
\|\hat{P}|\psi\rangle\|\|\hat{A}\hat{P}|\psi\rangle\|\leq\|\hat{A}\|\|\hat{P}|\psi\rangle\|^{2}=\|\hat{A}\|\langle\psi|\hat{P}|\psi\rangle
.
Setting
c=\|\hat{A}\|
yields
\hat{P}\hat{A}\hat{P}\preceq c\hat{P}
.

and then
\mathcal{E}^{n}[\hat{P}]\preceq c^{n}\hat{P}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
We thus find
\exp(s\mathcal{E})[\hat{P}]\preceq e^{cs}\hat{P}
for any
s>0
.
This implies that
\exp(s\mathcal{E})[\hat{P}]
is not positive definite for all
s>0
, as we have
\langle\psi|\exp(s\mathcal{E})[\hat{P}]|\psi\rangle\leq e^{cs}\langle\psi|\hat{P}|\psi\rangle=0
for
\ket{\psi}\in\textrm{ker}(\hat{P})
.
∎

Appendix C
Outline of the proof of the ergodicity explained in Sec.
5.1.1

Here, we show the proof of the ergodicity explained in Sec.
5.1.1
.
We basically follow Ref.
[
255
]
but try to illustrate it in a physicist-friendly way, at the cost of mathematical rigor.

We first consider the conditional expectation value

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}|b_{1},\cdots,b_{n-1}\right]=\sum_{b_{n}}\frac{\mathcal{E}_{b_{n}}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n-1}]}{\mathrm{Tr}[\mathcal{E}_{b_{n}}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n-1}]]}\cdot\mathrm{Tr}[\mathcal{E}_{b_{n}}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n-1}]]=\mathcal{E}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n-1}]

(415)

and define

\displaystyle\delta_{\bm{b};n}=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}-\mathcal{E}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n-1}]\right)\hat{A}\right],

(416)

which serves as the fluctuation of the trajectory measured by an observable
\hat{A}
, given
\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n-1}
.
We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\delta_{\bm{b};n}|b_{1},\cdots,b_{n-1}]=0.

(417)

Now, for the weighted sum of fluctuations

\displaystyle Y_{\bm{b};n}=\sum_{s=1}^{n}\frac{\delta_{\bm{b};s}}{s},

(418)

We find

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Y_{\bm{b};n}|b_{1},\cdots,b_{n-1}]=Y_{\bm{b};n-1}+\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}[\delta_{\bm{b};n}|b_{1},\cdots,b_{n-1}]=Y_{\bm{b};n-1}.

(419)

This means that
Y_{\bm{b};n}
is a martingale.
We also note that
Y_{\bm{b};n}
is bounded as well,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|Y_{\bm{b};n}|]^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}[Y_{\bm{b};n}^{2}]=\sum_{ss^{\prime}}\frac{1}{ss^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}[\delta_{\bm{b};s}\delta_{\bm{b};s^{\prime}}]=\sum_{s}\frac{1}{s^{2}}\mathbb{E}[\delta_{\bm{b};s}^{2}]\leq 4\|\hat{A}\|^{2}\sum_{s}\frac{1}{s^{2}}\leq\frac{2\pi^{2}\|\hat{A}\|^{2}}{3},

(420)

where
\|\hat{A}\|
is the operator norm for
\hat{A}
.
Therefore, we can use the martingale convergence theorem
[
260
]
to find that

\displaystyle Y_{\bm{b};\infty}:=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}Y_{\bm{b};n}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{s=1}^{n}\frac{\delta_{\bm{b};s}}{s}

(421)

exists almost surely with respect to the probability measure for quantum trajectories.

Now, using Kronecker’s lemma
92
92

92

Kronecker’s lemma states the following. Let us consider an infinite sequence
\{x_{s}\}_{s=1}^{\infty}
satisfying
\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}x_{s}<\infty
. Then, for all
\{g_{n}\}_{n}
such that
0<g_{1}\leq\cdots\leq g_{n}
with
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}g_{n}=\infty
, we find

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{g_{n}}\sum_{s=1}^{n}g_{s}x_{s}=0.

(422)

We here consider
g_{s}=s
and
x_{s}=\delta_{s}/s
.
, we find

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{s=1}^{n}\delta_{\bm{b};s}=0

(423)

almost surely.
This means

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{s=1}^{n}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};s}-\mathcal{E}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};s-1}]\right)\hat{A}\right]=0.

(424)

Since
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}\hat{A}]=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n=0}\hat{A}]=0
, we can rewrite this equation as

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{s=0}^{n-1}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};s}-\mathcal{E}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};s}]\right)\hat{A}\right]=0.

(425)

Next, we replace
\hat{A}
with

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}[\hat{A}]=\sum_{b}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{A}\hat{M}_{b},

(426)

obtaining

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{s=0}^{n-1}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\mathcal{E}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};s}]-\mathcal{E}^{2}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};s}]\right)\hat{A}\right]=0.

(427)

Summing up Eqs. (
425
) and (
427
), we find

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{s=0}^{n-1}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};s}-\mathcal{E}^{2}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};s}]\right)\hat{A}\right]=0.

(428)

We can repeat this procedure
m
times and take the average over
m
, obtaining

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{s=0}^{n-1}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};s}-\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=0}^{M-1}\mathcal{E}^{m}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};s}]\right)\hat{A}\right]=0.

(429)

By taking
M\rightarrow\infty
limit
93
93

93

We assume that
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}
and
\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}
commute.
,
we finally obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{s=0}^{n-1}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};s}-\overline{\mathcal{E}^{m}}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};s}]\right)\hat{A}\right]=0.

(430)

The next step is to notice
