\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{A}\overline{\mathcal{E}^{m}}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n+1}]\right]|b_{1}\cdots b_{n}\right]=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{A}\overline{\mathcal{E}^{m}}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}]\right]

(431)

because Eq. (
415
) and
\overline{\mathcal{E}^{m}}\circ\mathcal{E}=\overline{\mathcal{E}^{m}}
are satisfied.
This means that

\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{A}\overline{\mathcal{E}^{m}}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}]\right]
is also martingale, and we can again employ the martingale convergence theorem.
That is,

\displaystyle F_{\bm{b}}[\hat{A}]:=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{A}\overline{\mathcal{E}^{m}}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}]\right]

(432)

exists almost surely.
Substituting this into Eq. (
430
),
we find that
\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}}^{\mathrm{\infty}}=\overline{\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}}
exists and satisfies

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}}^{\infty}\hat{A}]=F_{\bm{b}}[\hat{A}].

(433)

Thus, (1) in Sec.
5.1.1
is proved.

As the final step, we note

\displaystyle F_{\bm{b}}[\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}[\hat{A}]]=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{A}\mathcal{E}\circ\overline{\mathcal{E}^{m}}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}]\right]=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{A}\overline{\mathcal{E}^{m}}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}]\right]=F_{\bm{b}}[\hat{A}]

(434)

and thus

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}}^{\infty}\hat{A}]=\mathrm{Tr}[\mathcal{E}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}}^{\infty}]\hat{A}],

(435)

where we have used
\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{A}\mathcal{E}[\hat{B}]]=\mathrm{Tr}[\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}[\hat{A}]\hat{B}]
.
Since this holds for arbitrary
\hat{A}
, we can conclude

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}}^{\infty}]=\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}}^{\infty}

(436)

almost surely.
We also find

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[F_{\bm{b}}[\hat{A}]\right]=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{A}\overline{\mathcal{E}^{m}}[\mathcal{E}^{n}[\hat{\rho}_{0}]]\right]=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{A}\overline{\mathcal{E}^{m}}[\hat{\rho}_{0}]\right]

(437)

because
\overline{\mathcal{E}^{m}}\circ\mathcal{E}^{n}=\overline{\mathcal{E}^{m}}
.
This means that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}}^{\infty}\right]=\overline{\mathcal{E}^{m}}[\hat{\rho}_{0}]

(438)

almost surely.
Therefore, (2) in Sec.
5.1.1
is proved.
Finally, if there is a unique stationary state
\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}
of
\mathcal{E}
, Eq. (
436
) ensures

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}}^{\infty}=\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}

(439)

almost surely, which proves (3).

Appendix D
Outline of the proof of the purification explained in Sec.
5.2.1

Here, we outline the proof of the sufficient condition for the purification of typical quantum trajectories presented in Ref.
[
256
]
.
First, we note that the statement in Sec.
5.2.1
is justified from the fact that either of the following statements holds (i.e., if (1)’ is not satisfied, then (2)’ holds):

(1)’

For all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}P_{\bm{b};n}^{(k)}=1

(440)

almost surely, where

\displaystyle P_{\bm{b};n}^{(k)}=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}\right)^{k}\right].

(441)

(2)’

There exists a mixed (i.e., not pure) state
\hat{\sigma}
such that for all
b
, there exists
\zeta_{b}\>(\geq 0)
and

\displaystyle\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\sigma}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\sim\zeta_{b}\hat{\sigma},

(442)

where
\sim
denotes the unitary equivalence.

In fact, (1)’ clearly indicates that a quantum trajectory purifies almost surely by considering
k=2
.
Moreover, as will be explained in the next paragraph, (2)’ indicates
(\star)
in Sec.
5.2.1
.
Therefore, if
(\star)
does not hold, (2)’ does not hold, either, which leads to the fact that (1)’ and then the purification of almost all trajectories hold.

Showing that (2)’ indicates (
\star
) is a bit complicated: suppose that
\hat{\sigma}
has a support onto which we can define a projection operator
\hat{\mathcal{Q}}
.
Let us denote
\mathrm{det}_{+}(\hat{A})
as the product of all strictly positive eigenvalues of a Hermitian operator
\hat{A}
.
Then, by using the polar decomposition of
\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\mathcal{Q}}
as
\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\mathcal{Q}}=\hat{v}_{b}\sqrt{\hat{\mathcal{Q}}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\mathcal{Q}}}
(
\hat{v}_{b}
is unitary), we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{det}_{+}({\zeta}_{b}\hat{\sigma})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{det}_{+}(\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\sigma}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger})=\mathrm{det}_{+}(\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\mathcal{Q}}\hat{\sigma}\hat{\mathcal{Q}}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger})=\mathrm{det}_{+}\left(\hat{v}_{b}\sqrt{\hat{\mathcal{Q}}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\mathcal{Q}}}\hat{\sigma}\sqrt{\hat{\mathcal{Q}}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\mathcal{Q}}}\hat{v}_{b}^{\dagger}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{det}_{+}\left(\sqrt{\hat{\mathcal{Q}}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\mathcal{Q}}}\hat{\sigma}\sqrt{\hat{\mathcal{Q}}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\mathcal{Q}}}\right)=\mathrm{det}_{\mathcal{Q}}\left(\sqrt{\hat{\mathcal{Q}}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\mathcal{Q}}}\hat{\sigma}\sqrt{\hat{\mathcal{Q}}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\mathcal{Q}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{det}_{\mathcal{Q}}\left({\hat{\mathcal{Q}}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\mathcal{Q}}}\right)\mathrm{det}_{\mathcal{Q}}\left(\hat{\sigma}\right)

(443)

where we have used the unitary invariance of the spectrum and
\det_{\mathcal{Q}}
is the standard determinant for the projected space determined by
\hat{\mathcal{Q}}
.
Since we also have
\mathrm{det}_{+}({\zeta}_{b}\hat{\sigma})=\mathrm{det}_{\mathcal{Q}}({\zeta}_{b}\hat{\mathcal{Q}}\hat{\sigma}\hat{\mathcal{Q}})=\mathrm{det}_{\mathcal{Q}}({\zeta}_{b}\hat{\mathcal{Q}})\mathrm{det}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{\sigma})
and
\mathrm{det}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{\sigma})>0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{det}_{\mathcal{Q}}({\zeta}_{b}\hat{\mathcal{Q}})=\mathrm{det}_{\mathcal{Q}}\left({\hat{\mathcal{Q}}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\mathcal{Q}}}\right).

(444)

This indicates

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}[\zeta_{b}{\hat{\mathcal{Q}}}]\leq\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\mathcal{Q}}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\mathcal{Q}}],

(445)

where the equality condition is achieved only when
\zeta_{b}{\hat{\mathcal{Q}}}=\hat{\mathcal{Q}}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\mathcal{Q}}
94
94

94

This is known by considering the eigenvalues of
\hat{\mathcal{Q}}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\mathcal{Q}}
, say
x_{1},\cdots,x_{q}
with
q=\mathrm{rank}(\hat{\mathcal{Q}})
. We find that Eq. (
444
) means
\zeta_{b}^{q}=x_{1}\cdots x_{q}\leq\left(\frac{1}{q}\sum_{s=1}^{q}x_{s}\right)^{q}
, where the equality condition is
x_{1}=\cdots=x_{q}
. This indicates
q\zeta_{b}\leq\sum_{s=1}^{q}x_{s}
.
.
In contrast, since
95
95

95

Note that
\sum_{b}\zeta_{b}=\sum_{b}\mathrm{Tr}[\zeta_{b}\hat{\sigma}]=\sum_{b}\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\sigma}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}]=1.

\sum_{b}\mathrm{Tr}[\zeta_{b}{\hat{\mathcal{Q}}}]=q=\sum_{b}\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\mathcal{Q}}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\mathcal{Q}}]
and
\mathrm{Tr}[\zeta_{b}{\hat{\mathcal{Q}}}]\geq 0
for each
b
, we actually find the equality in
(
445
).
Therefore, we finally have

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{Q}}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\mathcal{Q}}=\zeta_{b}\hat{\mathcal{Q}},

(446)

which is the condition (
\star
).

Now, let us show the statement that either (1)’ or (2)’ holds.
We first define the function
D_{k}
:

\displaystyle D_{k}[\hat{\rho}]=\sum_{b}p_{b}{\left\{\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\frac{\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\rho}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}}{p_{b}}\right)^{k}\right]-\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\rho}^{k}]\right\}}^{2},

(447)

where
p_{b}=\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\rho}]
.
By definition, we have

\displaystyle D_{k}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}]=\mathbb{E}\left[(P_{\bm{b};n+1}^{(k)}-P_{\bm{b};n}^{(k)})^{2}|b_{1},\cdots,b_{n}\right].

(448)

Now, applying the inequality by Nielsen
[
265
]
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[P_{\bm{b};n+1}^{(k)}|b_{1},\cdots,b_{n}\right]\geq\mathbb{E}\left[P_{\bm{b};n}^{(k)}|b_{1},\cdots,b_{n}\right]=P_{\bm{b};n}^{(k)}

(449)

with
\mathbb{E}[|P_{\bm{b};n}^{(k)}|]\leq 1
, which indicates that
P_{\bm{b};n}^{(k)}
is a positive submartingale bounded by 1.
Therefore, by the convergence theorem of submartingale, we have

\displaystyle P_{\bm{b};\infty}^{(k)}:=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}P_{\bm{b};n}^{(k)}

(450)

almost surely.
Then, we have
96
96

96

Note that
\mathbb{E}\left[P_{\bm{b};n}^{(k)}\left(P_{\bm{b};n+1}^{(k)}-P_{\bm{b};n}^{(k)}\right)\right]\geq 0
because
P_{\bm{b};n}^{(k)}
is the submartingale.

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}[D_{k}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}]]

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[{\left(P_{\bm{b};n+1}^{(k)}\right)^{2}-\left(P_{\bm{b};n}^{(k)}\right)^{2}}\right]-2\mathbb{E}\left[P_{\bm{b};n}^{(k)}\left(P_{\bm{b};n+1}^{(k)}-P_{\bm{b};n}^{(k)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(P_{\bm{b};n+1}^{(k)}\right)^{2}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\left(P_{\bm{b};n}^{(k)}\right)^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(P_{\bm{b};\infty}^{(k)}\right)^{2}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\left(P_{\bm{b};0}^{(k)}\right)^{2}\right]\leq 1.

(451)

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{d}\mathbb{E}\left[D_{k}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}]\right]=0,

(452)

where
d
is the dimension of the Hilbert space, which is assumed to be finite.

Now, suppose that (1)’ does not hold. Then, for some (and thus all)
k\geq 2
, we find

\displaystyle c_{k}:=\mathbb{E}\left[P_{\bm{b};\infty}^{(k)}\right]<1.

(453)

Employing the inequality by Nielsen, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[P_{\bm{b};n}^{(2)}]\leq\mathbb{E}[P_{\bm{b};\infty}^{(2)}]=c_{2}<1.

(454)

Let us define a set
S_{n}
of quantum trajectories that satisfies
P_{\bm{b};n}^{(2)}\leq\frac{c_{2}+1}{2}<1
and its complement
S_{n}^{c}
.
Then, using Eq. (
454
), we have

\displaystyle c_{2}\geq\mathbb{E}\left[P_{\bm{b};n}^{(2)}\right]\geq\mathbb{E}\left[P_{\bm{b};n}^{(2)}\chi_{S_{n}^{c}}(\bm{b})\right]\geq\frac{c_{2}+1}{2}\mathbb{P}[S_{n}^{c}]=\frac{c_{2}+1}{2}\left(1-\mathbb{P}[S_{n}]\right)

(455)

for all
n
, where
\chi
is the indicator function and
\mathbb{P}[S]
denotes the probability where quantum trajectories are in a set
S
.
This leads to

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[S_{n}]\geq\frac{1-c_{2}}{1+c_{2}}.

(456)

Next, we note that we can take a quantum trajectory characterized by a sequence of outcomes
\bm{b}^{*}\in S_{n}
such that the sequence satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[S_{n}]\sum_{k=1}^{d}{D_{k}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}^{*};n}]}\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\chi_{S_{n}}(\bm{b})\cdot\sum_{k=1}^{d}{D_{k}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}]}\right],

(457)

since the right-hand side denotes the average over
\bm{b}
.
For this quantum trajectory, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{d}{D_{k}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}^{*};n}]}\leq\frac{1+c_{2}}{1-c_{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{d}{\mathbb{E}\left[D_{k}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}]\right]}

(458)

due to Eq. (
456
) and the fact

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\chi_{S_{n}}(\bm{b})\cdot\sum_{k=1}^{d}{D_{k}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}]}\right]\leq\sum_{k=1}^{d}{\mathbb{E}\left[D_{k}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b};n}]\right]}.

(459)

Since the trajectory is in
S_{n}
,

\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}^{*};n}
is in the compact set of the quantum state

\displaystyle{\left\{\hat{\rho}\>:\>\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\rho}^{2}]\leq\frac{1+c_{2}}{2}\right\}}

(460)

for every
n
.

Now, let us consider
n\rightarrow\infty
.
From Eqs. (
452
) and (
458
), we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{d}{D_{k}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}^{*};n}]}=0

(461)

and, from Eq. (
460
), we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}P_{\bm{b}^{*};n}^{(2)}\leq\frac{1+c_{2}}{2}<1,

(462)

which indicates that the state remains a mixed state for the trajectory characterized by
\bm{b}^{*}
.

Finally, Eq. (
461
) means that
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{D_{k}[\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}^{*};n}]}=0

for all
k=1,\cdots,d
, and this condition is written as

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}^{*};n}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\right]{\left\{\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\frac{\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}^{*};n}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}}{\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}^{*};n}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\right]}\right)^{k}\right]-\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}^{*};n}\right)^{k}\right]\right\}}^{2}=0

(463)

for all
k=1,\cdots,d
and
b
.
This means that, for
n\rightarrow\infty
, either
\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}^{*};n}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\right]=0
or
\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\frac{\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}^{*};n}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}}{\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}^{*};n}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\right]}\right)^{k}\right]-\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}^{*};n}\right)^{k}\right]=0\>(k=1,\cdots,d)
holds. The former means that (2)’ holds with
\zeta_{b}=0
.
The latter indicates that
\frac{\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}^{*};n}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}}{\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{M}_{b}\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}^{*};n}\hat{M}_{b}^{\dagger}\right]}
and
\hat{\rho}_{\bm{b}^{*};n}
should be unitarily equivalent due to the coincidence of the
k
th moments with
k=1,\cdots,d
, meaning that (2)’ is proven.

Appendix E
Birkhoff’s ergodic theorem used in Sec.
6.2.2

We explore the time average of a function
f(\Psi_{1,\bm{b};m})
,

\displaystyle F_{n}(\bm{b})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{m=\tilde{m}}^{\tilde{m}+n-1}f(\Psi_{1,\bm{b};m}),

(464)

where
\tilde{m}
is an integer such that
\varepsilon_{1,\bm{b};m}
is not degenerate and thus
\ket{\Psi_{1,\bm{b};m}}
is unique for almost all trajectories and for all
m\geq\tilde{m}
. In the following, we focus on situations where
m\geq\tilde{m}
is satisfied. Here, we consider the case where the initial state is the stationary state,
\hat{\rho}_{0}=\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}
, and thus the probability distribution of
\{b\}
at each step obeys the invariant measure determined from
\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}
. On the basis of the invariant measure, Birkhoff’s ergodic theorem states that the three equations below are satisfied in typical trajectories:

\displaystyle F^{\pm}(\bm{b})

\displaystyle=F^{\pm}(\vartheta\bm{b}),

(465)

\displaystyle F^{+}(\bm{b})

\displaystyle=F^{-}(\bm{b}),

(466)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}\left[F^{\pm}(\bm{b})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}\left[f(\Psi_{1,\bm{b};m})\right],

(467)

where
F^{\pm}(\bm{b})
are defined as

\displaystyle F^{+}(\bm{b})=\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}F_{n}(\bm{b}),\ \ F^{-}(\bm{b})=\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}F_{n}(\bm{b}).

(468)

The outline of the proof for Eqs. (
465
)-(
467
) is explained in the following, on the basis of Ref.
[
268
]
. Equations (
465
) and (
466
)
mean that we can consider the limit
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}F_{n}(\bm{b})
invariant under the shift of
\bm{b}
. If the dynamics is ergodic with respect to the shift of
\bm{b}
, as in Eq. (
286
),
\{\vartheta^{-n}\mathfrak{B}\}_{n}
covers almost all trajectories for arbitrary set
\mathfrak{B}
with
P_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}(\mathfrak{B})\neq 0
, and thus
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}F_{n}(\bm{b})=F^{\pm}(\bm{b})
typically becomes independent of
\bm{b}
. Then, Eq. (
467
) can be written as

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}F_{n}(\bm{b})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}\left[f(\Psi_{1,\bm{b};m})\right],

(469)

which means that the time average of
f(\Psi_{1,\bm{b};m})
(in the left-hand side) corresponds to the sample average of
f(\Psi_{1,\bm{b};m})
(in the right-hand side).

We first show inequalities

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}^{\mathfrak{C}_{\alpha}^{+}\cap\mathfrak{D}}\left[f(\Psi_{1,\bm{b};m})\right]\geq\alpha P_{\mathrm{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}}(\mathfrak{C}_{\alpha}^{+}\cap\mathfrak{D}),\ \ \mathfrak{C}_{\alpha}^{+}=\left\{\bm{b}:\sup_{n\geq 1}F_{n}(\bm{b})>\alpha\right\},

(470)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}^{\mathfrak{C}_{\beta}^{-}\cap\mathfrak{D}}\left[f(\Psi_{1,\bm{b};m})\right]\leq\beta P_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}(\mathfrak{C}_{\beta}^{-}\cap\mathfrak{D}),\ \ \mathfrak{C}_{\beta}^{-}=\left\{\bm{b}:\inf_{n\geq 1}F_{n}(\bm{b})<\beta\right\},

(471)

where
\alpha
and
\beta
are arbitrary real values. Here,
\mathfrak{D}
is an arbitrary invariant set
\mathfrak{D}=\vartheta^{-1}\mathfrak{D}
and
\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}^{\mathfrak{B}}\left[f(\Psi_{1,\bm{b};m})\right]=\sum_{\bm{b}\in\mathfrak{B}}\mathrm{Tr}\left(\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};\infty}\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}\hat{\mathsf{M}}_{\bm{b};\infty}^{\dagger}\right)f(\Psi_{1,\bm{b};m})
is the average of
f(\Psi_{1,\bm{b};m})
over
\mathfrak{B}
. Birkhoff’s ergodic theorem can be derived from Eqs. (
470
) and (
471
). To show Eqs. (
470
) and (
471
), we evaluate functions

\displaystyle F_{N}^{+\alpha}(\bm{b})=\sup_{0\leq n\leq N}n\left[F_{n}(\bm{b})-\alpha\right],\ \ F_{N}^{-\beta}(\bm{b})=\inf_{0\leq n\leq N}n\left[F_{n}(\bm{b})-\beta\right].

(472)

Here, for
n=0
, we set
n\left[F_{n}(\bm{b})-\gamma\right]=0
with
\gamma=\alpha,\beta
. On the basis of
F_{N}^{\pm\gamma}(\bm{b})
, we consider two sets of outcomes
\bm{b}
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{N}^{+\alpha}=\left\{\bm{b}:F_{N}^{+\alpha}(\bm{b})>0\right\},\ \ \mathfrak{F}_{N}^{-\beta}=\left\{\bm{b}:F_{N}^{-\beta}(\bm{b})<0\right\}.

(473)

If a sequence
\bm{b}
is included in
\mathfrak{F}_{N}^{+\alpha}
,

\displaystyle F_{N}^{+\alpha}(\vartheta\bm{b})\geq n\left[F_{n}(\vartheta\bm{b})-\alpha\right]

(474)

is satisfied for an integer
n
in a range
0\leq n\leq N-1
. In the same way, if
\bm{b}\in\mathfrak{F}_{N}^{-\beta}
and
0\leq n\leq N-1
hold,

\displaystyle F_{N}^{-\beta}(\vartheta\bm{b})\leq n\left[F_{n}(\vartheta\bm{b})-\beta\right]

(475)

is satisfied. Adding
f(\Psi_{1,\bm{b};\tilde{m}})-\alpha
and
f(\Psi_{1,\bm{b};\tilde{m}})-\beta
on both sides of Eqs. (
474
) and (
475
), respectively, we can obtain

\displaystyle F_{N}^{+\alpha}(\vartheta\bm{b})+f(\Psi_{1,\bm{b};\tilde{m}})-\alpha

\displaystyle\geq(n+1)\left[F_{n+1}(\bm{b})-\alpha\right],\ \ \bm{b}\in\mathfrak{F}_{N}^{+\alpha}

(476)

\displaystyle F_{N}^{-\beta}(\vartheta\bm{b})+f(\Psi_{1,\bm{b};\tilde{m}})-\beta

\displaystyle\leq(n+1)\left[F_{n+1}(\bm{b})-\beta\right],\ \ \bm{b}\in\mathfrak{F}_{N}^{-\beta}.

(477)

These lead to

\displaystyle f(\Psi_{1,\bm{b};\tilde{m}})-\alpha

\displaystyle\geq F_{N}^{+\alpha}(\bm{b})-F_{N}^{+\alpha}(\vartheta\bm{b}),\ \ \bm{b}\in\mathfrak{F}_{N}^{+\alpha},

(478)

\displaystyle f(\Psi_{1,\bm{b};\tilde{m}})-\beta

\displaystyle\leq F_{N}^{-\beta}(\bm{b})-F_{N}^{-\beta}(\vartheta\bm{b}),\ \ \bm{b}\in\mathfrak{F}_{N}^{-\beta}.

(479)

Here, we focus on sequences
\bm{b}
included in an invariant set
\mathfrak{D}=\vartheta^{-1}\mathfrak{D}
. Then, taking the average of Eq. (
478
) over
\mathfrak{F}_{N}^{+\alpha}\cap\mathfrak{D}
, we can obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}^{\mathfrak{F}_{N}^{+\alpha}\cap\mathfrak{D}}\left[f(\Psi_{1,\bm{b};\tilde{m}})-\alpha\right]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}^{\mathfrak{F}_{N}^{+\alpha}\cap\mathfrak{D}}\left[F_{N}^{+\alpha}(\bm{b})-F_{N}^{+\alpha}(\vartheta\bm{b})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}^{\mathfrak{F}_{N}^{+\alpha}\cap\mathfrak{D}}\left[F_{N}^{+\alpha}(\bm{b})\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}^{\vartheta^{-1}(\mathfrak{F}_{N}^{+\alpha}\cap\mathfrak{D})}\left[F_{N}^{+\alpha}(\vartheta\bm{b})\right]=0,

(480)

where
\vartheta^{-1}(\mathfrak{F}_{N}^{+\alpha}\cap\mathfrak{D})=\mathfrak{F}_{N}^{+\alpha}\cap\mathfrak{D}
is used. In the same way, taking the average of Eq. (
479
) over
\mathfrak{F}_{N}^{-\beta}\cap\mathfrak{D}
, we can obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}^{\mathfrak{F}_{N}^{-\beta}\cap\mathfrak{D}}\left[f(\Psi_{1,\bm{b};\tilde{m}})-\beta\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}^{\mathfrak{F}_{N}^{-\beta}\cap\mathfrak{D}}\left[F_{N}^{-\beta}(\bm{b})-F_{N}^{-\beta}(\vartheta\bm{b})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}^{\mathfrak{F}_{N}^{-\beta}\cap\mathfrak{D}}\left[F_{N}^{-\beta}(\bm{b})\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}^{\vartheta^{-1}(\mathfrak{F}_{N}^{-\beta}\cap\mathfrak{D})}\left[F_{N}^{-\beta}(\vartheta\bm{b})\right]=0.

(481)

These inequalities are equivalent to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}^{\mathfrak{F}_{N}^{+\alpha}\cap\mathfrak{D}}\left[f(\Psi_{1,\bm{b};m})\right]\geq\alpha P_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}(\mathfrak{F}_{N}^{+\alpha}\cap\mathfrak{D}),\ \ \mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}^{\mathfrak{F}_{N}^{-\beta}\cap\mathfrak{D}}\left[f(\Psi_{1,\bm{b};m})\right]\leq\beta P_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}(\mathfrak{F}_{N}^{-\beta}\cap\mathfrak{D}),

(482)

where
\tilde{m}
is replaced by
m
. The replacement is valid because
\tilde{m}
in Eq. (
464
) can take arbitrary integers as long as
\varepsilon_{1,\bm{b};m\geq\tilde{m}}
is not degenerate almost surely. Here, we notice that
\mathfrak{C}_{\gamma}^{\pm}
can be written as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}_{\gamma}^{\pm}=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\mathfrak{F}_{N}^{\pm\gamma}.

(483)

Thus, taking the limit
N\rightarrow\infty
in Eq. (
482
), we can obtain Eqs. (
470
) and (
471
).

To show Eq. (
465
), we consider an identical equation

\displaystyle\frac{n+1}{n}F_{n+1}(\bm{b})=F_{n}(\vartheta\bm{b})+\frac{1}{n}f(\Psi_{1,\bm{b};\tilde{m}}).

(484)

Then, taking
\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}
and
\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}
, we can obtain Eq. (
465
) owing to
\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}f(\Psi_{1,\bm{b};\tilde{m}})/n=\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}f(\Psi_{1,\bm{b};\tilde{m}})/n=0
.

To show Eq. (
466
), we consider a set

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta}=\{\bm{b}:F^{-}(\bm{b})<\beta\ \mathrm{and}\ \alpha<F^{+}(\bm{b})\}.

(485)

From Eq. (
465
), we can understand that
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta}
is the invariant set,
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta}=\vartheta^{-1}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta}
. Therefore, Eq. (
470
) leads to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}^{\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta}}\left[f(\Psi_{1,\bm{b};m})\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}^{\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta}\cap\mathfrak{C}_{\alpha}^{+}}\left[f(\Psi_{1,\bm{b};m})\right]\geq\alpha P_{\mathrm{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta}).

(486)

Here,
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\alpha}^{+}
is used. In the same way, Eq. (
471
) and
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\beta}^{-}
result in

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}^{\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta}}\left[f(\Psi_{1,\bm{b};m})\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}^{\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta}\cap\mathfrak{C}_{\beta}^{-}}\left[f(\Psi_{1,\bm{b};m})\right]\leq\beta P_{\mathrm{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta}).

(487)

Thus,
\alpha P_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta})\leq\beta P_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta})
holds, which means

\displaystyle P_{\mathrm{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha\beta})=0

(488)

if
\alpha>\beta
is satisfied. Equation (
488
) indicates that Eq. (
466
) is satisfied almost surely.

To show Eq. (
467
), we consider invariant sets

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}_{nk}^{+}=\left\{\bm{b}:\frac{k}{n}<F^{+}(\bm{b})\leq\frac{k+1}{n}\right\},\ \ \mathfrak{D}_{nk}^{-}=\left\{\bm{b}:\frac{k}{n}\leq F^{-}(\bm{b})<\frac{k+1}{n}\right\}.

(489)

For arbitrary
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}^{\mathfrak{D}_{nk}^{+}}\left[f(\Psi_{1,\bm{b};m})\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}^{\mathfrak{D}_{nk}^{+}\cap\mathfrak{C}_{\frac{k}{n}-\epsilon}^{+}}\left[f(\Psi_{1,\bm{b};m})\right]\geq\left(\frac{k}{n}-\epsilon\right)P_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}(\mathfrak{D}_{nk}^{+})

(490)

is satisfied, which we can understand from Eq. (
470
). This leads to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}^{\mathfrak{D}_{nk}^{+}}\left[F^{+}(\bm{b})\right]\leq\frac{k+1}{n}P_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}(\mathfrak{D}_{nk}^{+})\leq\frac{1}{n}P_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}(\mathfrak{D}_{nk}^{+})+\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}^{\mathfrak{D}_{nk}^{+}}\left[f(\Psi_{1,\bm{b};m})\right],

(491)

where the limit
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
is taken. Taking the summation over all integers
k
and taking the limit
n\rightarrow\infty
in Eq. (
491
), we can obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}\left[F^{+}(\bm{b})\right]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}\left[f(\Psi_{1,\bm{b};m})\right].

(492)

In the same way, Eq. (
471
) leads to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}^{\mathfrak{D}_{nk}^{-}}\left[f(\Psi_{1,\bm{b};m})\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}^{\mathfrak{D}_{nk}^{-}\cap\mathfrak{C}_{\frac{k+1}{n}+\epsilon}^{-}}\left[f(\Psi_{1,\bm{b};m})\right]\leq\left(\frac{k+1}{n}+\epsilon\right)P_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}(\mathfrak{D}_{nk}^{-}),

(493)

from which we can obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}^{\mathfrak{D}_{nk}^{-}}\left[F^{-}(\bm{b})\right]\geq\frac{k}{n}P_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}(\mathfrak{D}_{nk}^{-})\geq-\frac{1}{n}P_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}(\mathfrak{D}_{nk}^{-})+\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}^{\mathfrak{D}_{nk}^{-}}\left[f(\Psi_{1,\bm{b};m})\right],

(494)

with
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
and thus

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}\left[f(\Psi_{1,\bm{b};m})\right]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}\left[F^{-}(\bm{b})\right]

(495)

is satisfied, where the summation over
k
and the limit
n\rightarrow\infty
are taken. From Eqs.
(
492
), (
495
), and (
466
), we can obtain Eq. (
467
).

Appendix F
Kingman’s subadditive ergodic theorem used in Sec.
6.3.1

We show Eq. (
323
) for a sequence of functions
\{f_{n}(\bm{b})\}_{n}
that satisfy the subadditivity

\displaystyle f_{n+m}(\bm{b})\leq f_{n}(\bm{b})+f_{m}(\vartheta^{n}\bm{b}).

(496)

The outline of the proof explained here is based on Ref.
[
340
]
. In the following, we assume that the initial state is a stationary state
\hat{\rho}_{0}=\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}}=\mathcal{E}(\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{ss}})
, which leads to the invariant measure
P_{\rho_{0}}(\mathfrak{B})=P_{\rho_{0}}(\vartheta^{-1}\mathfrak{B})
, where
\mathfrak{B}
is an arbitrary set of
\bm{b}
. We also assume
f_{n}(\bm{b})\leq 0
for arbitrary
n
and
\bm{b}
, which is satisfied when we choose the function as in Eq. (
331
). We consider two limits

\displaystyle f^{+}(\bm{b})=\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{f_{n}(\bm{b})}{n},\ \ f^{-}(\bm{b})=\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{f_{n}(\bm{b})}{n}.

(497)

If
f^{+}(\bm{b})=f^{-}(\bm{b})
is satisfied, the limit of
f_{n}(\bm{b})/n
exists. The limits are typically invariant under the shift,

\displaystyle f^{\pm}(\bm{b})=f^{\pm}(\vartheta\bm{b}).

(498)

Equation (
498
) originates from the subadditivity of
f_{n}(\bm{b})
, as explained below. Taking limits
\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}
and
\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}
of an inequality
\frac{n+1}{n}\frac{f_{n+1}(\bm{b})}{n+1}\leq\frac{f_{1}(\bm{b})+f_{n}(\vartheta\bm{b})}{n}
, which is obtained from Eq. (
496
),
f^{\pm}(\bm{b})\leq f^{\pm}(\vartheta\bm{b})
is satisfied. This inequality results in
\{\bm{b}:f^{\pm}(\bm{b})>\alpha\}\in\{\bm{b}:f^{\pm}(\vartheta\bm{b})>\alpha\}
where
\alpha
is a real number. Since
\bm{b}\rightarrow\vartheta\bm{b}
is a measure-preserving transformation,
\{\bm{b}:f^{\pm}(\bm{b})>\alpha\}
and
\{\bm{b}:f^{\pm}(\vartheta\bm{b})>\alpha\}=\vartheta^{-1}\{\bm{b}:f^{\pm}(\bm{b})>\alpha\}
are different at most by a measure-zero set,
P_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}(\left\{\bm{b}:f^{\pm}(\bm{b})>\alpha\right\})=P_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}(\left\{\bm{b}:f^{\pm}(\vartheta\bm{b})>\alpha\right\})
, for arbitrary
\alpha
. Therefore, Eq. (
498
) is satisfied for typical sequences of
\{\bm{b}\}
.

Figure 20:
How to separate the interval
[1,n]
for the proof of Kingman’s subadditive ergodic theorem.

To show the equivalence between
f^{+}(\bm{b})
and
f^{-}(\bm{b})
, we consider a function

\displaystyle F_{h}(\bm{b})=\max\{h,f^{-}(\bm{b})\},

(499)

with
h<0
being a real number. Owing to
f^{-}(\vartheta\bm{b})=f^{-}(\bm{b})
, the function is invariant by the shift of
\bm{b}
,
F_{h}(\vartheta\bm{b})=F_{h}(\bm{b})
. Defining a set of
\bm{b}
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{m,h}=\left\{\bm{b}:f_{\ell}(\bm{b})/\ell>F_{h}(\bm{b})+\delta\ \mathrm{for\ all}\ 1\leq\ell\leq m\right\},

(500)

we classify integers
i\in[1,n]
into three classes A, B, and C, where the real number
\delta
and the integer
m
satisfy
\delta>0
and
m<n
, respectively. For the classification, fixing a sequence of measurement outcomes
\bm{b}
, we sweep
i
from
i=1
and check whether
\vartheta^{i-1}\bm{b}
is included in
\mathfrak{B}_{m,h}^{c}
. Here,
\mathfrak{B}_{m,h}^{c}
is the complement of
\mathfrak{B}_{m,h}
. If
\vartheta^{i-1}\bm{b}\in\mathfrak{B}_{m,h}^{c}
is satisfied, we label the integer
i
as
\tau(\bm{b})
. In this case, there is a positive integer
\ell(\bm{b})\leq m
which satisfies
f_{\ell(\bm{b})}(\vartheta^{\tau(\bm{b})-1}\bm{b})/\ell(\bm{b})\leq F_{h}(\bm{b})+\delta
, where
F_{h}(\vartheta^{i-1}\bm{b})=F_{h}(\bm{b})
is used. Then, if
\tau(\bm{b})+\ell(\bm{b})<n
is satisfied, the interval
[\tau(\bm{b}),\tau(\bm{b})+\ell(\bm{b})-1]
is classified into A and we continue sweeping from
\tau(\bm{b})+\ell(\bm{b})
. We write the number of intervals in the class A as
u(\bm{b})
; there are intervals
\{[\tau_{j}(\bm{b}),\tau_{j}(\bm{b})+\ell_{j}(\bm{b})-1]\}_{j}
with
j=1,2,\cdots,u(\bm{b})
. If
\vartheta^{i-1}\bm{b}\in\mathfrak{B}_{m,h}
is satisfied, such
i
is classified into B and labeled as
\sigma(\bm{b})
. We write the number of integers in the class B as
v(\bm{b})
; there are integers
\{\sigma_{j}(\bm{b})\}_{j}
with
j=1,2,\cdots,v(\bm{b})
. If
\vartheta^{i-1}\bm{b}\in\mathfrak{B}_{m,h}^{c}
is satisfied but
i+\ell-1>n
holds for the smallest
\ell
that satisfies
f_{\ell}(\vartheta^{i-1}\bm{b})/\ell\leq F_{h}(\bm{b})+\delta
, such
i
is classified into C. We write the number of integers in the class C as
w(\bm{b})
. The schematic picture for the classification is shown in Fig.
20
.
Then, through the subadditivity in Eq. (
496
), we can obtain an inequality

\displaystyle f_{n}(\bm{b})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{u(\bm{b})}f_{\ell_{i}(\bm{b})}\left(\vartheta^{\tau_{i}(\bm{b})-1}\bm{b}\right)+\sum_{j=1}^{v(\bm{b})}f_{1}\left(\vartheta^{\sigma_{j}(\bm{b})-1}\bm{b}\right)+\sum_{k=1}^{w(\bm{b})}f_{1}(\vartheta^{n-k}\bm{b})

\displaystyle\leq\left[F_{h}(\bm{b})+\delta\right]\sum_{i=1}^{u(\bm{b})}\ell_{i}(\bm{b})

(501)

where
f_{1}(\bm{b})\leq 0
is used. Here, by construction, an inequality

\displaystyle n-m\leq\sum_{i=1}^{u(\bm{b})}\ell_{i}(\bm{b})+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\chi_{\mathfrak{B}_{m,h}}(\vartheta^{j-1}\bm{b})

(502)

is also satisfied, where
\chi_{\mathfrak{B}_{m,h}}(\bm{b})=1
if
\bm{b}\in\mathfrak{B}_{m,h}
and
\chi_{\mathfrak{B}_{m,h}}(\bm{b})=0
if
\bm{b}\in\mathfrak{B}_{m,h}^{c}
. Dividing Eq. (
502
) by
n
and taking the limit
n\rightarrow\infty
lead to

\displaystyle 1\leq\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{u(\bm{b})}\ell_{i}(\bm{b})+\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\chi_{\mathfrak{B}_{m,h}}(\vartheta^{j-1}\bm{b}).

(503)

Then, we also take the limit
m\rightarrow\infty
, which leads to
\chi_{\mathfrak{B}_{m,h}}(\bm{b})=0
. This means

\displaystyle\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{u(\bm{b})}\ell_{i}(\bm{b})=1,

(504)

since
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{u(\bm{b})}\ell_{i}(\bm{b})\leq 1
is always satisfied. On the basis of Eq. (
504
), diving Eq. (
501
) by
n
and taking the limit
\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}
, we can obtain

\displaystyle f^{+}(\bm{b})\leq F_{h}(\bm{b})+\delta.

(505)

Since
h
and
\delta
can take arbitrary small values within the range
h<0
and
\delta>0
, Eq. (
505
) results in

\displaystyle f^{+}(\bm{b})\leq f^{-}(\bm{b}),

(506)

which means
f^{+}(\bm{b})=f^{-}(\bm{b})
. When the dynamics is ergodic with respect to the shift
\bm{b}\rightarrow\vartheta\bm{b}
, i.e., Eq. (
286
) is satisfied,
f^{\pm}(\bm{b})=f^{\pm}(\vartheta\bm{b})
almost surely becomes independent of
\bm{b}
. This is because
\{\vartheta^{-n}\mathfrak{B}\}_{n}
cover almost all trajectories for an arbitrary set
\mathfrak{B}
as long as
P_{\rho_{\mathrm{ss}}}(\mathfrak{B})\neq 0
is satisfied. Then, Eq. (
506
) leads to the typical convergence of
f_{n}(\bm{b})/n
to a
\bm{b}
-independent value
\gamma
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{f_{n}(\bm{b})}{n}=\gamma,

(507)

which corresponds to Eq. (
323
) in the main text.
